## Tip 001 — Flavor Infusion: What, With What, and How (Parts 1–3, incl. Cold & Hot Infusion methods)

**Source:** from B1-01

FLAVOR INFUSION: WHAT, WITH WHAT, AND HOW

At first glance, this topic may seem very simple, as if there is not much to discuss. But! You might be surprised to learn that even when I was running a masterclass at a large-scale production facility, my recommendation to switch to cold aromatization came as a surprise to the team.

In reality, aroma and taste are inseparably connected and should always be considered as a whole.

Flavor–aroma pairing is a fascinating field you inevitably encounter when working in pastry arts. Once baking basic shortbread cookies or classic apple cakes is no longer inspiring, a natural desire arises to explore new or unconventional flavor combinations and to introduce subtle aromatic notes.

A well-chosen aroma and the dessert's aftertaste can calm a person or, on the contrary, make them feel more energized; it can bring comfort or encourage reflection. For example, vanilla conveys warmth and a sense of calm, cinnamon evokes the feeling of a cozy autumn, while lemongrass and lavender have soothing properties.

To transfer aroma from one ingredient to another, they must be combined under specific conditions:

🔸 mixed together (stirred or blended)
🔸 chilled together
🔸 heated together
🔸 simmered together

What can be aromatized?
In mousses:

🔺 the liquid base (cream / milk)
🔺 the sugar syrup used for whipping egg whites (if the mousse is based on Italian meringue)
🔺 mousses may also be aromatized using pastes

📍 Only thick pastes are used — those that do not compromise, but rather improve the dessert's texture:
— nut pastes, praline paste, certain fruit pastes

In ganaches:

🔺 cream
🔺 fruit purée
🔺 water

In berry fillings:

🔺 the berries themselves or berry purée are aromatized using the cold method or through vacuum infusion
In sponge cakes and other baked goods exposed to high temperatures:
🔺 the aromatizing ingredient is added directly to the recipe

In the previous part, we discussed WHAT can be flavored. Today, let's talk about WHAT TO USE to do that.

Most likely, the first thing that comes to mind is flavorings. Both natural or nature-identical flavorings, as well as synthetic ones, can help add aroma to desserts.

This method has 3 obvious advantages:

1️⃣ it doesn't require much time or effort — add a few drops, stir, and it's done;

2️⃣ it helps in situations where you aren't allowed to add extra ingredients in order not to change the texture;

3️⃣ it's convenient when you need an unusual flavor (bubble gum, California strawberry, tiramisu).

In addition to flavorings, you can enhance the aroma of your desserts with:

🔸 vanilla (pods / paste / extract)
🔸 spices and seasonings (pepper, cinnamon, cardamom, anise, coriander, star anise, cloves, ginger)
🔸 herbs and flowers (tarragon, basil, dill, thyme, rosemary, lavender)
🔸 tea (black, matcha, red, green)
🔸 fruit pastes
🔸 citrus zest
🔸 freeze-dried berries
🔸 tonka beans
🔸 coffee beans
🔸 alcoholic beverages (rum, brandy, liqueurs, infusions)
🔸 syrups
🔸 natural essential extracts and oils

And this list is far from complete!

We've figured out WHAT to use. Now let's talk about HOW to use it. There are several flavoring and infusion methods:

🔺 cold infusion
🔺 hot infusion
🔺 flavoring by simmering
🔺 vacuum sealing

COLD INFUSION

Cold infusion involves adding a specific flavoring component (spices, herbs, zest, etc.) to a liquid (cream, milk, berry purée) and then infusing it in the refrigerator for at least 12 hours.

This is the longest method — flavor develops slowly and is less intense than when using the hot infusion method.
To completely extract aroma from fresh herbs, you'll need 3–5 days, and from dried ones — about 20 days.

Continuing to explore the topic of flavor infusion, today we'll look at the hot method, which I use quite often when developing recipes.
THE HOT METHOD

Many of you have probably already guessed that the hot method involves heat treatment—and you're absolutely right.

Hot infusion means bringing a liquid to a boil together with spices, herbs, or other flavoring ingredients, and then letting it steep for 30–60 minutes.

You can use cream, milk, or berry purée as the base for infusion. For example, you can infuse cream with dried lavender flowers, as I did in the Blueberry–Lavender Cheesecake, or create a rich coffee aroma by infusing cream with ground coffee (the Coffee–Baileys Cheesecake).

It's very simple. But there are a few secrets I'd love to share with you.

SECRETS OF THE HOT METHOD

📌 Heat the liquid for infusion over medium or low heat so it warms up gradually.

📌 During the infusion process, try to use as little extra equipment as possible when preparing ingredients. For example, it's better to zest citrus directly into the cream rather than onto a board first and then transferring it. Spices, herbs, and citrus contain a large amount of essential oils, which evaporate easily.

📌 Once the liquid reaches a boil, remove the saucepan from the heat immediately. At excessively high temperatures, flavoring ingredients may release compounds that negatively affect the taste and aroma.

📌 After heating, it's best to cover the liquid with plastic wrap directly on the surface. If you use a lid, excess condensation may form and drip back into the liquid, and a film can develop on the surface that you'll later have to remove.

📌 After the infusion time, strain the liquid through a sieve or cheesecloth.

📌 Once completely cooled, the infused liquid can be stored in the refrigerator for the same shelf life as the base product. However, keep in mind that the aroma will gradually fade with each day of storage.

## Tip 002 — Factors That Affect Starch Functionality

**Source:** from B1-02

FACTORS THAT AFFECT STARCH FUNCTIONALITY

In previous posts, we discussed different types of starch and how starch works. Today, let's focus on what specifically affects the rate of starch thickening.

📚 So, the factors that influence the gelatinization temperature (t°) of starch include:

— TYPE OF STARCH — each type of starch has an optimal amount of heat required for proper gelatinization.

The time required for complete gelatinization of root starches is always shorter than that required for corn starch. In most cases, unmodified root starches should not be brought to a boil. When cooked for too long, unmodified root starches develop an excessively elastic or stringy texture.

— AMOUNT OF TENDERIZERS — sugars and fats slow down the rate at which starch granules absorb water and swell.

The slower they absorb water, the longer it takes for starch granules to gelatinize. In fact, if enough sugar is present, it can completely prevent starch gelatinization. This is one of the ways sugars and fats tenderize baked goods: they reduce the amount of gelatinized starch, which is responsible for structure formation. Sugar also increases the transparency of starch-thickened mixtures.

— AMOUNT OF ACID — acids hydrolyze (break down) large starch molecules into smaller ones, reducing their thickening power.

Acids also weaken starch granules, allowing them to gelatinize faster and more easily. In fact, if enough acid is present, gelatinization occurs so rapidly that the starch mixture may appear not to thicken at all.

## Tip 003 — Starch: How Does It Work?

**Source:** from B1-03

STARCH. How does it work?

The process depends on the temperature setting. When starch granules are placed in cold water, its molecules attract water molecules and the granules swell just slightly. However, when the water is heated, the starch granules undergo an irreversible process called GELATINIZATION.

There is an optimal amount of heat for proper thickening and gel formation:

— too little heat → too few granules swell

— too much heat → too many granules break down

Undercooking or overcooking starch results in insufficient thickening and gel formation.

UNDERCOOKING starch causes additional problems as well:

— because raw granules are hard and dense, undercooked starch feels gritty in the mouth

— undercooked starch is opaque and has an unpleasant starch taste

— if stored for a day or longer, undercooked starch mixtures tend to release droplets or even pools of water around the gel

There are many factors affect the gelatinization t° of starches. The higher the gelatinization t°, the longer starch takes to gelatinize and, thus, the greater the risk of undercooking.

Here are the factors that affect starch gelatinization t°:

• type of starch used

• amount of fats and sugar

• amount of acid

## Tip 004 — All About Starch: Types and Uses

**Source:** from B1-04

ALL ABOUT STARCH: TYPES AND USES

A starch molecule consists of two polysaccharides—amylose and amylopectin, which are themselves made of glucose.

Different types of starch have unique properties. There are four main types of starch:
⁃ Grain starches (corn, rice, wheat, waxy corn)
⁃ Root starches (potato, tapioca)
⁃ Modified starches
⁃ Instant (quick-action) starches

All starches start as either grain or root starches. Instant and modified starches are made from these by chemical treatment or physical processing.

ROOT starches differ from GRAIN starches because they contain fewer amylose molecules and more amylopectin:
⁃ they don't have a grainy taste
⁃ they have better clarity
⁃ they form a softer gel

MODIFIED starches are designer starches created by manufacturers to achieve specific desired properties:
⁃ increased resistance to excessive heat and acid, which usually thins products thickened with starch
⁃ better stability during freezing, which usually causes starch gels to shrink, stick together, or spread
⁃ to change texture
⁃ to speed up or slow down gelatinization

However, the primary reason for using modified starch is its enhanced stability.

INSTANT starches thicken and form gels without heating. To make instant starch, the manufacturer pre-cooks (gelatinizes) it and then dries it, so the granules absorb water without heat.

They are perfect for thickening heat-sensitive products. For example, the bright green color and delicate flavor of kiwi balls are preserved when thickened with instant starch.

## Tip 005 — Thickening and Gel Formation: What's the Difference?

**Source:** from B1-05

THICKENING and GEL FORMATION. What's the difference?

We all use starch and gelatin to make desserts, but few people really know how they work.

Let's break it down.

Thickeners and gelling agents (thick. & gell.) work by absorbing or holding a large amount of water. Fun fact: the most common thick. & gell. in baking is a plain old egg! 🥚

Thick. & gell. — like gelatin, plant gums, and starches — all have one thing in common: they're made of very large molecules. Some, like starches and gums, are polysaccharides. Others, like gelatin, are proteins.

POLYSACCHARIDES are giant molecules made of many sugar units (saccharides) linked together.

PROTEINS are giant molecules made of many amino acids linked together.

THICKENING happens when water and other molecules or particles in your recipe move very slowly.

This happens when:
⁃ Large molecules touch each other and tangle freely
⁃ Water is absorbed and trapped by swollen starch granules
⁃ Air bubbles (in foams) or fat droplets (in emulsions) slow down water movement

GEL FORMATION occurs when water and other molecules in the recipe cannot move at all.

This happens when large molecules bind tightly or tangle into a network that traps water and other substances.

Some gelling agents (like agar-agar) are so powerful that a gel can form even when water makes up over 99% of the product!

Some ingredients can do both — thicken in small amounts and form gels in larger amounts — like gelatin, corn starch, and pectin.

Other ingredients only thicken. They never form gels, no matter how much you add. They simply make the mixture thicker and stickier — similar to guar gum or waxy cornstarch.

## Tip 006 — Shelf Life of Frostings

**Source:** from B1-06

SHELF LIFE OF FROSTINGS

✅ Preparing frostings

I constantly remind my students that making frosting is a process inseparable from assembling the cake.

All whipped (aerated) frostings — buttercream, ganache, sour-cream or cream-cheese frosting — must be used right after preparation.

‼️ They cannot be stored and shouldn't be prepared in advance.

Butter-based frostings can be kept in the fridge at 2–6°C (~35–43°F) for no more than 5 hours.

✅ Storing finished products that contain frostings

The container must be clean, dry, and free of foreign odors.

All baked goods must be stored in the fridge at 2–6°C (~35–43°F) after being made.

The shelf life of frostings depends on their composition (eggs, alcohol, purées, fat content of dairy products, etc.) and storage conditions (room-temp display, fridge, and how often it is opened).

Buttercream frosting: 24 to 72 hours depending on ingredients (24 hours if eggs, like "Charlotte").

Buttercream can be frozen for up to 1 month.

Meringue frosting: up to 72 hours in the fridge, up to 12 hours at room temp (~20–22°C/~68–72°F).

Custard-based frosting: at 4–6°C (~39–43°F) it keeps 48–72 hours (2–3 days). At room temp (~23–50°C/~73–122°F) only 6–12 hours because it spoils quickly. Cool quickly to 4°C (~39°F) after cooking to prevent bacterial growth, especially in the 23–50°C (~73–122°F) range.

Sour-cream frosting: in the fridge 4–6°C (~39–43°F) - 12–36 hours depending on fat content and ingredients. At room temp (~23–50°C/~73–122°F) - up to 6 hours. If used in cakes, keeps up to 3 days in the fridge at 4–6°C (~39–43°F).

Cream-based frosting: depends on ingredients and preparation method. Cream-cheese, sour-cream-and-cream, and butter-and-cream frostings keep up to 36 hours in the fridge at 4–6°C (~39–43°F).

Cheesecake-style or cream-cheese frosting: up to 48 hours in the fridge at 4–6°C (~39–43°F). Cream cheese should not sit at room temp (~23–50°C/~73–122°F) for more than 2 hours.

Ganache: depending on composition, from 72 hours in the fridge 4–6°C (~39–43°F) to several months at room temp (~23–50°C/~73–122°F).

## Tip 007 — Mascarpone vs. Cream Cheese: Key Differences

**Source:** from B1-07

Mascarpone vs. Cream Cheese: Key Differences

Despite their similarities and wide use in cooking, mascarpone and cream cheese differ in production methods, composition, and properties.

Understanding these differences helps you choose the right product each time and substitute it when needed.

Mascarpone

🔸Origin: traditional Italian cheese
🔸Fat content: 70–75%, almost as rich as butter
🔸Flavor and aroma: delicate, creamy, with a buttery note; almost neutral, enhancing other flavors harmoniously
🔸Texture: very soft, silky, prone to separating if overwhipped
🔸Production: typically made by adding acid to warmed heavy cream. The combination of acid and heat coagulates the casein, forming a fine, smooth cheese mass
🔸Uses: classic ingredient in tiramisu; used in mousses, creams, and desserts where high fat content and velvety texture are desired

Cream Cheese

🔸Origin: industrially developed product, common in the US and Europe
🔸Fat content: on average 30–35%
🔸Flavor and aroma: mild with noticeable tang and slight saltiness, which helps balance the sweetness in desserts
🔸Texture: denser and more stable compared to mascarpone
🔸Production: made by adding lactic acid bacteria and often enzymes to milk or cream. Once the liquid whey is drained, the cheese is processed until it reaches the desired consistency
🔸Uses: base for cheesecakes, frostings, and fillings; provides structure and holds its shape well

Mascarpone offers a rich flavor, creaminess, and a buttery texture, but is less stable during processing.

Cream cheese ensures density and structure, adds a light tang that balances sweetness, but is less intensely creamy.

To sum up, the choice depends on the desired result:
• Mascarpone is ideal for delicate frostings and mousse-based desserts
• Cream cheese is best for recipes where stability, shape, and flavor balance are important👌

## Tip 008 — Why Baking Soda ≠ Baking Powder?

**Source:** from B1-08

Why baking soda ≠ baking powder? 🤔

And how to know which one you need for what you're baking?

✔️Baking soda

Works only in combination with an acid—kefir, lemon juice, honey, etc.

The reaction starts immediately as soon as the ingredients are combined. Carbon dioxide begins to release, so the dough must go straight into the oven. If you wait too long, the baked goods won't rise properly.

✔️Baking powder

It's a combination of baking soda and a dry acid.

It comes in several forms, the most common being double-acting. Baked goods made with double-acting baking powder need heat to fully activate the leavening. A small amount of carbon dioxide is released when mixing; the rest is released during baking. This removes the need for rushing to portion and bake to ensure the dough rises just as good.

✔️Ammonium (ammonium carbonate)

Lesser known, but a very effective leavening agent.

Perfect for thin and dry baked goods— various cookies, including gingerbread cookies, wafers. Gives incredible crispness and lightness. However, it's not used in large cakes or loaves: the smell of ammonia can stay in the finished product.

❗️Substitution

•Baking soda for baking powder—1 : 3 or triple the amount of baking powder.

•Baking powder for baking soda—3 : 1 or use 1 part soda + 2 parts cream of tartar for every 3 parts baking powder.

👉So if you're making sponges or cakes with acidic ingredients, you can use baking soda.

👉If you need more control and stability—choose baking powder or a combo of soda + baking powder.

👉For thin crispy cookies or gingerbread dough—ammonium will be your secret ingredient.

## Tip 009 — Why Does Crème Anglaise Turn Out Silky vs. Curdled?

**Source:** from B1-09

Why does crème anglaise turn out silky and delicate for some, but curdled and eggy for others?
👉 And which cooking method is the safest?

Crème anglaise—also known as vanilla custard sauce—serves as the base for many desserts, including Bavarian creams, ice cream, and crèmeux. It's made from milk, sugar, and egg yolks that are gently heated until lightly thickened.

⚡ Here precision is key: the temperature should not exceed about 82°C (~180°F), otherwise the yolks will scramble. Always cook slowly and attentively.

💡 Technically, it can be cooked up to 85°C (~185°F) without curdling, but it's safer to stop slightly earlier, as the sauce continues to heat even after being removed from the stove due to the residual heat in the pan.

🥄 Main tips for success:

Whisk yolks with sugar right away—if you let them sit, the sugar will draw out moisture, leaving lumps of dried yolk.

Heat the milk almost to boiling so the process goes faster.

Temper the yolks by slowly pouring in the hot milk in a thin stream while whisking constantly.

Cook only over low heat, stirring continuously.

Test doneness in two ways:
• With a thermometer—82°C (~180°F).
• By the spoon test—the sauce should lightly coat the back of a spoon instead of running off like milk.

Keep in mind this is a very light sauce, so don't expect it to thicken too much.

Immediately strain the sauce into a bowl set over an ice bath to cool quickly. Stir occasionally to ensure even cooling.

💡 If the sauce curdles, sometimes you can rescue it. Quickly add 30–60 ml (2–4 tbsp) of cold milk, transfer to a blender, and blend on high speed until smooth again.

Cooking methods:

▶️ Steaming—slowest, but the safest method.
▶️ Bowl in simmering water—a balance of speed and control.
▶️ Direct heat—best for experienced chefs, since the risk of ending up with scrambled eggs is much higher.

## Tip 010 — Butter: The Ideal (But Tricky) Fat

**Source:** from B1-10

Butter — the ideal fat for any baked goods, but along with its advantages, it also comes with some obvious drawbacks.

Let's break it down.

Advantages of butter:
• Pleasant mouthfeel;
• Rich, creamy flavor.

Drawbacks of butter:
• Expensive — several times pricier than alternative fats;
• One of the trickiest fats to work with due to its narrow plasticity range—too hard to work with straight from the fridge, but it softens and melts very quickly in a warm room.

❗️The best temperature for creaming butter lies in a very narrow window: 18–21°C (~64–70°F).

• Spoils faster than other fats, especially unsalted butter. It's highly prone to bacterial spoilage if not kept refrigerated for short storage or frozen for longer storage. Spoiled butter tastes sour, like bad milk, or develops a sharp rancid flavor.

One of the trickiest parts of working with butter—especially for beginners—is finding the right consistency for creaming.

❗️Cold butter (below 16°C/~60°F) is too firm to trap nice, even air pockets, while overly warm butter (above 24°C/~75°F) is too soft.

However, just as important as the butter temperature is, the room temperature is also crucial.

In summer, even if you start with butter at the perfect (~21°C/~70°F), after just a few minutes of mixing, the warmth of the bowl and additional friction heat can quickly raise the butter's temperature to 24–25°C (~75–77°F). At that point, it creams very poorly.

❗️That's why in summer, I always begin creaming butter at no higher than 16–17°C (~60–63°F).

Also, pay attention to mixing speed—it should be a moderate (medium) speed.

If you whip on high speed, the friction heats the ingredients too much. You'll trap fewer air pockets, and the ones that do form will often be too large and uneven.

## Tip 011 — Sugar: Caramelization and the Maillard Reaction

**Source:** from B1-11

Sugar: Caramelization and the Maillard reaction—what they are and how they differ

In the culinary world, these two chemical processes play a key role in creating the color, aroma, and taste of many desserts. They are similar, but have important differences that every professional should understand ⬇️

Caramelization is the process of heating sugar, during which it melts, darkens, and develops a characteristic sweet, nutty flavor and aroma. It is a chemical reaction that occurs at high temperatures (over 160–170°C), when sugar breaks down into different compounds, creating new flavors and colors.

The Maillard reaction is a chemical interaction between sugars and amino acids in proteins (for example, from flour, eggs, milk), which starts at lower temperatures and produces more complex roasted flavors such as cocoa, coffee, and nuts.

Even a small amount of protein greatly accelerates this reaction, which is why baked goods with dairy products and eggs brown faster.

The Maillard reaction can occur slowly even at room temperature — for example, in powdered milk or white chocolate, which change color and taste over time.

❕ A few more important points:

• Monosaccharides (fructose, glucose) brown faster than disaccharides (sucrose), so invert syrup speeds up browning.

• Among sweeteners, the browning speed usually goes (from fastest to slowest):
fructose > glucose > lactose > maltose > sucrose > isomalt.

• Minerals such as copper and iron, even in trace amounts, significantly enhance browning. They are present in some water, unrefined syrups (molasses, honey, maple syrup), and salt.

• The pH of the environment also matters: alkalis (such as baking soda) raise pH and increase browning, while acids (kefir, sour cream, cream of tartar) slow it down.

• Higher baking temperatures intensify browning, which is why the crust on bread or sponge cake is always darker than the inside.

## Tip 012 — Sugar: How It Works in Batter

**Source:** from B1-12

Sugar: How It Works in Batter

All sugars are hygroscopic, meaning they have the ability to attract and retain water.

That's why sugar plays a key role in:
✔️creating a tender texture
✔️retaining moisture
✔️extending shelf life

CREATING TENDER TEXTURES

Once dissolved, sugar prevents:
• gluten formation
• protein coagulation
• starch gelatinization

In other words, sugar slows down structure formation — and because of that, the batter has more time in the oven to rise, and the baked good remains soft and tender after baking.

Why does this happen?

Because gluten, proteins, and starch need water to form structure, and sugar — as a hygroscopic substance — absorbs and binds water, leaving less available liquid for the structure-builders.

Sugar raises the temperature at which proteins coagulate and starch gelatinizes, which delays structure formation.

👉 As a result, the more sugar in a recipe, the more tender the baked good becomes.

❕BUT it's important to find the right balance

▪ If there's too much sugar, structure barely forms: the product either doesn't rise, or rises — and quickly collapses when cooled.

▪ If there's too little sugar, structure forms too early — before steam has time to expand the dough. The baked good ends up with poor volume, and its surface tears because steam breaks through the prematurely formed crust.

RETAINING MOISTURE AND EXTENDING SHELF LIFE

Sugar's hygroscopicity is the main reason why baked goods stay soft even after a few days.

🔹 The most effective for this is fructose — it retains moisture better than any other sugar.

High-fructose syrups also work well, including:

– invert syrup
– honey
– molasses
– agave syrup

They retain moisture better than regular granulated sugar and are often used in recipes where lasting freshness is important.

## Tip 013 — Hot Cream & Cool Nerves: Everything About Caramel

**Source:** from B1-13

Hot Cream & Cool Nerves: Everything You Need To Know About Caramel 👌

Caramel can be temperamental, but it's not nearly as scary once you know a few important things. All it really "asks" for is attention, consistency, and a little respect for physics!

❕ Here are some essential tips 👇

• To avoid crystallization, glucose syrup is often used—about 10–20% of the sugar amount. It helps keep the mixture smooth. If you don't have syrup, it's very important that your cookware is perfectly clean.

• Another critical factor is the heavy whipping cream. It must be hot. Cold cream coming into contact with dry caramel causes a shock reaction: the mixture seizes, splatters, and forms lumps. To avoid this, add very hot cream gradually, in small portions, while constantly stirring.

• In large-batch recipes, it's helpful to divide the sugar into several parts. For example, melt part of it first, then gradually add the rest. This helps better control color, temperature, and prevents uneven melting.

• Caramel can be made in two ways:

dry (no liquid added)

or wet (with a small amount of water).
If you opt for the wet method, remember: do not stir the syrup, as this may trigger crystallization.

• Judging the degree of caramelization by the color in the saucepan isn't ideal—it often looks darker than it actually is. To see the true shade, let some caramel drip off your spatula. That way, you can get a better sense of the real color.

• It's best to add the butter once the caramel has cooled slightly (to about 45–50°C/113-122°F). This reduces the risk of splitting and gives you a smooth, silky texture with no oil on the surface and a gentler, creamy flavor.

Nothing supernatural here—just a clear understanding of how caramel behaves at each stage.

---

## Tip 014 — Factors Affecting Meringue Stability: Temperature, Density, Whipping Time

**Source:** from B1-14

Factors affecting meringue stability: TEMPERATURE, DENSITY OF EGG WHITES, WHIPPING TIME

Lets put the final touches on our topic of Meringue ✨

🔸 Temperature

Egg whites straight from the fridge don't whip well.
The ideal temperature for whipping is room temperature, around 21 °C (~70°F).

Warm egg whites capture air better, foam faster, and create a more stable structure.

🔸 Density of egg whites (freshness)

Runny (older) whites are easy to whip, give more volume,
❕but the foam is less stable and collapses quickly.
Fresh whites are thicker, and harder to whip,
❕but the foam structure is strong and stable.

📌If volume is important—it's better to use older eggs.
📌If you need stability—choose fresh ones, and leave the older whites for regular baking.

🔸Whipping time

Both extremes—underwhipping and overwhipping—hurt meringue stability.

⏩Underwhipped whites—proteins don't aggregate enough to form a solid film.
This kind of foam is unstable and spreads quickly.
⏩Overwhipped whites—with too long or aggressive whipping, the proteins denature and over-aggregate. The film around each air bubble becomes overstretched and loses elasticity.

In other words, overwhipping has a curdling/separating effect on the egg white structure, similar to overheating.
The result is tightly bound, inelastic clumps of protein sitting in expelled liquid. This mass is no longer usable—you'll have to throw it away.

🔸Other factors

⏩Salt
Decreases foam stability. It shouldn't be added during whipping.

⏩Whisk type
Thick wires—create large bubbles, less stable foam.
Thin whisk—creates small, uniform bubbles, more stable foam.

## Tip 015 — Top 7 Tips for Baking Sponge Cakes

**Source:** from B1-15

TOP 7 TIPS FOR BAKING SPONGE CAKES

1.The batter should be a smooth, homogeneous emulsion

Properly mixed batter is an emulsion of water in fat; which means that water is held in the form of tiny droplets surrounded by fat.
Proper emulsification ensures the right texture.

2.All ingredients should be at 21–22°C (~70–72°F)

At this temperature, eggs are more stable, gain more volume, and emulsify better with fats.
Even one cold ingredient can cause the batter to split.

3.Proper preparation of ingredients, tools, and a preheated oven — "mise en place", as French chefs say 👌

Always carefully read the entire recipe before you start the process.

4.Opening the oven door too early or moving the pans during baking may cause the sponge to collapse

This leads to a temperature drop and can damage the proteins and starches that are not yet fully set.

5.Thin sponge layers should be baked at higher temperatures

Baking thin sponge layers at low temperatures increases moisture loss, thus drying the sponge.
Tall sponge layers are always baked at lower temperatures, allowing them to cook all the way to the center while gently browning on the outside.

6.Properly baked pound cake sponges slightly pull away from the sides of the pan, while egg-foam sponges should be soft to the touch and spring back when gently pressed

You can use a bamboo skewer to check for doneness, but the best method is by look and touch.
For thin layers, gently lift the edge with an offset spatula — browning on the bottom is a sign of doneness.

7.Baked sponge layers should cool completely on a wire rack

Wrapping warm sponges traps too much moisture, making the layers sticky later on.

## Tip 016 — Factors Affecting Meringue Stability: Fats & Acids

**Source:** from B1-16

Factors Affecting Meringue Stability:

❕FATS & ACIDS

LIPIDS
Everyone knows that if even a drop of yolk gets into your egg whites, it's best not to whip them into meringue.

But do you know why? What exactly does that yolk do that even beginner bakers are well aware it's the sworn enemy of perfect meringue?

Let's break it down⬇️

Lipids — fats, oils, and emulsifiers — interfere with aeration. Depending on their type and amount, lipids can either slow down or completely prevent aeration.

This is especially true for the lipids found in egg yolks, particularly lecithin. Even a small amount of yolk can prevent egg whites from whipping properly.

Lipids disrupt aeration by coating the proteins, thus preventing them from unfolding and bonding. But they do more than that — lipids compete with proteins for space on the surface of air bubbles. Since lipids on their own can't form a strong, cohesive, and elastic network like egg whites can, the bubbles they coat expand quickly and then collapse.

PRO TIP
To avoid problems with fats, oils, or emulsifiers when making meringue, always use a clean bowl, carefully separate every trace of yolk from the whites, and avoid plastic bowls and utensils — they tend to absorb grease and are difficult to fully clean.

ACID

Acid helps stabilize the meringue by lowering the pH. Cream of tartar is the most commonly used acid, but lemon juice or vinegar can also stabilize the foam.

Add acid early in the whipping process — it may take a bit longer to whip, but the resulting protein network will be more elastic and resistant to overwhipping, folding, piping, and baking. The meringue will also be whiter.

---

## Tip 017 — Factors Affecting Meringue Stability: Sugar

**Source:** from B1-17

Factors Affecting Meringue Stability: SUGAR

Meringue stability is vital. A stable meringue is dense and thick, yet elastic and springy, so it can easily withstand mixing, piping, and baking

Often, the very thing that increases meringue's stability also reduces its volume and tenderness. As always, the goal of a pastry chef is to balance these opposing characteristics — stability with volume and delicacy

So, what exactly affects the stability of egg white foam?

• sugar
• lipids (fats, oils, emulsifiers)
• acidity
• temperature
• protein density
• whipping time
• salt
• type of whisk

Sugar

Sugar greatly stabilizes meringue, although it also slows down the whipping process and slightly reduces the volume. In French meringue, where room-temperature egg whites are whipped with sugar, the sugar stabilizes it best when added slowly and in the proper amount

Sugar should be added gradually to give the sugar crystals time to dissolve and avoid weighing down the foam. In addition, if sugar is added too quickly, the protein molecules may not unfold properly.* As a result, we may end up with a softer meringue or, in extreme cases, egg whites that won't whip at all

*Remember from the previous post: the unfolding of protein molecules is one of the two key conditions for creating egg white foam

Sugar stabilizes meringue by slowing down the unfolding and aggregation (binding) of protein molecules. This contributes to stability by protecting against overwhipping

Another way sugar stabilizes meringue is by dissolving into the liquid film of the foam, creating a thick, viscous syrup that protects air bubbles from collapsing

Sugar syrup also adds a satiny shine to the surface of the meringue

USEFUL TIPS

➡️ Undissolved sugar crystals weigh down the meringue and weaken it. After baking, these crystals attract moisture and can sometimes form unappealing syrup beads on the surface of the meringue
To minimize beading and volume loss, use powdered sugar instead of granulated sugar. Always add it slowly, allowing it to fully dissolve between additions

➡️ Since sugar slows down whipping, be sure to add it only after the egg whites have reached the "beer foam" stage

---

## Tip 018 — How Liquid Egg Whites Turn Into Foam During Whipping

**Source:** from B1-18

Have you ever wondered how liquid egg whites turn into foam during whipping?

When you whip eggs, two things happen simultaneously.

Air bubbles are beaten into the liquid, and the proteins in the egg whites denature—or unfold. The unfolded proteins move quickly through the liquid to the surface of the air bubbles.

Once there, neighboring proteins bond—or aggregate—around the bubbles, forming a film-like network. Surrounded by these strong, flexible films, the air bubbles are less likely to burst, allowing more bubbles to be beaten in.

What happens to egg white proteins during whipping is similar, though not identical, to what occurs with them during heating. In both cases, protein molecules unfold and bond, forming a type of structure.

## Tip 020 — Poor Cake Texture? Creaming the Butter: Simple Rules

**Source:** from B1-20

POOR CAKE TEXTURE?
CREAMING THE BUTTER: SIMPLE RULES

The process of making batter often begins with creaming the butter and sugar. Few people are aware of this, but this step is crucial for achieving the desired texture.

After all, it's during creaming that air bubbles get trapped in the batter. A fine-pored texture is the result of many small, uniform air bubbles. Large and uneven bubbles in the batter lead to a coarse texture.

⚠️The correct ingredient temperature 🌡️ and mixing speed are two essential conditions for forming perfect air pockets.

Cold butter is too firm to form a large number of tiny air bubbles, while overly warm butter is too soft.

The mixer speed should be moderate. If you cream at high speed, friction will quickly warm the butter. Fewer air bubbles will form, and they'll be large and uneven.

## Tip 021 — A Whisk, a Paddle, or a Dough Hook? (Parts 1–3, incl. Creaming Method)

**Source:** from B1-21

A WHISK, A PADDLE, OR A DOUGH HOOK?
At first glance, the answer seems simple: ✔️ A whisk for whipping ✔️ A paddle for mixing soft dough or batter ✔️ A dough hook for kneading stiff dough

But in practice, as I've observed, these distinctions often confuse students. Choosing the correct attachment isn't always straightforward!

For example, should you use a whisk or a paddle attachment when creaming butter for a sponge cake or cupcake?

What about making frosting or shortbread dough?

Is a whisk better when mixing the cheese filling for a cheesecake, or should you opt for a paddle attachment? Perhaps a paddle with flexible silicone edges?

And what if you're using a hand mixer—does that change the choice entirely?😳

Let's break it down entirely because selecting the proper attachment is crucial to achieving perfect desserts.

WHISK

The whisk is specifically designed for whipping, a process where the goal is to incorporate as many tiny air bubbles as possible into the mixture.

You'll need a whisk for tasks like whipping whole eggs for sponge cakes, beating egg whites for meringue, preparing egg yolks for sponge cake or frosting, whipping cream or any cream-based frosting, preparing marshmallows, mousse, or soufflés with gelatin.

Seems straightforward, right? But here's a twist…

Have you ever noticed that some Kenwood whisks have thin rods while others have thicker ones? 🤔

Yes, the thickness of your whisk's rods truly matters. A whisk with thick rods cannot whip meringue or cream to achieve the correct consistency.

Foams whipped with thick rods will have large, uneven, and unstable air bubbles. This results in sponge cakes with poor volume and mousses that lack tenderness and airiness.

Therefore, if your stand mixer only comes with a whisk with thick rods, I recommend purchasing one with thin rods separately. Until then, you'll need to whip meringues and cream using a hand mixer.

So, what is a whisk with thick rods good for? It's perfect for preparing frostings made with cream cheese or mascarpone combined with heavy whipped cream.

PADDLE – This attachment is used for mixing batters, soft doughs and for creaming butter.

Yes, creaming! While we often use the term "whipping," in baking literature, butter is always "creamed," not whipped.

First of all, I want to sincerely thank you all for the incredible engagement on the previous post. Your comments and ❤️s truly inspire me to keep sharing more valuable content with you.

Today, let's talk about the CREAMING METHOD—a fundamental technique in baking that involves softening a solid fat, such as butter or shortening, into a smooth, homogeneous mass before combining it with other ingredients. This method is commonly used when making buttercream, cake and cupcake batters, and cookie dough.

During creaming, fat is whipped together with sugar/dry ingredients until the mixture becomes light and fluffy, and increases in volume. This happens because tiny air bubbles are incorporated into the mass and retained within the semi-solid fat. These air bubbles remain in the batter and expand during baking, contributing to the leavening and fluffy texture of the final product.

So, if our goal is to introduce air into the butter—just like we do when whipping cream—why don't we use a whisk?

The answer lies in the nature of the ingredients. A whisk is perfect for incorporating air into liquid ingredients like eggs or cream. Butter, however, is best creamed while it's in a solid but pliable (plastic) state.

If you try using a whisk, it will likely become clogged with butter, causing the mixer to work "idle". That's why a paddle attachment is more suitable for creaming butter.

But you might rightfully point out that a paddle attachment can also just smear the butter along the sides of the bowl, causing the mixer to run idle as well.

And you'd be absolutely right—especially when working with a small amount of butter and using a paddle that doesn't have flexible silicone edges. In that case, you'll often need to stop the mixer periodically to scrape the butter off the sides and bottom of the bowl manually.

This is where the paddle attachment with soft silicone edges becomes a game changer…

## Tip 022 — How to Work with Shortcrust Pastry Effectively (Parts 1–2)

**Source:** from B1-22

HOW TO WORK WITH SHORTCRUST PASTRY EFFECTIVELY?

Working with shortcrust pastry is a very detailed topic, but I have compiled key points to help you achieve good results.

1. Proper Mixing is Key to Success

The success of your pastry largely depends on correct mixing techniques. If the dough is overmixed or mixed improperly—using the wrong tool or speed—you may end up with a tough product instead of a delicate, crumbly texture. This happens due to the overdevelopment of gluten when water from the butter and eggs interacts with the flour.
✔️Recommendation: Mix the dough as quickly as possible using the correct technique to isolate the proteins and minimize gluten formation.

2. Chilling the Dough

Chilling the dough is crucial. After mixing, the dough should rest to ensure the flour is fully hydrated and the fat solidifies.
✔️Recommendation: Refrigerate the dough for several hours before use or before freezing. If you freeze it immediately, the water will freeze without properly hydrating the flour.

3. Working Temperature is Key to Pliability

The dough should be elastic and soft, but not overly pliable. Before rolling out chilled dough, work it gently with a rolling pin or by hand to enhance its flexibility.

🔜 In the upcoming post, I will discuss the following topics:
• Rolling the dough;
• Chilling after rolling;
• Baking.

4. Rolling and Stretching

📏 For optimal results, roll out the dough between two sheets of parchment paper or plastic wrap. Once the dough is soft and pliable, use gentle stretching motions.
❗️Important: Roll to a thickness of 3 mm. To ensure even thickness, place two strips of the same height on either side of the dough.

5. Chilling After Rolling

❄️ After rolling, allow the dough to cool again so it firms up and retains its shape when transferred to a mold or baking sheet.
⚠️ Caution: Unevenly rolled dough may bake inconsistently, leading to burnt thin areas and undercooked thicker sections.

6. Baking

🔥 Shortcrust pastry can be baked at temperatures ranging from 160 °C to 220 °C (~320 - 428 °F) with 190 °C (~374 °F) being ideal. Utilize perforated baking sheets or trays to prevent air pockets.
✔️Tip: Employ two perforated silicone sheets for consistently shaped cookies.

## Tip 023 — How to Make Cut Cake Slice Look Unforgettable

**Source:** from B1-23

HOW TO MAKE CUT CAKE SLICE LOOK UNFORGETTABLE? 🍰

We're all familiar with the classic cake assembly process: cake layer, frosting, filling, cake layer. However, did you know that by slightly altering this technique, you can create something truly spectacular that will leave everyone in awe?

Here are some creative ways to make your cake slices look truly impressive:

1️⃣ DUAL-COLORED FROSTING
Use two different shades of frosting and pipe them in concentric circles. The colors can be either complementary or contrasting. For a striking visual effect, try assembling a Coffee-Baileys cake.

2️⃣ INNOVATIVE FILLING TECHNIQUE
Instead of layering the filling on top of the frosting, pipe it inside of it, or alternate filling rings with frosting rings on one layer. The result will be visually stunning! We recommend trying this method with cakes like Pistachio-Raspberry and Black Currant-Coffee.

3️⃣ VERTICAL CAKE ROLL
For a unique twist, assemble your cake vertically by rolling the layers into a large spiral. This technique works best with thin, flexible cake layers and is guaranteed to impress. Try this with the Raspberry-Lychee-Rose honey cake or the Mega Chocolate Cherry honey cake.

4️⃣ CONTRASTING FILLINGS
Incorporate multiple fillings with contrasting or complementary colors, such as jelly with frosting or jelly with mousse. Experiment like this with the Raspberry-Rose honey cake for a striking presentation.

## Tip 024 — Guidelines for Storing, Sweetening, and Mixing Heavy Whipped Cream

**Source:** from B1-24

Guidelines for Storing, Sweetening, and Mixing Heavy Whipped Cream 👩🏻‍🍳

Continuing our discussion on working with heavy whipped cream, here are some essential tips👇🏻

SWEETENING 🧁
The classic method for sweetening heavy whipped cream is adding sugar near the end of the whipping process when the cream forms soft peaks.

👆🏻Alternatively, you can add sugar at the beginning of the whipping process. This will significantly increase the whipping time, so it's best to do this with a mixer rather than by hand.

❕ For sweetening, use very fine granulated sugar or sifted powdered sugar for better stability.

WHIPPING AND MIXING 🥣
1. Stop whipping once the heavy whipped cream forms peaks that hold their shape. 👆🏻If you continue whipping, the cream will first become grainy and then separate into butter and whey.

2. Avoid overbeating heavy whipped cream that will be mixed with other ingredients. During mixing, the cream will continue to whip, potentially leading to overwhipping.

3. Add flavorings only after the heavy whipped cream has been whipped.

STORAGE❄️
If the heavy whipped cream won't be used immediately, cover it and store it in the refrigerator.

## Tip 025 — Top Tips for Whipping Cream

**Source:** from B1-25

Top Tips for Whipping Cream 👩🏻‍🍳

👇🏻 Heavy whipped cream is not only one of the most popular frostings but also a crucial component in many desserts, such as mousses.

💬If you find this topic useful, hit the ❤️, save the post, and share any challenges you face when working with heavy whipped cream in the comments.

When preparing heavy whipped cream, follow these expert guidelines:

1. Age Matters: Use a heavy whipping cream that is at least one day old. Very fresh, heavy whipped cream may not whip as well.

2. Keep It Cool: Thoroughly chill the heavy whipped cream and all equipment, especially in warm weather. Heavy whipped cream that isn't sufficiently cooled can be difficult to whip and may separate easily.

4. Right Tools, Right Speed: Use a whisk with thin rods and set your mixer to medium speed for whipping.

5. Don't Overwhip! Properly whipped cream should double in volume and form soft peaks.

As you whip, fat particles begin to cluster. If you continue whipping to stiff peaks, more fat particles will bond together, making your whipping cream thicker but without any increase in volume.

❕Completely overwhipped cream results in the fat separating from the liquid. In other words, you'll have butter and the whey.

❌Heavy whipped cream with very stiff peaks will be difficult to incorporate into a mousse and may cause overmixing.

✅Correctly whipped cream will ensure stable volume and the ideal final texture in your mousse.

## Tip 026 — Tempering Gelatin: A Step-by-Step Guide

**Source:** from B1-26

Tempering Gelatin: A Step-by-Step Guide

You asked for it, and here it is 🔥 Be sure to save this post for easy reference 👌🏻

❕ When working with stabilizers, paying close attention to product temperatures, the quantities used, and the tempering process is crucial.
Here's a step-by-step guide to ensure success👇🏻

1. Soak the Gelatin
✔️ For sheet gelatin, soak in ice water for 15 minutes.
✔️ For powdered gelatin, soak in the liquid specified in the recipe for 5 minutes. If the recipe doesn't specify the amount/type of liquid to use, use water in an amount six times the weight of the gelatin.

2. Dissolve the Gelatin
☀️ For warm processes (e.g., Bavarian cream), add the soaked gelatin to the warm mixture, such as a Crème Anglaise.
❄️ For cold processes (e.g., fruit mousse), reheat the gelatin in a steam bath or microwave until fully dissolved.

3. Incorporate Gelatin
Add 10% of the mousse mixture to the dissolved gelatin.
4. Heat the Mixture
Heat to 38-40 °C (~100-105 °F).
5. Combine with Mousse
Gradually return the heated gelatin mixture to the mousse, mixing gently in smooth, circular motions.

❕Whether your mousse is stabilized with cocoa butter or gelatin, there are key indicators that the process was executed correctly👇🏻

✔️Your mousse should be:
• Smooth
• Free of lumps
• Shiny
• Elastic
🌡These characteristics indicate that the cocoa butter was at the proper temperature and did not crystallize during mixing.

## Tip 027 — Common Mistakes When Working with Mousses

**Source:** from B1-27

Common Mistakes When Working with Mousses🥣

Mousses generally consist of three key components:

✔️ Base (such as crème anglaise, custard, ganache, cheese mixture, or fruit purée)
✔️ Aerator (such as egg foam or whipped cream)
✔️ Stabilizer (such as cocoa butter, gelatin, or agar).

Mistakes can happen within each component individually or across multiple components simultaneously.

The most frequent errors include:

▫️ Underwhipped or overwhipped aerators, such as creams and meringues.
▫️ Incorrect temperature conditions when incorporating stabilizers, such as cocoa butter or gelatin.

✔️ For instance, chocolate mousse should be smooth, shiny, and elastic after the addition of chocolate. This texture indicates that the cocoa butter was at the correct temperature and did not crystallize during mixing.

✔️ When using stabilizers, it's crucial to pay close attention to their temperature, quantity (as specified in the recipe), and the tempering process.

## Tip 028 — Sponge Cake Defects and How to Avoid Them

**Source:** from B1-28

Sponge Cake Defects and How to Avoid Them
❤️ Thank you for your comments and engagement.
This topic is highly relevant and requires a detailed examination of various nuances.

🤝As promised, I have identified the three most common defects and will tell you how to resolve them👇🏻:
1. SPONGE CAKES COLLAPSE DURING BAKING OR COOLING:
✔️The cakes may have been moved around before the structure was set (coagulation of proteins and gelatinization of starch). Avoid opening the oven or moving the cakes during the initial stages of baking.
✔️Incorrect proportions—insufficient drying agents or too much liquid in the batter.
✔️Excessive chemical leavening agent. Ensure all ingredients are accurately measured.

2. CAKE TOP CRACKS DURING BAKING:
✔️Baking temperature is too high.
✔️Too much flour or the flour is too strong.
✔️Insufficient liquid ingredients.
✔️Incorrect kneading technique.

3. UNEVEN FORM:
✔️Incorrect batter kneading technique.
✔️Uneven oven heating—try baking in Convection mode.
✔️Deformed/misshapen baking rings.
✔️Uneven oven grates—ensure the grates and baking trays are level.
✔️Uneven distribution of batter—ensure the batter is evenly spread before baking.
✔️Uneven distribution of baking powder—sift the baking powder with the dry ingredients.

## Tip 029 — Avoid Doing This When Working with Frosting

**Source:** from B1-29

AVOID DOING THIS WHEN WORKING WITH FROSTING🍰

Most frostings and mousses are typically composed of three main components:
✔️ Base (such as crème anglaise, custard, ganache, cheese or fruit puree)
✔️ Aerators (egg foam, whipped cream)
✔️ Stabilizers (cocoa butter, gelatin, or agar)

⚠️Errors can occur when working with these components individually or in combination.
Here are some common mistakes to watch out for and some quick notes on how to correct them:

▫️INSUFFICIENT FAT CONTENT AND IMPROPERLY CHILLED CREAM
Whipping cream should have a fat content of at least 30–33%, ideally 33–35%, and be cooled to approximately 4°C (~39°F).

▫️INCORRECT TEMPERATURE OF BUTTER FOR WHIPPING
Butter that is either too cold or too warm can lead to improper whipping results. This is a frequent yet often overlooked mistake among students.

▫️UNDERWHIPPED OR OVERWHIPPED FROSTING
It's easy to overbeat frostings such as Chantilly cream, mascarpone-based frostings, and whipped ganaches, especially those made with dark chocolate.

▫️SEPARATED BUTTERCREAM
This can occur for two reasons: differing temperatures of the ingredients or improper proportions of fat and water needed to achieve a perfect emulsion.

## Tip 030 — The Four Ingredient Function Categories in Cake Recipes

**Source:** from B1-30

Did you know that the ingredients in every cake recipe can be categorized into four main groups based on their functions?

The "perfect recipe" is achieved when these four categories are balanced.

Understanding the primary functions of these ingredients and their correct ratios not only allows you to modify recipes to suit your taste but also to evaluate and correct untested recipes.

Interested? Let's dive into it.

✔️Tougheners or Stabilizers:
These ingredients, including flour and eggs, provide a stable texture.

Insufficient structure formers result in a delicate sponge cake that crumbles in your hands. Have you experienced this? I certainly have.

✔️Softeners or Tenderizers:
These ingredients, such as sugar, fat, and chemical leavening agents, ensure softness by shortening protein fibers.

A lack of softeners leads to a dense, hardened cake that is challenging to cut with a knife. Does this sound familiar?

✔️Moisturizers:
Ingredients like water, milk, syrups, and eggs provide moisture.

Interestingly, some ingredients serve multiple functions. You didn't think it was all so straightforward, did you?

If a cake is too dry, increasing the amount of moisturizers can help. However, be cautious, as most ingredients have multiple roles. For example, adding more eggs to a dry cake could result in an even drier and tougher texture.

✔️ Dryers:
Ingredients such as flour, starch, and cocoa absorb moisture.

The idea of a well-balanced recipe formula lies in the proportionate amounts of stabilizers that will balance out the softeners and dryers that will balance out the moisturizers. For instance, increasing stabilizers requires a corresponding increase in softeners.

---

## Tip 031 — Flour Theory: Protein, Starch, and Damaged Starch Granules

**Source:** from B1-31

Have been delving into the theory of flour for a while.

I have known about the power and importance of gluten (protein) for quite some time. But this time it's a completely different level.

In nutshell. In addition to protein (~12%), the main part of flour is starch (~71%).

On which type of wheat for making flour is used (hard or soft kernel) will depend not only the quantity and quality of protein, the texture of the flour, but also the number of broken and damaged starch granules.

That is, the harder the wheat kernel, the more difficult it is to grind it into fine flour and the more starch granules will be damaged in the process.

And damaged starch granules absorb much more water than intact ones, which slows staling.

Damaged granules are also more susceptible than intact ones to breakdown down by amylase, which further slows staling.

In addition, as the amylase breaks down starches into sugars, yeast fermentation can increase.

That's why flour for bread is milled from hard red wheat, which not only has a high protein content, but also many damaged starch granules.

Bread flours are typically used for pan breads, rolls, croissants and sweet yeast doughs.

---

## Tip 032 — How to Cover a Cake with Crumbs Neatly

**Source:** from B1-32

How to cover a cake with crumbs neatly?

We've repeatedly been asked this question in DM, so we decided to make a short video in which we would share the main tips of this simple technique:

☝️first, grind the dry crumbs in a food processor, wet — using hands, and then pass them through a large sieve so that the crumbs become fine and uniform;

☝️secondly, in order for crumbs to hold well on the sides of the cake, you should do this technique immediately after the final leveling. If using ganache, work especially quickly;

☝️thirdly, start applying crumbs from the very bottom, gradually moving up. Scoop up a large handful and gently press it to the surface. Repeat until the desired part of the cake is covered. After that, work with a small spatula — scoop up the crumbs from the cake board and pull it up with a light, careful movements. In this way, you will close the gaps and remove the excess, making the coating as uniform as possible;

☝️and, fourthly, work on a turntable and do not forget to put a large sheet of parchment paper next to it, so that cleaning the kitchen does not take half a day later.

That's all! Nothing complicated, and the cake already looks festive.

## Tip 033 — 2 Secret Ingredients for the Perfect Carrot Sponge

**Source:** from B1-33

2 SECRET INGREDIENTS FOR THE PERFECT CARROT SPONGE

Traditionally, the carrot sponge is made solely with vegetable oil, yielding a soft and tender yet excessively crumbly layer that defies slicing — it simply falls apart when you pick it up.

Being a perfectionist at heart, I couldn't accept this and embarked on a mission to create a sponge cake that retained its structure flawlessly.

And the journey of experimentation began.

I tested a 50:50 blend of butter and vegetable oil, resulting in a delicious but overly dense sponge reminiscent of a tea cake. While the issue of density was resolved, a new challenge emerged — how to seamlessly marry density with tenderness.

Indeed, how?

Initially, my instinct was to reduce the butter content in the recipe. However, after a moment of contemplation, an alternative idea surfaced — to partially substitute the oil with a small quantity of kefir. And there it was! The outcome: a tender yet structurally resilient sponge layer!

In essence,

🥛 THE FIRST SECRET INGREDIENT 🥛

is kefir.

🥕 AND THE SECOND SECRET INGREDIENT 🥕

is carrot juice.

🔸 similar to kefir, carrot juice contributes a substantial liquid content, serving to bind the sponge together.
🔸 + it functions as a potent natural colorant
🔸 + it imparts a deep, rich taste to the sponge

The result is a carrot sponge that strikes a perfect balance — moderately tender, slightly dense, and incredibly vibrant and delectable!

## Tip 034 — 4 Primary Ways in Which Fat Contributes to Leavening

**Source:** from B1-34

4 PRIMARY WAYS IN WHICH FAT CONTRIBUTES TO LEAVENING

1️⃣ All plastic fats contain tiny air bubbles, and some fats (such as butter and margarine) contain droplets of water.

The real leavening agents are air and water (steam), while fat serves an auxiliary function by helping trap and preserve air within the dough.

2️⃣ Loosening of puff pastry

Solid fat layers create cavities in the dough upon melting, subsequently filled by hot steam. As the steam expands due to heat, it pushes the layers of dough apart.

3️⃣ During the whipping process, additional air bubbles get into the plastic fats.

The air bubbles are surrounded and protected by numerous tiny, hard fat crystals that prevent them from bursting.

4️⃣ Emulsifiers have the ability to trap and retain large volumes of air, especially when using high-ratio shortenings.

For instance, during the whipping process of plastic shortening, air is trapped. With the help of an emulsifier, it is first retained in the fat and then in the dough in the form of very small, strong bubbles evenly distributed throughout the dough.

## Tip 036 — How to Make Puff Pastry for Pies Tender

**Source:** merged B1-36 + B2-02 (identical content; B1's fuller title heading kept)

HOW TO MAKE PUFF PASTRY FOR PIES TENDER

Concluding the analysis of the second fundamental function of fats in baking — providing flakiness — I will now share another secret for you to take note of.

And this secret will concern puff pastry for pies.

The perfect dough for the pie base should possess both tenderness and flakiness simultaneously. How can we achieve such a seemingly contradictory texture?

To achieve flakiness, incorporate relatively large pieces of fat. To guarantee the dough's tenderness, follow these tips:

👩🏻‍🍳 use weak, low gluten flour

👩🏻‍🍳 use a minimal amount of flour to dust the work surface during the kneading process

👩🏻‍🍳 if necessary, increase the amount of fat in the formula

👩🏻‍🍳 make sure to use a minimal amount of water

👩🏻‍🍳 do not overdo it with mixing the dough after adding water

👩🏻‍🍳 let the dough chill in the fridge for several hours or overnight to allow water to migrate freely in the dough

## Tip 037 — Determining the Doneness of Honey Cake Layers

**Source:** merged B1-37 + B2-03 (identical content in both)

DETERMINING THE DONENESS OF HONEY CAKE LAYERS

Delicate and seemingly innocuous, honey cake layers demand attention in the baking process, much like any other type of cakes.

When preparing honey cake layers, there are two potential, yet undesirable mistakes:

▪️ Underbaking them
▪️ Overbaking them

Determining the doneness of your cakes is simple — just observe their appearance! Let's explore cakes made from three types of honey dough as an illustration (for a refresher on honey dough types, refer to the post from April 23).

⚫️ CAKES FROM STIFF OR SOFT HONEY DOUGH

🔸 Properly baked

have a consistent golden or caramel color

▪️ for chocolate cakes, assess doneness based on the baking time of classic cakes, as their dark color makes visual cues challenging

🔸 Underbaked

appear flat, resembling pancakes, indicating insufficient protein and starch denaturation, causing the cakes to collapse

🔸 Overbaked

result in excessive dryness, with the cake edges prone to crumbling

▪️ after soaking, a honey cake with overbaked layers remains moist and tender, while one with underbaked layers tends to be excessively moist and prone to sogginess

⚫️ CAKES FROM LIQUID HONEY DOUGH (VERTICAL LAYER HONEY CAKES)

🔸 Properly baked

display softness, tenderness, and high pliability, making them easy to roll into a roll

🔸 Underbaked

reveal characteristic dark spots, indicating depressions formed due to incomplete protein denaturation

🔸 Overbaked

develop dry areas that crumble, making it challenging to roll such cakes into a roll

Always assess the doneness based on the first cake layer — allow the cake to cool completely, examine its appearance, and adjust the baking time or mode if necessary.

---

## Tip 038 — The Physics of Flaky Puff Pastry Formation During Baking

**Source:** merged B1-38 + B2-04 (identical content in both)

THE PHYSICS OF FLAKY PUFF PASTRY FORMATION DURING BAKING

Puff pastry consists of multiple, invisible to the eye, but intact layers of dough, separated from each other by equally thin and intact layers of pliable fat.

As the oven temperature rises, the layers of fat gradually melt. Simultaneously, water within the fat transforms into steam, expanding as the temperature ascends. This steam fills the voids left by the melting fat.

The melted fat acts as a barrier, delaying the escape of steam from the dough. Consequently, under the pressure of the expanding steam, the layers of dough puff up, creating the characteristic flakiness. Meanwhile, the lipids in the fat cause the dough to fry, resulting in a delectably golden-brown and crispy exterior.

Towards the end of the baking process, the dough stabilizes, yielding a final product with numerous delicate layers.

⚠️ The rising of baked goods occurs in the interstices between the layers of dough; however, the individual layers of dough themselves remain flat.

Hence, in areas where the layers of dough make contact, there is minimal flakiness and rising.

Unintentional occurrences of this issue may arise due to:

▪️ uneven rolling of fat between dough layers
▪️ tears in the dough
▪️ cuts in the dough
▪️ premature melting of fat before baking, causing the dough layers to fuse

For easier rolling of fat without damaging dough layers, it is crucial to use pliable fat of similar density to the dough.

If opting for butter, it should be slightly cooled (~16–18 °C) and, optionally, mixed with a small amount of flour.

To prevent tearing, the use of strong flour is recommended, with adequate resting time for the dough between lamination sessions.

Sometimes the dough is intentionally pierced, or small cuts are made in a random fashion to avoid excessive separation. Additionally, pressing along the edges of puff pastry can prevent it from complete separation into individual thin layers during baking.

## Tip 040 — Blue Spots Between Honey Cake Layers and Filling

**Source:** merged B1-40 + B2-06 (identical content in both)

BLUE SPOTS BETWEEN HONEY CAKE LAYERS AND FILLING

On multiple occasions, concerned students have messaged me images of honey cakes exhibiting blue or bluish spots, accompanied by the question: "Marusya, is it safe to eat this? What's going on?"

❓ Can this cake be consumed?
✅ Absolutely

❓ What causes the appearance of blue spots?
❌ Blue spots indicate incomplete chemical reaction of baking soda in the cake layers

To be more specific, these blue spots don't develop within the cake layers themselves but rather at the junctions where the cake and its filling meet.

If baking soda fails to fully react, either during batter preparation or cake baking, the acid in berry fillings and/or sour cream frostings (lactic acid) reacts with the incompletely reacted baking soda in the cakes. Consequently, localized blue spots emerge.

❓ How to eliminate the risk of blue spots

Our primary objective is to ensure that most of the baking soda undergoes the reaction during the batter preparation, and only a small percentage is left for a swift and final reaction in the oven — higher temperatures speed up the process.

But to be on the safe side, after adding baking soda to the heated honey mixture, we can incorporate lemon juice or citric acid to monitor the reaction.

✅ If foaming occurs, albeit weakly, it indicates that the baking soda has already reacted adequately -> remove the mixture from the heat and add flour.

❌ If the mixture foams vigorously, signaling an incomplete reaction between baking soda and the acid in the honey, take corrective measures -> add a small amount of lemon juice or further heat the mixture until the reaction weakens.

❗️Do not allow the mixture to overheat; otherwise, during the addition of flour to the batter, it may partially cook, resulting in a 'rubbery' texture. Similarly, when incorporating eggs into an overheated mixture, there is a risk of protein coagulation. To prevent this, use a water bath and/or actively stir with a whisk while introducing eggs.

## Tip 041 — A Formula for Simple Yet Stylish Cake Decoration

**Source:** merged B1-41 + B2-07 (identical content; B2's "Sart"→"Start" typo fix adopted for readability)

A FORMULA FOR SIMPLE YET STYLISH CAKE DECORATION

If the idea of slicing into your cake feels like committing a culinary crime, it signifies that what is in front of you is not just a dessert to satisfy sweet cravings, but rather a genuine masterpiece.

But how can you ensure that your cake captures the attention of even the most discerning aesthetes?

Today, I'm unveiling a formula for a simple yet sophisticated and striking cake decoration. It involves six key variables.

1️⃣ Ensure that decorative elements mirror the essence of the cake. If the cake features violet frosting and blueberry-lemon confit, opt for violet flowers, lemon slices, and blueberries. For a dessert showcasing sour cherries and chocolate, use nappage-coated sour cherries and handmade chocolates, etc.

2️⃣ Conceptualize an appealing shape; in my case, it's a wreath.

3️⃣ Start the formation of the wreath with the largest decorative elements, such as a lemon slice.

4️⃣ Arrange the largest decorative elements in a circular fashion, maintaining spacing between them and positioning them opposite each other.

5️⃣ Fill any remaining gaps after placing the larger elements with smaller ones, like blueberries and violet flowers.

6️⃣ The size and/or height of the decorative elements should correspond to the dimensions of your cake — the taller and wider the cake, the larger and/or taller the decorative elements should be.

📎 given that most of my cakes measure 14–16 cm in height, I arrange small decorative elements, such as blueberries and raspberries, in 2 or even 3 layers for added visual impact

📎 for convenience, you can initially sketch the placement of all decorative elements on paper before arranging them on the cake, using the drawing as a guide

## Tip 042 — Functions of Fats in Baking, Part 1: Impact on Softness and Tenderness

**Source:** merged B1-42 (stub only, truncated at "Have you ever thought about why we add butter...") + B2-08 (complete version kept)

FUNCTIONS OF FATS IN BAKING
part 1

Have you ever thought about why we add butter into cupcake batter? And why do we whip it?

One of the most important functions of fats in baking is to impart softness and tenderness to the baked goods. Today, I'll explain how this works.

Fats serve to coat and envelop the structure formers (gluten proteins, egg whites, and starch granules). They prevent their hydration and the development of structure. Essentially, fats act to shorten the gluten strands, thus preventing the formation of an extensive, interconnected gluten network.

In baking, this quality is often referred to as 'shortness', a term that emphasizes the desired tenderness in the final product.

Fat's impact on baked goods' softness and tenderness depends on:

▪️amount of fat

The higher the fat content, the softer and crumblier the product will be.

All-purpose shortening (confectionery fat) or 100% fat vegetable oils are much more effective at shortening gluten strands compared to, for example, 82% butter, which contains residual water.

▪️fat consistency

The softer and more liquid the fat, the better it softens the batter, resulting in crumblier and more tender baked goods.

Liquid fats, like vegetable oil, blend more readily with the batter, enveloping the flour particles. Soft whipped butter is more effective at softening than unwhipped butter.

▪️size of fat particles

The more thoroughly the fat is incorporated into the flour, the more tender and crumblier the product will be. This occurs as the fat particles become smaller and better cover the structure-forming particles.

▪️presence of emulsifiers

Like thorough dough mixing, emulsifiers assist in even fat distribution throughout baked goods and are highly effective at enveloping the structure-forming particles.

▪️leavening ability of fat or emulsifier

The greater the leavening ability of the fat, the more it contributes to softening. The rising of baked goods during baking stretches and thins the cell walls, weakening them.

Hence, vegetable oil, devoid of leavening properties, softens pie batter but yields denser cakes and tea cakes.

---

## Tip 043 — Functions of Fats in Baking, Part 2: Providing Flakiness (Puff Pastry Varieties)

**Source:** from B2-09

part 2

Exploring the intricacies of baking is a must for dedicated confectioners who value a thoughtful and precise approach in their culinary endeavors. Understanding the fundamentals of confectionery and the underlying physics of the baking process is essential to avoid unnecessary or incorrect actions in the kitchen.

Let's begin examining the second function of fats in baking.

PROVIDING FLAKINESS

🖊 Flakiness is the ability of baked goods to form numerous thin, flat, and often crispy layers.

🖊 Puff pastry is a culinary product made from numerous thin layers of dough separated by layers of fat, achieved through meticulous folding and rolling. Some puff pastry types have over 80 layers.

VARIETIES OF PUFF PASTRY BY PREPARATION METHOD

🔸 classic
🔸 chopped
🔸 easy-make

In classic puff pastry, fat is repetitively folded and rolled together with the dough (individual puff pastries, croissants, Danish pastries).

In chopped – solid pieces of fat are chopped alongside flour until they resemble crumbs (pies).

In easy-make puff pastry, solid fat, often in a frozen state, is grated coarsely and then swiftly mixed with flour (Napoleon cakes, individual puff pastries).

Irrespective of how fat is incorporated into the dough, whether in pieces or as a single rolled-out layer, the key to forming a quality, flaky puff pastry lies in the subsequent processes of rolling and lamination.

Furthermore, to ensure top-notch puff pastry, the plasticity of the fat is of utmost importance. It is crucial that the consistency of the fat matches that of the dough to prevent it from breaking through the dough layer or being squeezed out.

To prevent the fat from melting entirely during rolling and to maintain its necessary plasticity, it is a common practice to work with the dough at a temperature not exceeding 18 °C.

---

## Tip 044 — Functions of Fats in Baking, Part 3: The Main Rule for Achieving Perfect Puff Pastry

**Source:** from B2-10

part 3

The main rule for achieving perfect puff pastry is as follows:

☝️the later the fat melts in the oven, the better the flakiness☝️

In chopped pie dough and easy-make puff pastry, the pieces of fat must be well chilled and large in size to ensure that the layers of dough and fat do not mix together.

Hence, when working in a warm pastry shop, the fat should be thoroughly chilled before starting the puff pastry preparation (for easy-make puff pastry, consider freezing it) so that the fat does not completely melt and combine with the flour during the kneading process.

For the same reason, using a mixer is not recommended, as it quickly combines the flour and fat into a homogeneous mass, which diminishes the formation of flakiness during the baking process.

It is also essential to use ice-cold water when kneading puff pastry to prevent the butter in the dough from heating up and melting prematurely.

Similarly, it's imperative to thoroughly chill the dough before rolling it out. The colder the dough and the lower the room temperature, the flakier the final product will be.

Notice how the flaky texture of the dough cannot be matched by its tenderness, a result of the careful combination of fat and flour.

In short, the ability of fat to provide flakiness to baked goods depends on:

▪️ fat hardness: the harder the fat and the higher its melting point, the greater the flakiness

▪️ fat pieces size: larger fat pieces in the dough lead to better flakiness

## Tip 046 — How and Why to Drain Sour Cream

**Source:** from B2-12

#marusya_theory_tips

Dear friends, I've received numerous inquiries regarding drained sour cream and where to obtain it🤷‍♀️

Allow me to shed light on this for my beloved followers😘

❓What is drained sour cream

🖊 Drained sour cream is sour cream with artificially increased fat content, achieved by removing some of the moisture, specifically whey, during the draining process.

❓Why drain sour cream

To obtain a harmonious flavor and prolong the shelf life of honey cakes, I recommend using store-bought sour cream.

To get a reliably stable sour cream frosting that is both easy to work with and delightful to the palate, you should aim for sour cream with a fat content of approximately ~36–40%.

However, most store-bought sour creams typically have a maximum fat content of 30%. Consequently, to increase the fat content of sour cream, we must engage in the process of draining.

❓How to drain sour cream

▪️ To obtain drained sour cream, place a strainer over a bowl, ensuring there's adequate space at the base of the bowl to collect any excess liquid.
▪️ Next, place a double layer of cheesecloth within the strainer, pour 30% sour cream into it, and allow the whey to drain for 6–8 hours in the fridge. During this process, 30% sour cream should lose approximately 18% of its whey, thereby increasing its fat content to approximately ~36%.

📎 if the highest fat content available to you is 20–25%, place the sour cream in the fridge to drain for an entire night or a minimum of 10–12 hours. The resulting frosting might not be as stable as with 30% sour cream, but is still suitable for cake assembly.

And now, the question that has piqued the interest of many.

❓Which sour cream brand do I prefer

▪️ President, and only President 🤍❤️

## Tip 047 — All About Cake Fillings: Coulis, Confit, Compote

**Source:** from B2-13

I noticed your keen interest in the topic of fillings, so as promised, I'm going to provide you with an in-depth exploration of the 3 most popular fruit fillings: coulis, confit, and compote.

Distinguishing between these fillings might be tricky initially, but I'm here to clarify. Save or write it down for future reference.

🟡 COULIS

The term "coulis" comes from French, meaning "sauce" or "to rub". Now, here's an ⚠️IMPORTANT⚠️ point – coulis can serve a dual role. It can be an independent sauce poured over a finished dessert or function as a filling within a cake.

In the former case, coulis takes the form of a berry or fruit sauce made by blending or "rubbing" the fruits, sometimes thickened with pectin or starch. In the latter case, it is a jelly-like, dense, and stable filling, made from fruit or berry juice, sugar, and a thickening agent. No matter how it's used, coulis consistently imparts a rich flavor derived from the base fruit or berry.

🟡 CONFIT

The French term "confiture" is derived from "confire," meaning "cooking in sugar."

Confiture, or simply confit, is a filling made by simmering fruits or berries in their own juices along with sugar or sugar syrup. Typically, it features whole berries or fruit pieces, although puree can also be used. Its most distinctive characteristic, setting it apart from coulis, is its consistency – confit closely resembles jam, while coulis has a more jelly-like texture.

✒️ the process of boiling fruits or berries in their own juice with sugar or sugar syrup is also known as "candisation"

🟡 COMPOTE

Compote can be either a standalone dessert or a filling inside other desserts.

When enjoyed as a dessert, it consists of berries or fruit pieces simmered in a sugar syrup, sometimes infused with spices. As a filling, it involves cooking berries or fruit pieces with sugar and adding one of thickeners (starch, gelatin, pectin, or agar-agar). The resulting texture is notably dense, akin to jelly or marmalade.
