## Tip 048 — Cake Fillings, Part 3: Caramel — Dry, Fruit, Creamy and Salty

**Source:** from B2-14

Caramel desserts have always been popular in the world of sweets. Naturally, everyone fine-tunes their caramel intake based on their DNA-encoded cravings for sweetness, but no one can resist the allure of its deep, enveloping aroma!

This allure is particularly pronounced during the fall season when our taste buds gravitate towards warming flavors. So, on this splendid September day, let's delve into the world of caramel. Let's begin, as usual, with a definition.

🖊Caramel (Spanish "caramelo", from the late Latin "cannamella," meaning "sugar cane") is a confectionery creation or an ingredient used in confections, formed by heating sugar or boiling a sugar solution with glucose or glucose-fructose syrup.

This caramel is often referred to as 🟠DRY.

👩🏻‍🍳for those who may have puzzled over the phrase "wait until the sugar has completely melted to form DRY caramel" in my recipes, it's essential to clarify that 'dry' implies the absence of additives, consisting solely of sugar or a combination of sugar and syrup.

When you introduce hot cream and room-temperature butter into dry caramel, you transform it into 🟠CREAMY CARAMEL.

🖊room temperature butter is typically around 14–16 °C.

If you decide to add a pinch of salt to the mixture after incorporating the cream, 🟠SALTED CARAMEL emerges.

But what about incorporating fruit, you may wonder?

🟠FRUIT OR BERRY CARAMEL is the delightful result of replacing some or all of the cream with fruit or berry puree.

You can also experiment with additional ingredients such as chocolate or nut butter. In general, I've covered the main types of caramel, leaving the rest to your creative whims. Sometimes, gelatin is also introduced to caramel as a stabilizing agent.

That essentially sums up the core of caramel. Well, almost. I almost forgot to mention that this post also goes by another name:

## Tip 049 — Cake Fillings, Part 4: Curd

**Source:** from B2-15

Coulis, compote, caramel, confit... What other fillings can we explore for cakes? Naturally, curd comes to mind.

▪️DEFINITION

Curd is a filling that closely resembles custard and can also stand alone as a confectionery creation. It is made by cooking fruit or berry puree or juice with eggs and butter.

▪️MAIN INGREDIENTS

🔸egg yolks
🔸sugar
🔸fruit base
🔸starch
🔸butter

▪️COOKING PROCESS

Begin by beating sugar with egg yolks, then incorporate juice or fruit puree and starch. Boil the mixture until it thickens, then remove it from the heat and gently stir in the butter.

▪️CURD VS CUSTARD

In fact, curd is essentially custard with a unique twist — it is made using fruit or berry puree instead of milk or cream.

▪️HOW TO MAKE YOUR CURD SPECIAL

🔸add the zest of yuzu, orange, lime, lemon...
🔸pour in a splash of liqueur like Limoncello, Cointreau, Curaçao, or Crème de Pêche...
🔸incorporate some white, dark, or milk chocolate
🔸infuse it with a sprig of thyme, rosemary, or lemongrass
🔸enhance the flavor with real vanilla, cinnamon, turmeric, or other spices

▪️IMPORTANT POINTS REGARDING CURDS

🔸to simplify the cooking process, start by dissolving the starch in a few tablespoons of juice beforehand
🔸for the best results when making curds, opt for farm-fresh egg yolks

## Tip 050 — Cake Fillings, Part 5: Crémeux

**Source:** from B2-16

It's been a while since we delved into the world of cake fillings. Inspired by yesterday's photos of chocolate desserts, I've decided to continue our exploration, this time focusing on crémeux.

Shall we begin?

Crémeux is a timeless French dessert which consists of five key ingredients: milk, sugar, eggs, cream, and chocolate. Beyond its role as a standalone dessert, it shines as a filling for cakes, pies, or individual cakes.

Traditionally, crème anglaise serves as a base for crémeux, as you may recall from our earlier #marusya_about_crèmeanglaise series. Crème anglaise includes:

▪️milk / milk and cream / milk and fruit puree
▪️egg yolks
▪️sugar

With the addition of cream and chocolate, crème anglaise transforms into a luxurious cake filling.

However, confectionery artistry allows room for creativity in the composition of crémeux.

In fact, crème anglaise as the base for crémeux is not set in stone. Let's consider its function in crémeux🤔

As is the case with any custard, during heat treatment, all the water is bound with the help of thickeners. In a classic crème anglaise, without the addition of flour or starch, the thickener is lecithin found in egg yolks.

The primary objective in making crémeux is to achieve a stable filling. However, crème anglaise isn't the sole method for providing thickness and stability. Personally, I don't use crème anglaise in my crémeux for cakes.

🟤 Firstly, chocolate itself acts as a natural thickener.

🟤 Secondly, other stabilizers and thickeners can be used, such as gelatin or pectin. My preference leans toward gelatin when making crémeux.

🟤 Thirdly, and most excitingly, one can give free rein to their imagination by

▪️adding nut or flower pastes into the crémeux
▪️sprinkling the crémeux with additions like nut pieces
▪️infusing the cream / milk with lavender or lemongrass...

## Tip 051 — Cake Fillings, Part 6: Ganache and Mousse

**Source:** from B2-17

#marusya_about_fillings

Coulis, confit, compote, caramel, curd, crémeux – what other options exist for cake fillings? Let's explore some choices that don't all start with the letter "c," shall we? 😂

Among these options, we have:

🔴 GANACHE

🖊 Ganache, borrowed from the French, is a delectable mixture made from chocolate (whether it's white, milk, or dark) and a liquid component. Additionally, it can incorporate butter, sugar, and an array of aromatic enhancements. The liquid component in ganache can be sourced from:

▪️milk
▪️heavy cream
▪️fruit or berry juice
▪️fruit or berry puree

Fundamentally, ganache is an emulsion of water and fat. To obtain a uniform texture and prevent separation, the proportion of fat must align with that of water.

So, when working with ganache, it's important to meticulously maintain the balance between liquid ingredients and chocolate while using chocolate with the exact percentage of cocoa butter indicated in the recipe!

For comprehensive insights into ganache, including the precise ratios of liquids, chocolate, butter, and sugar, along with numerous finer details, you can delve deeper using the hashtag #marusya_about_ganache.

Another option for cake fillings is:

🔴 MOUSSE

🖊 Mousse (derived from the French term for "foam") is a delightful dessert or cake filling composed of:

▪️ a flavor base (be it fruit or berry juice, puree, grape wine, chocolate, coffee, cocoa, etc.)
▪️ and auxiliary components that facilitate frothing (like heavy cream and egg whites, etc.) and maintain the foamy consistency of the mousse (like gelatin or agar-agar).

## Tip 052 — Forming the Perfect Cheesecake Crust: Bottom & Shape (Using a Bent Spoon)

**Source:** from B2-18

Explore the beginning here #marusya_cheesecake_crust

If you recall my previous instructions on shaping the sides of the cheesecake crust, it's now time to learn how to properly create the bottom and give the crust its ideal shape using a bent tablespoon.

Let's get started!🚀

⭕️ add the remaining shortbread crumbs to those that became detached during the shaping of the sides
⭕️ bend a tablespoon at an obtuse angle that is comfortable for your work
📍a soft metal tablespoon is the best choice for forming a crust, as it easily bends at any angle

⭕️ using the bent tablespoon, evenly distribute the crumbs over the entire bottom and compact them tightly
⭕️ when compacting the crumbs along the edges of the bottom, gently press the tip of the spoon into the side, creating a small depression
⭕️ use the back of a spoon to compact the lower part of the side, running it around the cake ring. At this point, the tip of the spoon should slightly press the crust, forming a beautiful 90° angle
⭕️ compact any crumbs that have crumbled as a result of pressing the bottom
📍 if you notice that the crust's side is thicker at the base, you can carefully scrape off the excess with a spoon

⭕️ compact the central part of the crust's side with the middle part of the back of a spoon, running it along the cake ring
📍at the same time, the sides of the crust will rise slightly; this is normal

⭕️ compact the top part of the crust's side with four fingers, while not forgetting to constantly press the top with the thumb of the other hand

The perfect cheesecake crust is now finished!

And here are insider tips exclusively for you:

💎 cheesecake crust thickness: 0.7–0.8 mm
💎 approximately 57% of shortbread crumbs are used for the sides, while 43% are used for the bottom (I use 240 g and 180 g)

**[ANOMALY]** This tip references "previous instructions on shaping the sides" as already covered, but the sides-shaping tip (Tip 054 / B2-21 below) appears LATER in the source than this bottom-shaping tip — an out-of-order sequencing issue in the original document (see Flagged Anomalies section).

## Tip 054 — How to Know When the Custard Is Ready

**Source:** from B2-20

#marusya_theory_tips

The custard is ready once it has boiled and reached 82°C. Is it so? Yes. Can we rely on this alone? Not at all.

Why?

▪️Firstly, the custard's boiling point can vary between 82 to 85 °C based on the recipe you follow

▪️Secondly, measuring the temperature of boiling custard is challenging for the following reasons:

a) to ensure even cooking, it must be stirred continuously, making it highly inconvenient to simultaneously measure the temperature

b) the presence of air bubbles during boiling can interfere with accurate thermometer readings

▪️Thirdly, it's easy to miss the initial boiling point of custard

When custard is cooked correctly (over very low heat with constant stirring), you may not notice it reaching the boiling point, as bubbles typically form after some time.

⁉️ So, how do you determine when the custard is ready?

I prioritize the thickening process. The custard should reach a complete and uniform thickness with no liquid or thicker parts remaining – it should thicken evenly throughout.

After achieving this complete thickness, I continue to stir constantly and let it boil for an additional one to two minutes, adjusting the duration based on the quantity of custard, to ensure there are no uncooked areas remaining.

## Tip 055 — Forming the Perfect Cheesecake Crust: Sides (Using Fingers)

**Source:** from B2-21

After more than six months of experimentation and practice, I've developed my unique method for forming the ideal cheesecake crust.

"Why did you have to reinvent the wheel?" you ask. Well, because few people, besides me, were concerned about achieving a perfectly flat side and a beautiful right angle between the bottom and the side of the crust.

The way a pastry looks affects our taste perception. Thus, a flawless crust is a crucial element of the ultimate cheesecake. So how can it be made?

After six months of experiments, I've learned to form the perfect crust in just 5 minutes. Want me to teach you?

Let's get started.

⭕️ take a small handful of shortcrust crumbs in your palm
⭕️ using your four fingers, firmly press the shortcrust crumbs against the walls of a greased cake ring, ensuring even distribution over the entire height
📍press the shortcrust crumbs multiple times, not just once; this involves continuous, repetitive movements

⭕️ simultaneously, run your thumb from the other hand around the top to create a level surface
⭕️ remove any crumbs that become detached during shaping and place them closer to the center of the crust's bottom
⭕️ use the tips of your four fingers to press the crumbs at the very base of the wall. Press them along the entire height of the ring until the layer is uniformly thick both above and below. Maintain consistent pressure on the top with your thumb from the other hand
⭕️ grab the next handful of crumbs and repeat all previous steps
📍when forming the side, you should develop the ability to gauge its thickness with your fingers – apply a bit more pressure to the crumbs where the side is thicker, distributing them evenly around the ring's circumference

In this manner, shape the sides around the entire circumference of the ring.

## Tip 056 — When the Cheesecake Is Ready: Egg White Coagulation, Part 1 (Intro)

**Source:** from B2-22

#marusya_about_coagulation

Through numerous experiments and master classes, I stumbled upon a fascinating observation – cheesecakes with different fillings require varying baking times.

When baking 10 cheesecakes simultaneously in a convection oven and removing them all at once, there's always an overcooked filling, another that's perfectly done, and there are a few cheesecakes with runny centers that could have spent more time in the oven.

Now, one might wonder, why does this occur? After all, the cheesecakes are nearly the same in size, and the amount of cream cheese mixture in each is also similar. So, shouldn't the doneness solely depend on the product's size, baking time, and temperature? Is there something else at play here?

I pondered over this question for a long time until I delved deep into the study of egg, one of the primary ingredients in confectionery.

Intrigued? Let's explore it together.

Cheesecake can be seen as an adaptation of the basic custard recipe, incorporating eggs as both a thickener and gelling agent.

The thickening process takes place as the temperature of the cream cheese mixture rises and the egg whites coagulate.

As the eggs are subjected to heat, the protein structure starts to denature and unfold. These unfolded protein molecules then interact and connect with one another, creating a strong yet highly flexible network.

This network effectively binds water and other liquids within the cheesecake mixture, ultimately signaling that the product is properly cooked.

Now, let's examine the primary factors that influence the rate of egg protein coagulation, along with the approximate temperatures at which this process takes place.

Would you like to read a continuation? Let me know in the comments.

---

## Tip 057 — Egg White Coagulation, Part 2: Quantity of Eggs, Cooking Temperature, Part of Egg Used

**Source:** from B2-23

Today we continue the article on egg white coagulation.

Discover the beginning at #marusya_about_coagulation

The rate of protein coagulation, and consequently, the cooking time of cheesecake, creme brulee, or custard, depends on:

▪️quantity or ratio of eggs

Normally, egg proteins coagulate at 70 °C; however, if the eggs are mixed with milk, water, or other ingredients, the coagulation temperature rises.

As an illustration, the proteins in custard coagulate at 82–85 °C. The addition of milk, cream, or sugar makes it more challenging for them to collide and form bonds.

So, the greater the amount of eggs in the mixture, the quicker it will cook.

▪️cooking temperatures

Egg coagulation is not an instantaneous process; it requires some time to occur. The higher the temperature, the faster the mixture cooks, and therefore, the proteins fully coagulate in less time.

Nevertheless, when eggs coagulate too quickly, the proteins may not have sufficient time to unfold, leading to an issue where the mixture might not fully thicken.

For instance, if custard is cooked over high heat, not only does it risk curdling and burning, but there's also a likelihood that it won't reach the desired thickness.

For enhanced thickening, cook it on low heat while continuously stirring. This is why water baths are so good for making custard, pudding, and cheesecakes.

Water baths maintain temperatures below the boiling point (at 82–88 °C), even when the oven temperature is set at 165 °C or higher. With a water bath, the coagulation process slows down, and the baking process becomes more uniform, preventing the outer layer of the cheesecake from becoming overly tight, rubbery, or curdled while ensuring proper baking on the inside.

▪️part of the egg used

Egg yolks coagulate at higher temperatures (65–70 °C) compared to egg whites (60–65 °C), resulting in a lower risk of protein hypercoagulation (folding) when using only yolks.

It's because the proteins in the yolks are lipoproteins bound to fats and emulsifiers, which complicates the protein binding process.

---

## Tip 058 — Egg White Coagulation, Part 3: Amount of Sugar, Amount of Lipids, Presence of Acid

**Source:** from B2-24

#marusya_about_coagulation

In the previous parts, we've already discussed that the rate of protein coagulation depends on the quantity or ratio of eggs, the cooking temperatures, and the part of the egg used.

However, these are not the only factors.

The rate of coagulation, and consequently the cooking time of the cheesecake, will also be influenced by:

▪️ amount of sugar

Apart from its role in diluting protein molecules, sugar also slows down the coagulation of the egg whites, preventing the proteins from unfolding.

This means that sugar aids in preventing the curdling of egg whites. Hence, the egg filling in quiche, which lacks added sugar, tends to curdle after baking and release liquid.

Consequently, it's not surprising that sugar is considered a tenderizing agent in baking. By slowing down coagulation, sugar also retards the formation of the egg structure, as well as the networks of gluten and starch.

When a sufficient amount of sugar is present, it can stop coagulation entirely, potentially leading to the product appearing undercooked despite prolonged cooking.

▪️amounts of lipids (fats)

Similar to sugar, lipids (fats, oils, and emulsifiers) slow down the coagulation process of proteins through direct interaction with them.

This explains why custards made with cream and yolks possess a silkier, softer, and creamier texture compared to those prepared with milk and whole eggs.

▪️presence and amount of acid

Acids accelerate coagulation by reducing the temperature required for this process.

📎 Acids in cheesecake or custard can stem from sources such as lemon juice, various fruits, raisins, or fermented dairy products.

This explains why fruit and berry cheesecakes bake more quickly than their counterparts.

There are 3 additional factors that influence the rate of protein coagulation. Are you familiar with them?

---

## Tip 059 — Egg White Coagulation, Part 4: Starch, Salt, Proteolytic Enzymes

**Source:** from B2-25

part 4, the last one

#marusya_about_coagulation

So, here are the last three 🤫secret factors🤫 that affect the cooking time of cheesecake:

▪️presence and quantity of starch

Starch increases the temperature of protein coagulation, interfering with the process.

As an example, let's compare the process of making custard sauce (without starch) and custard (with starch or flour).

The custard must be brought to boiling temperature (~85 °C) and boiled for 2 minutes or more. The custard sauce will curdle before it reaches boiling point.

Always make sure that custard containing starch is cooked properly. If it is not cooked long enough, not only will the starch not jellify (gelatinize) properly, but the small amount of amylase that is present in egg yolks may not be inactivated.

🖊Amylase is an enzyme that hydrolyzes starch to produce simple sugars. Unless completely deactivated, amylase will easily liquefy the custard overnight.

▪️amount of salt

Salt in hard water and dairy products, or adding a small amount of table salt, speeds up and enhances the coagulation of proteins in the egg.

Milk proteins can also interact with egg proteins to enhance gel formation. If you make the custard with water instead of milk or cream, it will turn out very liquid, barely thickened.

▪️presence of proteolytic enzymes

Enzymes break down egg proteins in the same way they break down the proteins in gelatin.

If you add raw pineapple with active protease to custard or cheesecake, it will not thicken. If you first boil the pineapple, thereby inactivating the protease, the intact egg proteins in the custard will coagulate and it will thicken.

## Tip 060 — The Relevance of Fat Content in Dairy Products for Frosting

**Source:** from B2-26

If you are following my page or if it has appeared as a suggestion on your feed, chances are you've prepared cake frosting at least once in your life.

Whether you used my recipe or that of another professional chef, you likely observed that every dairy product — like cream cheese, heavy cream, or sour cream — comes with its designated fat content.

This isn't just an arbitrary detail. The fat content of dairy products used for making frosting, which serves as an interlayer, plays a pivotal role. It significantly influences:

🥛 frosting stability — a higher fat content leads to greater stability in the frosting, making it easier to work with. Such frosting is particularly suitable for assembling tall and multi-tiered cakes

🥛permissible frosting quantity — with increased fat content, more frosting can be applied between cake layers

🥛 cake texture — incorporating high-fat dairy products results in well-soaked yet not overly wet cakes, achieving a balance of moderate tenderness and moisture

🥛 palatability — higher fat content yields a richer taste in the final product, enhancing its overall flavor profile

🥛 expiration dates — increased fat content extends the shelf life of the final product

In general, as you can see, the frosting's fat content correlates with expanded creative possibilities and heightened pleasure for our taste buds!;)

You might have come across this information from me before, but in case you haven't, I'll reiterate:

🖋 FAT MOLECULES ARE CARRIERS OF TASTE TO OUR TASTE RECEPTORS

## Tip 061 — Cultured Butter in Napoleon Cake Layers

**Source:** from B2-27

#marusya_theory_tips

Lately, I've noticed a growing number of inquiries regarding cultured butter. Students are curious about its nature and its role in making the layers of a Napoleon cake.

So, let me tell you.
Be all ears and save the post to bookmarks.

The two most common butter types are sweet cream and cultured butter. So, what is the difference between them?

🖊Sweet cream butter is made from pasteurized cream. It has a gentle milky flavor and aroma.

🖊Cultured butter, on the other hand, is made from pasteurized fermented cream. It involves the use of pure cultures of lactic acid bacteria during fermentation. This variety of butter has a tangier, more complex flavor and aroma.

❓How to identify the type of butter

In cases where the packaging doesn't specify the type of butter you have, you can distinguish between them by examining their composition. Cultured butter can be readily identified by the presence of lactic acid bacteria, whereas sweet cream butter consists solely of cream.

And now, let's move on to the most interesting aspect. Why is cultured butter preferred for making flaky puff pastry for Napoleon cake? Is it due to its taste? Not entirely. We should focus on a seemingly minor yet important distinction in physicochemical properties.

And that is the

❗️MELTING POINT❗️

Sweet cream butter – 32-33 °C
Cultured butter – 34-35 °C

When making layers for the Napoleon cake, our aim is to maintain the butter's low temperature for as long as possible, preventing it from melting due to ambient conditions and the warmth of our hands while grating it during dough kneading. This approach is crucial for achieving the ultra-thin and flaky puff pastry.

Here, a mere difference of a few degrees plays a significant role, particularly when working in a warm cooking environment.

📎 before grating cultured butter, I place it in the freezer overnight, and while grating, I consistently coat it with flour

📎 while it's possible to make puff pastry using sweet cream butter, the resulting pastry will be less flaky

## Tip 062 — Interchangeability of Varieties of Flour, Part 2: Seed Flours

**Source:** from B2-28

part 2

#marusya_60shades_offlour

Today we'll analyze the seed flour.

🌿 FLAX FLOUR

Absorbency – High
Gluten-free – Yes
Interchangeability —>

You can substitute 10–25% of the total weight of all-purpose flour.

❇️ Since flax flour absorbs water significantly, you'll need to increase the quantity of liquid ingredients in your recipe. Flax flour boasts a distinct and bold nutty flavor profile, making it advisable to start with 10%. From there, you can fine-tune the taste according to your preference.

🌿 SESAME FLOUR

Absorbency – High
Gluten-free – Yes
Interchangeability —>

Similar to flax flour.

❇️ Because sesame flour contains a substantial amount of fat, its texture is notably denser compared to that of all-purpose flour. Excessive use of sesame flour can result in an overly "heavy" product. Begin with a modest quantity, around 10%, and make adjustments as needed.

🌿 SUNFLOWER FLOUR

Absorbency – Medium
Gluten-free – Yes
Interchangeability —>

Replace no more than 25% of the total weight of all-purpose flour.

❇️ This flour can fully replace all-purpose flour (100%) for gluten-free baking, such as bread, cookies, or muffins. In this case, one should use additional binding agents like eggs or xanthan gum to maintain the structure of the pastry.

🌿 AMARANTH FLOUR

Absorbency – Above medium
Gluten-free – Yes
Interchangeability —>

Replace 25% of the total weight of all-purpose flour + take into account the absorbency and add a little more liquid.

🌿 QUINOA FLOUR

Absorbency – High
Gluten-free – Yes
Interchangeability —>

Replace from 25 to 50% of the total weight of all-purpose flour.

❇️ Quinoa flour possesses higher absorbency than all-purpose flour, potentially requiring additional liquid.

🌿 HEMP FLOUR

Absorbency – High
Gluten-free – Yes
Interchangeability —>

Replace up to 25% of the total weight of all-purpose flour.

❇️ Hemp flour is made by grinding hemp plant seeds and should not be confused with CBD (cannabidiol), which is extracted from the plant's flowers, leaves, and stems.

## Tip 064 — The Perfect Cheesecake: How to Choose a Cake Ring

**Source:** from B2-30

As perfectionists, we understand the importance of every detail in making a flawless cheesecake, even seemingly insignificant factors like the cake ring.

Indeed, the selection of the ring holds a crucial role in achieving the ultimate perfection in baking a cheesecake. So, what key aspects demand your attention when opting for the ideal cake ring?

DIAMETER TO HEIGHT RATIO

If you've made a cheesecake at least once according to my recipe, then you know that I use a ring d=18 cm, h=6 cm. This specific size is not a coincidence; it has been carefully chosen to create a cheesecake that appears visually indulgent, evoking sincere delight even from the most discerning aesthetes.

An almost identical cheesecake prepared in a ring d=20 cm, h=6 cm, won't produce the same effect, as scrutinized by genuine perfectionists🧐

THICKNESS

min = 1 mm

Working with a thinner ring can be less convenient since obtaining the perfect cheesecake crust requires firm pressure of the crumble against the ring's walls. However, a thin ring tends to bend slightly, compromising the integrity of the crust, and you have to start the pressing process anew to achieve a visually appealing outcome.

Even the slightest difference of tenths of a millimeter can impact the thickness, so be mindful that when applying gentle pressure to the ring from both sides with your hands, it shouldn't be compressed.

JOINT (WELD)

It should be well polished. Even if the rings are entirely identical and from the same brand, their quality can vary: one may have a flawless joint, while the other might exhibit a noticeable gap. This gap can leave an unattractive streak on the cheesecake, which is particularly distressing for us perfectionists😁

I recommend selecting cake rings from pastry shops rather than ordering them online. Alternatively, opt for brands with a proven track record of exceptional performance to ensure top-notch quality.

## Tip 065 — Why Should Cream Be Cooled Before Whipping?

**Source:** from B2-31

#marusya_theory_tips

Whipped cream is an essential ingredient in most desserts. Cream with a fat content of at least 33% is considered ideal for whipping. Have you ever wondered why?🤔

The fats in the cream crystallize around the air bubbles that form during whipping. If the cream is low-fat, there will be too few crystals to hold air bubbles, and the cream won't whip.

However, even when whipping heavy cream, instead of a stable lush foam and beautiful stable peaks, there is a risk of getting either an unstable fluid or split mass.

The fact is that one of the most important factors affecting the quality of whipping cream is temperature: the cream must be cooled before whipping. In short, the crystallization of fats occurs only in a cold environment – frosting made of insufficiently cold cream will deflate.

To understand why this happens, you need to delve into the physics and chemistry of the process.

📎 Cream is stabilized by milk fat, which is presented in the form of fat grains – the so-called globules, surrounded by a protective membrane. In the process of whipping cold cream, the whisk begins to destroy the integrity of the membranes that surround the globule, and partially exposes the fat component. Globules with their bare parts begin to be attracted either to each other or to air, thus forming a fat network inside which air bubbles are sealed.

🖇This property of the globules allows the cream to expand and turn into a light, airy mass. At higher temperatures, fat grains melt, become liquid, and repel air, so warm cream cannot whip properly, and it takes longer to reach maximum volume.

❌ But, the longer the cream is whipped, the more the protective membrane is destroyed. As a result, bare fat particles begin to strive only towards themselves – they displace air, clump into a dense mass, repel water, which results in the cream with a grainy texture.

---

## Tip 066 — Chocolate Fat Bloom: How to Store Chocolate, Part 2

**Source:** from B2-32

or

EVERYTHING YOU NEED TO KNOW ON HOW TO STORE CHOCOLATE
Part 2

#marusya_chocolate_storage

We began Part 1 of this post series by stating that chocolate is ideally stored at temperatures between 15-21°C to prevent fat bloom. Now, let's delve into what exactly 'fat bloom' is and what causes it.

🖊 Fat bloom is when the unstable solid forms of cocoa butter transform into a stable yet uneven crystal structure on the surface of chocolate. Put simply, cocoa butter crystals separate from the other components and migrate to the chocolate's surface, solidifying and creating greyish or whitish streaks.

This blooming happens for a number of reasons:

🔺the chocolate wasn't tempered or was tempered incorrectly

⤴️read more about the secrets of chocolate tempering using the hashtag #marusya_about_chocolate

🔺 the recommended temperature range for storing chocolate (15-21 °C) wasn't followed
🔺during storage or transportation, the chocolate was exposed to significant temperature fluctuations
🔺cocoa butter substitutes, such as coconut oil, palm oil, or other fats, were added to the chocolate

📍 for chocolate products
🔺 when molding (pouring chocolate into molds), the molds were either too cold or too hot
🔺 the molding process took too long and the chocolate's working temperature dropped below the allowable range
🔺 working with chocolate under conditions involving heat, such as using a surface that is either too cold or too hot for packaging, can result in localized blooming
🔺 the chocolate was placed in a storage area that was too cold before it had completely solidified (usually takes 24-48 hours for complete solidification)

📎 photo 4 in the carousel shows an example of chocolate fat bloom

Generally, chocolate affected by fat bloom remains edible, but it loses its appealing gloss and crispness, and quickly melts upon touch.

**[ANOMALY]** Title says "Chocolate Sugar Bloom" but the content is entirely about FAT bloom (sugar bloom is the subject of Part 3, the next tip) — an inconsistency in the original document's heading (see Flagged Anomalies section).

The second fundamental principle is to maintain the humidity level below 70% to avoid sugar bloom.

## Tip 068 — How Moisture Comes in Contact With Chocolate

**Source:** from B2-34

part 1

#marusya_chocolate_storage

There are a lot of controversies and rumors regarding the best way to store chocolate. This means that this topic is interesting and important. Let's sort things out!

Basically, the key to long-term chocolate storage comes down to just two things: temperature and humidity. Chocolate is best stored at cool ambient temperatures (15-21°C) to prevent fat bloom or melting, and in a dry place (70% humidity or below) to keep it from interacting with water and developing sugar bloom or seizing.

But let's dig deeper and figure out all the subtleties and nuances, as we're used to make things clear, and hope you've already managed to fall in love with that too🤎

So, here's the first question that we will consider:

🟤 HOW LONG DOES CHOCOLATE LAST?

Usually, chocolate has an expiration date. But it only serves as a general guideline set by manufacturers as a best before date, until which they can guarantee the best taste and texture of chocolate.

In fact, chocolate can be stored much longer than the use-by date indicated on the package:

🍫 Dark chocolate – can maintain its quality 2 years from the manufacturing date in proper ambient storage conditions, and up to 4 years refrigerated with some degradation.
🍫 White chocolate – can maintain its quality up to 1 year from its manufacturing date in proper ambient storage conditions, and up to 2 years refrigerated with some degradation.
🍫 Milk chocolate – the same as with white chocolate.

🟤 Firstly, chocolate has the ability to absorb moisture!

Dark chocolate starts absorbing moisture when the humidity level exceeds 82%, whereas milk chocolate is susceptible to moisture absorption above 78%. Thus, to preserve the delightful appearance of chocolate, it is advisable to maintain an environmental humidity level of 70% or below.

🟤 Secondly, condensation may develop on the chocolate.

This can occur due to two factors:

1. Low storage temperature, such as when chocolate is stored in the fridge.

2. Sudden temperature fluctuations exceeding 10°C, such as when chocolate is transitioned from a cold place to a warmer one.

The reasons for moisture coming in contact with chocolate can differ, but the outcome remains consistent: the sugar within the chocolate dissolves in the water and migrates to the surface. As the moisture evaporates, tiny white crystals become visible, ultimately forming the distinctive "dusty" surface.

So let's summarize. In order to prevent sugar bloom in chocolate, we should:

▪️ store chocolate in a place with a consistently stable temperature
▪️ avoid rapid temperature fluctuations of more than 10 °C within a short span of time
▪️ prevent any liquid from coming into contact with the chocolate

## Tip 070 — 60 Shades of Flour, Part 2: Top 5 Types Detailed

**Source:** from B2-36

part 2

#marusya_60shades_offlour

As promised, today we'll analyze 5 most popular types of flour according to your answers:

1. Rice flour
2. Coconut flour
3. Buckwheat flour
4. Oat flour
5. Teff flour

RICE FLOUR

Obtained by grinding white rice into a fine powder. Unlike the all-purpose flour, it doesn't contain protein (gluten). Perfect for baking gluten-free cakes, biscuits and bread, and for making noodles. Often used as a thickener for sauces.

COCONUT FLOUR

Made by grinding sun-dried / oven-dried / or dehydrated coconut pulp into a powder. It's a gluten-free flour with a pleasant, slightly sweet flavor. Most often used for sweet pastries, cakes.

Contains 4 times more fiber than the all-purpose flour —> absorbs more moisture —> requires more liquid ingredients —> more moist and dense baked goods.

BUCKWHEAT FLOUR

Made by grinding buckwheat seeds. Buckwheat is a pseudocereal that has nothing to do with wheat; it's actually a fruit seed related to sorrel and rhubarb. Buckwheat flour is gluten-free. In recipes, it's most often combined with regular flour and other types of gluten-free flours. Best used in yeast-free pastries.

OAT FLOUR

A gluten-free flour made by grinding whole oat groats (hulled grains) into a fine powder. It has a slightly denser texture than regular flour. Contains 3 times more fiber than the all-purpose flour. Oat flour makes delicious banana bread, muffins, and cookies.

TEFF FLOUR

One of the most unusual types of flour, obtained from ancient grain native to Ethiopia, the Eragrostis teff. Because the grains of this plant are very small, teff flour contains more bran and germ than other whole grains, making it a good source of amino acids, iron, fiber, and calcium. It has a unique taste with pleasant nutty notes. The aroma of waffles or crepes made with teff flour will make any breakfast unforgettable!

**[NOTE]** This tip's content (buckwheat/oat/teff entries) is also the TAIL portion independently captured by Batch 3 as B3-01 ("Flour Glossary: Buckwheat/Oat/Teff Flour"), which begins mid-sentence with the same "Contains 4 times more fiber..." line. B2's version is the complete, properly-headed tip (includes rice and coconut flour too, plus the "part 2" label and intro); B3-01 is redundant with the second half of this tip and is not counted separately.

## Tip 071 — 60 Shades of Flour, Part 3: Substitution Table Principles

**Source:** merged B2-37 + B3-02 (identical content in both batches)

part 3

#marusya_60shades_offlour

In the previous parts, we introduced you to a wide range of varieties of flour, classifying it according to the raw materials (wheat, grain, bean, nut, vegetable and others), and analyzed in detail the varieties that were of most interest to you.

It doesn't make much sense to write definitions for all 60 varieties (this information is publicly available on the Internet), but sharing a foolproof table, which shows whether each flour can be substituted for all-purpose flour (and vice versa) might come in handy.

Before proceeding to the table itself, let's find out what are the general principles that lay the basis for this table⬇️

◼️ Any recipe is based on the interaction of ingredients, therefore, using the table, be sure to take into account the chemical processes occurring in each specific recipe.

◼️ Gluten-free flours work differently than the all-purpose flour, meaning baked goods don't rise as much as they do with gluten-containing flour. Therefore, even if the table says "100% interchangeable", it is only true for recipes in which the dough is not supposed to proof.

◼️ The table also shows the absorption ability of flour (high / medium / low). Take this information into account to adjust the amount of liquid in the recipe in case you swap the type of flour

ℹ️ The 'low', 'medium', and 'high' value is based on all-purpose flour being medium absorbency and comparing it to that.

The liquid absorbency estimate is based on an amalgamation of the water absorption capacity of the flour, oil absorption capacity, swelling capacity and hydrophobia of some flours (lack of water absorption capacity). The absorbency of flour can also depend on:

◽️protein level
◽️fiber level
◽️fat content
◽️presence and level of starch
◽️grinding fractions
◽️flour brand (compositions and processing methods may vary)
◽️time of exposure to moisture
◽️exposure to high / low temperatures

## Tip 072 — Hydrocolloids in Confectionery, Part 3: Gellan Gum

**Source:** merged B2-38 + B3-03 (identical content in both batches; B3's version has the full title header "HYDROCOLLOIDS IN CONFECTIONERY")

HYDROCOLLOIDS IN CONFECTIONERY

Part 3. The beginning can be found here #marusya_hydrocolloids

Previously, we analyzed in detail the first hydrocolloid - carrageenan. Now let's explore the following −

🔴 GELLAN GUM:
✔️ produced by algae fermentation
✔️dissolves in cold water
✔️rapid stability within minutes
✔️ there are two types - low-acyl and high-acyl

♦️ low-acyl gellan gum:
✔️is not thermally reversible
✔️has a brittle gel texture
✔️has a transparent gel
✔️doesn't melt
✔️setting point 60 C
✔️catalysts - calcium, sodium, potassium, acids
✔️ inhibitors - sodium and calcium salts

♦️high-acyl gellan gum:
✔️thermally reversible
✔️has elastic gel texture
✔️has an opaque gel
✔️melting point 80 C
✔️setting point 70 C
✔️no catalysts or inhibitors

Functions and other properties:
🌱used to create gels, sugar-reduced jams, fillings for pies and pastry, as well as for -

SPHERIFICATION is the process of making one liquid spherical by adding drops of that liquid to another one.

🌱 can be used with almost any product, regardless of its acidity
🌱 doesn't affect the taste of the final product
🌱 gel texture has a unique mouthfeel
🌱 when used together with pectin, it creates a filling that won't leak out during the baking process

## Tip 073 — Flavor Pairing: Blackberry

**Source:** merged B2-39 + B3-04 (identical content in both)

FLAVOR PAIRING. BLACKBERRY 🖤

#marusya_flavor_pairing

Did you miss this series of posts? 'cause we did. Therefore, today we are carrying out a flavor pairing examination of blackberries 🔍

Let's begin with the following — guys, no matter what culinary experiments you start with blackberries, if there's an opportunity to use wild berries, do give preference to them — wild berries have a much more interesting taste and a deeper aroma than berries grown in greenhouses.

Perfect blackberries will have the following flavor profile:

🌿 Flavor description
◾️ sweet and tart / sweet
◾️ refreshing
◾️ spicy notes
◾️ slightly astringent
◾️ sweet nutty aftertaste

🌿 Aroma profile

◾️ berry aroma
◾️ pronounced musky scent
◾️ clove notes
◾️ floral notes of tea rose
◾️ astringency of blackcurrant

Also, when choosing blackberries, be sure to pick ripe ones! Black ≠ ripe! A ripe blackberry will pull free from the stem with only a slight tug.

Pair well with:

🔻 spices and herbs — cinnamon, vanilla, ginger and mint; make a perfect match with such spices as cloves and star anise, which is proved by juicy photos of the delicious Blackberry, bilberry and star anise cupcakes posted in the carousel

🔻alcoholic drinks — Kirsch, Cointreau, Crème de cassis and Grand Marnier liqueurs

🔻 other fruits and berries such as apple, banana, blueberry, bilberry, orange, peach, plum and strawberry

A blackberry–raspberry pair deserves special attention — both berries belong to the same genus and have similar taste characteristics; when paired with raspberries, blackberries most clearly reveal their noble cedar wood notes

🔻 chocolate — white chocolate will add a dessert with blackberries a special charm and elegance — the sweetness of chocolate perfectly balances the light tartness of blackberries, and the vanilla notes, typical of both white chocolate and blackberries, will make this duet a "Perfect match" 📹 Just look at the cross section of the Chiffon honey cake with blackberries and white chocolate–coconut ganache 🤤

## Tip 074 — Life Hack: How to Know When Zephyr Syrup Is Cooked Without a Thermometer

**Source:** merged B2-40 + B3-05 (identical content in both)

LIFE HACK: HOW TO KNOW WHEN THE SYRUP FOR ZEPHYR IS COOKED WITHOUT A THERMOMETER

#marusya_theory_tips

Experienced zephyr makers know how important it is to start adding boiling syrup to the whipped berry puree in time — if the syrup is undercooked, the zephyr mass will not be thick enough, and if it's overcooked, the zephyr will quickly crystallize, and when tasting, the sugar will crunch on your teeth.

How to determine that very moment when the syrup is cooked?

There's a magic number — 110 °C. It is up to this temperature that it is recommended to cook syrup for zephyr. But I'd say that this number is literally "magic" — it's very difficult to correctly measure the temperature of a boiling syrup:

▫️ firstly, the syrup actively boils, which makes it impossible to correctly measure the temperature on the surface of the syrup using a pyrometer

▫️ secondly, there should be a certain minimum amount of the liquid in the saucepan so that the probe thermometer can correctly read the temperature, and if you don't make zephyrs in large batches, a small amount of syrup, which is usually required to make them, won't allow you to do that

Therefore, I never determine the doneness of the syrup by temperature — only by consistency! But how to do that?

💡 Here's a life hack for you!

Take a small amount of syrup with a spatula and raise it to eye level: if the syrup flows down in a thin stream, then the syrup is ready.

📎 In the carousel, you can find a short video from the live broadcast on the preparation of zephyr, where I clearly show how to check the doneness of syrup visually.

---

## Tip 075 — Why One Shouldn't Open the Oven While Baking a Sponge

**Source:** merged B2-41 + B3-06 (identical content in both)

WHY ONE SHOULDN'T OPEN THE OVEN WHILE BAKING A SPONGE

#marusya_theory_tips

If you open the oven while baking the sponge, the sponge will deflate, it's true. But why does this happen? Have you ever thought about it?

Let's figure it out.

Factors to consider:

▪️ the presence of baking powder in the recipe
▪️ the presence of water in the recipe (in the composition of eggs)
▪️ the presence of protein and starch (in the recipe or in the composition of the baking powder)
▪️ incorporated air
▪️ heating to high temperatures

Now let's have a closer look at each of the factors, step by step. Follow me.

🌬 By whipping the egg whites with sugar, we incorporate air into the meringue (and subsequently, into our sponge batter).

💧 When the baking powder is added along with the rest of the dry ingredients to the meringue, it reacts with the water contained in the eggs, and a neutralization reaction occurs, during which the carbon dioxide is released.

🔥Then, when we bake the sponge, this is what happens in the oven:

🗿 Under the influence of high temperatures, the process of protein and starch denaturation occurs, and they change from a liquid into a solid state

🫧 air bubbles, or bubbles of carbon dioxide, like any other gas, expand when heated (you might remember this from Physics lessons at school) and remain in the form of cavities in the sponge, in other words, make it porous, and after cooling, carbon dioxide and steam come out from these cavities, and they are filled with air

❌ If you open the oven while baking the sponge, the temperature in it will drop sharply, the gas will shrink back, the bubbles will become smaller, and if the protein has not yet had time to denature, the sponge will deflate.

---

## Tip 076 — Lumpy Cream Cheese Mixture: Why Does It Happen and How to Prevent It?

**Source:** merged B2-42 + B3-07 (identical content in both; B2 had flagged this as a possible boundary tip, B3's full surrounding context confirms it is complete and not truncated)

LUMPY CREAM CHEESE MIXTURE: WHY DOES IT HAPPEN AND HOW TO PREVENT IT?

#marusya_theory_tips

Once, at a masterclass, I approached a student and, seeing her cheesecake mixture, asked: "Have you used a sieve?", she replied: "Yes, how do you know?"

Guys, it's very simple — even if you push a lumpy cream cheese mixture through a sieve, you still will have a lumpy mixture. The only difference will be the size of the lumps which will be equal to the diameter of the sieve mesh.

Therefore, there are 2 answers to the question of why the cheesecake mixture is lumpy:

✖️ the mixture wasn't properly mixed
✖️ the mixture wasn't properly mixed + you tried to fix it in the wrong way

To prevent lumps in the cheesecake mixture, one should adhere to the following rules ⬇️

☑️ Use a flex edge flat beater

Don't mix the cheesecake mixture with a whisk attachment — firstly, the cream cheese will get caught up in the whisk's wires and lumps will form; secondly, the whisk will incorporate lots of air into the mixture, and as a result, you won't be able to fit all the mixture into the crust, so after baking the cheesecake will sink in the center.

📎 You can also use a paddle attachment, however, you'll have to periodically stop the mixer and scrape the cream cheese from the sides of the bowl and the attachment itself, while the flex edge flat beater removes the mixture from the sides of the bowl during mixing

☑️ Stick to the sequence of adding the ingredients:

🔽 cream cheese
🔽 sugar
🔽 eggs
🔽 flavoring additives (purees, ganaches, pastes)
🔽 cream, milk, alcohol

First, mix the cream cheese and sugar (if listed in the recipe) into a pasty consistency. Next, pour in the egg melange in a thin stream, or add the egg yolks one at a time, mixing thoroughly after each addition.

🖊 Egg melange is a homogeneous mass of eggs combined with a whisk or blender

Then add the flavorings, and lastly — the liquid ingredients.

📎 if the flavorings have a very thick consistency, e.g. dulce de leche, thick nut paste, it's better to add them before you start introducing the eggs

## Tip 077 — Honey Dough: 3 Consistencies You Should Know About

**Source:** from B3-08

HONEY DOUGH: 3 CONSISTENCIES YOU SHOULD KNOW ABOUT

If you still think that a honey cake is an uninteresting dessert that can only amaze you with the number of cake layers, just scroll through my feed and the myth will be dispelled instantly.

How to get such appetizing cross sections?

One of the keys is the optimal consistency of honey dough for each particular honey cake. Let me cover the crucial questions.

What determines the consistency of honey dough?
✔️ the amount of flour in its composition — the less flour you use, the more liquid and / or sticky the dough will be

❓What are the types of honey dough consistencies?
✔️ 1. Stiff
✔️ 2. Soft
✔️ 3. Liquid

STIFF HONEY DOUGH:

▪️obtained by adding a moderate amount of flour
▪️should be slightly sticky when mixed
▪️ready to work with (!!!) only after sitting for 15–20 minutes at room temperature — the fats contained in butter stabilize and the dough becomes non-sticky
▪️rolled out with a rolling pin
▪️easy, almost effortless to roll out

SOFT HONEY DOUGH:

▪️used for cake layers with a reduced flour content
▪️turns out very sticky when mixed
▪️ready to work with after 4–12 hours in the fridge (depending on the recipe) — during this time the fats become solid and stabilize completely –> the dough becomes non-sticky
▪️easy to roll out with a rolling pin

LIQUID HONEY DOUGH (BATTER):

▪️contains the least amount of flour
▪️ready to work with immediately after mixing
▪️cannot be rolled out — for this batter you'll need an offset spatula — evenly spread the batter over the parchment paper, giving it the desired shape

❓What does the consistency of honey dough affect?
✔️ the texture of finished cake layers

▪️ crispy and crumbly cake layers are obtained from stiff dough
▪️ soft dough ensures tender, soft and slightly airy cake layers
▪️ honey batter helps to get elastic and at the same time very pliable cake layers

## Tip 078 — Temperature for Adding Butter to Caramel

**Source:** from B3-09

TEMPERATURE FOR ADDING BUTTER TO CARAMEL

#marusya_theory_tips

Creamy caramel — a well-known to all confectioners and, seemingly, an easy-to-prepare French dessert, consisting of only 4 ingredients, in fact, is full of many secrets.

One of them is the temperature at which the butter is added to the caramel.

Let's have a look at 3 options, which I've tried and tested myself👌

🟠1. Adding the butter to caramel at 50 °C ensures
⬇️⬇️⬇️
🔸 the lightest shade
🔸 the thickest texture
🔸 rich creamy taste
🔸 pronounced buttery taste
🔸 mild caramel flavor

🟠 2. Adding the butter to caramel at 60 °C ensures
⬇️⬇️⬇️
🔸 caramel shade
🔸 nice soft texture
🔸 mild taste of butter or burnt sugar
🔸 perfectly balanced creamy caramel flavor

ℹ️ I normally add the butter at this temperature

🟠 3. Adding the butter to caramel at 170 °C (right into the dry caramel, before adding the cream) ensures
⬇️⬇️⬇️
🔸 the darkest shade ⬅️ the temperature doesn't drop as sharply as with the addition of cream
🔸 gooey texture, somewhat reminiscent of toffee
🔸 intense caramel flavor
🔸 nutty flavor ⬅️ the butter melts fast and boils, and as a result it acquires nutty notes, as in the preparation of beurre noisette

ℹ️ you can read more about the beurre noisette by following the hashtag #marusya_beurre_noisette

## Tip 079 — What You Need to Know About Mousses, Part 1: Mousse Base & Aerator (Meringue)

**Source:** from B3-10

WHAT YOU NEED TO KNOW ABOUT MOUSSES
part 1

#marusya_about_mousse

Guys, today I'm presenting to you a new series of posts. Let's learn the science of making mousses from A to Z so you can feel confident when making them.

Let's start, as always, with the basics. What is a mousse?

Mousse (from French 'mousse' – "foam") is a dish or a filling for cakes and other desserts with the consistency of an airy, light foam, composed of:

1️⃣ flavoring component (base)
2️⃣ whipped component (aerator)
3️⃣ thickener

Now let's take a closer look at each component.

✅ MOUSSE BASE is the main flavoring component which can be:

🔸 custard
🔸 ganache
🔸 fruit puree
🔸 creamy caramel
🔸 nut or flower paste
🔸 yogurt or fermented baked milk (ryazhenka)
🔸 coffee/tea/alcohol

There are mousses with a "pure" base, i.e. only one ingredient is used, for example, caramel. But in my opinion, the most interesting are mousses with complex bases – you can combine coffee with fruit puree, caramel with nut paste or even crème anglaise with alcohol!

The mousse base takes up ~35-55% of the total mass, depending on the thickness of the base and the desired consistency of the finished mousse.

✅ WHIPPED COMPONENT OR AERATOR is responsible for airiness and lightness of the mousse. These aerators can be:

🔸 whipping cream
🔸 meringue
🔸 pâte à bombe

🖊 Pâte à bombe – a cream made of whipped egg yolks and boiled sugar syrup.

Before being added to the mousse, any of these ingredients is whipped to incorporate air bubbles, which helps to make the product airy and fluffy.

Meringue-based mousses

🔺most often, the Italian meringue is used, as it's the most stable of all types of meringue
🔺contain the greatest number of air bubbles, and therefore are the most unstable, soft and delicate
🔺 have the shortest shelf life
🔺 keep maximum taste of the flavoring base

---

## Tip 080 — What You Need to Know About Mousses, Part 2: Cream-Based Mousses & Thickener (Gelatin)

**Source:** from B3-11

part 2

#marusya_about_mousse

Thanks to a large number of pluses under yesterday's post, I decided to immediately write a continuation – it's always nice to see mutual interest❤️

Cream-based mousses

🔺 make the taste of the mousse base less prominent, unlike meringue-based mousses
🔺 more stable and thicker than those made with meringue
🔺 use cold 30–33% cream
🔺 whip it into soft peaks at medium speed – fast whipping leads to a less stable foam
🔺 stick to the whipping temperature – 0–5 °C – if the temperature is at least 3–4 °C higher, the cream will whip much worse or may even curdle
🔺 it's important not to over-whip the cream, otherwise it'll be difficult to combine it with the base, and the texture of the mousse won't be perfect
🔺 the temperature of the base to which we add the whipped cream shouldn't exceed 35 °C, otherwise the mousse will turn out to be too liquid

It's not common to use pure pâte à bombe to make mousses – most often it's combined with cream or meringue.

In general, in one mousse you can use:

▪️whipped cream and meringue
▪️meringue and pâte à bombe
▪️whipped cream and pâte à bombe
▪️whipped cream, meringue and pâte à bombe

The whipped component can take up around 35–60% of the total mass of the mousse, depending on the thickness of the base, the type of aeration and the desired consistency of the finished mousse.

✅ THICKENER: traditionally, mousses are made with gelatin. However, some of the recipes call for agar-agar or cocoa butter, or gelatin + cocoa butter. The more gelatin you add, the thicker the mousse will be, and vice versa.

Gelatin can take up 0.5–2% of the total mass of the mousse:

▪️ if the mousse contains alcohol, tart fruits or lots of air (meringue-based mousses), the percentage of gelatin will be around 2%
▪️ mousses made from milk or white couvertures (chocolate with a high content of cocoa butter), contain around 0.5% gelatin
▪️if it's a chocolate-based mousse which contains enough cocoa butter (couverture/dark/bitter chocolate), you can skip adding the gelatin

## Tip 081 — Pistachio Paste: 2 Tips You Didn't Know About

**Source:** from B3-12

PISTACHIO PASTE: 2 TIPS YOU DIDN'T KNOW ABOUT

Pistachio paste has been added to all the desserts you see in the carousel, whether it be crémeux, cream for coating, cream cheese layer or frosting used between layers.

For me, it was extremely important to get a beautiful pistachio color and, at the same time, a pronounced yet natural pistachio taste.

How have I achieved this?

Let me share my 2 little tips

🌿 TIP #1

✅ Combination of natural pistachio paste with pistachio delipaste

A natural nutty taste is obtained using natural pistachio paste that has 100% pistachio in the composition. But most often, it has an unappetizing greenish brown color, so it cannot give the product the very beautiful pistachio color that instantly makes us want to try the dessert.

On the contrary, pistachio delipaste (with only ~ 7% pistachio in the composition) contains a natural dye chlorophyll, which gives a beautiful pistachio color to products. What is more, the addition of delipaste balances out the slight bitterness of natural pistachio paste, and you get a pleasant taste reminiscent of pistachio ice cream.

🖊delipaste is a concentrated flavoring paste that gives a pronounced taste, aroma and color of a certain product to a dessert.

🌿 TIP #2

✅Natural paste and delipaste ratio is 1:1 or 2:1

You can adjust the amount of paste added depending on what you want to emphasize more — taste or color.

🌿 BRANDS I USE (*BONUS)

✅ Pastes — Joygelato, ТМ Irca (Italy) or NutFarine (Ukraine)
✅ Delipaste — Fabbri (Italy)

## Tip 082 — How to Temper Eggs and Why You Need To, Part 1: Definition

**Source:** from B3-13

HOW TO TEMPER EGGS AND WHY YOU NEED TO🥚🌡
part 1

#marusya_theory_tips

Every year I become more convinced that eggs are one of the most interesting products in the confectionery world!

This post is about egg tempering. I'll tell you how to temper the eggs and, more importantly, why we need to.

I think you immediately started to wonder:

❓ Tempering eggs? How come? It's all about chocolate!
✔️ In fact, in the confectionery world, the word "tempering" has 2 meanings:

🌡 1st — it's the process of heating and cooling chocolate to certain temperatures in order to obtain stable cocoa butter crystals responsible for giving the chocolate a glossy look and a nice crunch;

🌡 2d — it's the process of slowly heating the eggs.

Scientifically, this method can be described as follows:

TEMPERING EGGS means slowly combining beaten cold or room-temperature eggs with a hot liquid without scrambling or curdling the eggs in the process.

HOW TO TEMPER EGGS (IN BRIEF)

1. Slowly stream the hot/boiling liquid into the cold/room-temperature eggs while whisking.
2. Pour the warm eggs back into the saucepan.

---

## Tip 083 — How to Temper Eggs and Why You Need To, Part 2: Why & How Much Liquid

**Source:** from B3-14

part 2

#marusya_theory_tips

In a previous post, we discussed with you what tempering eggs is. So where does this process take place?

In fact, it's quite simple and, most likely, you have already tempered eggs without knowing it. We temper eggs when we prepare the base for custards, pudding, ice cream, flan, crème brûlée, etc.

In the preparation of any custard, for example, there's a step of trickling hot milk or cream into the egg mixture — when gradually adding a hot liquid to the eggs, we thereby temper them.

🔴 WHY WE NEED TO TEMPER EGGS

Tempering eggs prevents the proteins from coagulating. Eggs are very sensitive to temperature. If you combine the eggs with hot liquid in one go, they will curdle. Therefore, when tempering, 2 rules must be observed:

✔️ add the hot liquid gradually, in a thin stream
✔️ constantly stir the mixture with a whisk

Lastly, let's analyze one more interesting nuance.

🔴 HOW MUCH HOT LIQUID SHOULD BE ADDED TO EGGS

Tempering is a means of getting something (eggs) from point A to point B (from cold to hot) slowly and steadily. And is a technique, not a scientific formula, which is why there's no definitive answer for this question.

But there is no need to add ✖️all the hot liquid✖️ to the eggs — you should add just enough liquid to make the eggs hot (not warm, but hot!). You can then safely pour the tempered eggs into the saucepan with the remaining hot liquid. Just do not forget to stir with a whisk until the mixture becomes homogeneous.

---

## Tip 084 — Hydrocolloids in Confectionery Art, Part 1: What Are Hydrocolloids?

**Source:** from B3-15

HYDROCOLLOIDS IN CONFECTIONERY ART
part 1

#marusya_hydrocolloids

The other day, while searching for useful information about the functions of sugar in shortcrust pastry, I came across an interesting block "Modern techniques in confectionery art", devoted to hydrocolloids.

Let's figure out what they are.

Basically, hydrocolloids are a heterogeneous group of long chain polymers (polysaccharides and proteins) characterized by their property of forming viscous dispersions and/or gels when dispersed in water.

It may sound confusing, but most of them are familiar to you. In simple terms, hydrocolloids are stabilizers and thickeners, derived from animal and plant sources.

Hydrocolloids of animal origin include the well-known gelatin. But I suggest having a closer look at hydrocolloids of vegetable origin.

Hydrocolloids derived from plant sources include:
🌱agar-agar
🌱pectin
🌱carrageenans
🌱gellan gum
🌱metilgel/methylcellulose
🌱sodium alginate
🌱tapioca maltodextrin
🌱xanthan gum
🌱guar gum

I think most of you only know the agar-agar and pectin from this list.

Pastry enthusiasts obsessed with confectionery could have seen the rest on the labels of bright, colorful and alluring SOSA bottles.

Or on white, plain and strict but no less attractive large containers with a red lid from LOUIS FRANCOIS, arranged in beautiful rows in the DGF boutique.

Those who are not into the confectionery business and are not familiar with the brands mentioned above, may remember some of the stabilizers and thickeners from the compositions of dairy and meat products.

So why, where and how are hydrocolloids used in modern confectionery production?

First of all, they are used as thickeners, of course, — to obtain gels, jellies, sauces, jams and other stable fillings. This is well known to everyone. But few people know that with the help of vegetable hydrocolloids, you can make ...

Can you guess the answer? Type it in the comments section.

P.S. Let me give you a little hint — hydrocolloids are often used in modern molecular cuisine 🤫

## Tip 085 — Hydrocolloids in Confectionery Art, Part 2: Applications & Carrageenan

**Source:** from B3-16

part 2

#marusya_hydrocolloids

Judging by the answers in the comments section under the previous part of this post, you don't know much about the application of hydrocolloids in modern cuisine. Let's try to change this.

So, here is a list of the most interesting and unusual, in my opinion, applications of plant-derived hydrocolloids:

🌱foams – vary from thick and rich (like shaving foam) to light "soapy" foams with fruit, milk or coffee taste
🌱 crispy dry foams made from milk and juices
🌱 "hot ice cream" that melts when it starts cooling
🌱 "caviar" – semi-solid beads with thin membranes out of liquids
🌱 "raw egg yolk" made from mango or apricot puree – a large sphere with thin gel-like membrane out of liquid
🌱 dry caramel, nut paste
🌱 gluten-free flour.

I'm curious to know which of the above applications of hydrocolloids interested you the most? Type in the comments. If there are many comments, I will share a recipe 😉

In the meantime, let's get acquainted with the most mysterious representative of hydrocolloids of vegetable origin.

🔴 Carrageenan
✔️extracted from some red seaweeds
✔️thermally reversible
✔️dissolves in cold water when mixed with sugar
✔️melting point 80ºC
✔️setting point 70ºC
✔️catalyst – calcium / potassium (usually in dairy products)

Functions:
🌱 turns sauces into gels (rigid or elastic, tough or tender)

🌱 increases the viscosity of liquids – is used, for example, in soy milk to give it a milk-like texture (without carrageenan it would have the consistency of water)

🌱 suspends particles in liquid – is used, for example, in chocolate milk, so that solid cocoa particles do not settle at the bottom

🌱 is used as a vegetable substitute for gelatin to stabilize mousses and frostings (mix the carrageenan powder with sugar and add to a cold liquid, then bring it to a boil, pour into molds and allow to stabilize)

**Notes:** This confirms Tip 072's "part 3" (gellan gum) is indeed the direct sequel to this "part 2" (carrageenan), which in turn follows Tip 084's "part 1" — the hydrocolloids series (parts 1, 2, 3) spans Tips 084, 085, and 072, appearing out of chronological order in the master's numbering because Tip 072 was merged earlier (at its B2/B3 seam position) before Batch 3's remaining unique content (which contains parts 1 and 2) was reached. This is a source/document ordering artifact, not a merge error — the three parts are correctly identified and cross-referenced here.

## Tip 086 — Everything You Need to Know About Ganaches, Part 1: What Is Ganache?

**Source:** from B3-17

EVERYTHING YOU NEED TO KNOW ABOUT GANASHES
part 1

#marusya_about_ganache

I wanted to post a brief instruction on how to make chocolate ganache, but when I started writing, I realized that this topic requires more than one post. So welcome the new series of posts😉

Let's start, as always, with the basics.

⚫️What is ganache?

Ganache is a mixture (emulsion) of chocolate (white, milk or dark) and liquid.

Ganache is made using a hot method — you heat the liquid and pour it over chocolate, after which you allow the ganache to cool.

What exactly is meant by liquid?

If on the Internet you come across an article that says that there are three types of ganache — made with heavy cream, butter and heavy cream with the addition of butter — you can immediately close the tab, since the author has only superficial knowledge about such an interesting product as ganache.

Firstly, the liquid in ganache can be represented by:

▪️milk
▪️heavy cream
▪️fruit or berry juice
▪️fruit or berry puree
▪️and even crème anglaise or other custard

Secondly, in some cases, you can add to your ganache the following ingredients:

▪️butter
▪️sugar
▪️all kinds of flavoring additives

Let's continue.

Depending on the texture and purpose, the ganache can be thick or more runny:

▪️ runny — is used for tarts and chocolates
▪️ soft — can be used as an interlayer or filling for cakes, individual cakes or desserts
▪️ thick — is used for bonbons and cake coating

The texture of the ganache can also be changed — it depends on the way you stir it — e.g, ganache can be whipped or made using the Chantilly cream method.

Ganache is a stable and balanced emulsion type chain in which fat molecules (in particular cocoa butter, milk fat, milk and/or heavy cream) are combined into a homogeneous substance together with water molecules thanks to emulsifiers present in chocolate (lecithin) and in heavy cream (proteins).

**Notes:** First of the 6-part ganache series (this Tip 086 = part 1; parts 2–6 already appear earlier in the master as Tips 031/072's neighbors — see cross-reference below). **MERGE NOTE:** Parts 2–6 of this series were captured independently by both Batch 3 (B3-31 through B3-35) and Batch 4 (B4-01 through B4-04, covering parts 4–6 only) at their overlapping seam (source lines 3184–3376). Comparing the two: Batch 3's versions (B3-31–35) include full title/hashtag context and complete intro paragraphs for parts 2 and 3, which Batch 4's versions of the same parts 4–6 lack (B4-01 in particular is explicitly noted by Batch 4 itself as a truncated tail fragment missing its own opening). Batch 3's fuller renditions are used for Tips 087–091 below.

## Tip 087 — Everything You Need to Know About Ganaches, Part 2: Chocolate Used in Ganache

**Source:** merged B3-31 + tail of B4-41 references (B4 did not independently capture part 2 in full; B3's version used as the complete source)

Part 2

#marusya_about_ganache

One of the last posts of 2022 was the dedicated to ganache theory. I am very pleased that you liked that post, so here's the continuation😉

As we have already discussed, ganache is a mixture of chocolate and liquid. Now let's talk in detail about the chocolate used to make ganaches.

The following types of chocolate can be used for making ganaches:
▪️white
▪️milk
▪️dark
▪️bitter

Chocolate can be used in any form:
▪️chocolate bars (tablets)
▪️chocolate blocks (chunks)
▪️chocolate callets (chips)
▪️chocolate drops

I usually work with callets, as they help to speed up the melting of chocolate when in contact with hot liquid.

📎 the main requirement is that callets should be stored in sealed packaging, protected from moisture — if water gets into chocolate, its shelf life decreases and the quality of the product deteriorates

Impact factors

⚫️TEXTURE

▪️ thanks to the cocoa butter, ganache becomes fluid when heated and sets when cooled (due to the crystallization of cocoa butter molecules)
▪️the lower the cocoa butter content and the higher the sugar content are, the softer the consistency of the ganache is

💎for making ganache the chocolate should contain at least 18% cocoa butter and 40-70% cocoa products (these figures are usually indicated by manufacturers on the packaging)

📎By cocoa products I mean the content of cocoa butter and cocoa mass (cocoa powder).

⚫️ TASTE

▪️ characteristic flavor of chocolate:

⚪️white – sweet milky
🟤milk – vanilla-caramel
⚫️dark – rich chocolate

▪️ characteristic chocolate bitterness: it reinforces in ganache due to the increased content of cocoa products (70% or more) and is leveled when the content of cocoa products is around 50% (medium).

## Tip 088 — Everything You Need to Know About Ganaches, Part 3: Liquid Types & Dosage

**Source:** from B3-32

part 3

#marusya_about_ganache

Apart from chocolate, ganache must contain liquid. But here is where it gets fun: you can use different liquids, e.g:

⚪️ Fresh or pasteurized cream

The fat content of cream should be 35-40%. Thanks to minimal heat treatment, the finished ganache will have a rich creamy taste, but at the same time ‒ a reduced shelf life.

⚪️ Sterilized or UHT cream

The fat content is 35-37%. These types of cream are exposed to high temperatures for a short period of time. This process extends the product's shelf life making these creams chefs' favorites when working with ganaches.

⚪️ Milk

Is used to replace some or all of the cream in ganache. Unsweetened condensed milk (also called evaporated or concentrated milk) is often used to reduce the amount of water added, which negatively affects the shelf life of the ganache.

🟣 Fruit or berry puree

Part of the cream can be replaced with puree. Most often, the ratio of puree and cream is 1:1. It is added to give ganache a fruity flavor and aroma, as well as interesting vivid colors.

💎DOSAGE

▫️milk: maximum 40%
▫️cream: 20-50%
▫️puree: maximum 50%

How the liquid added affects the ganache?

🟣 TEXTURE
◽️ gives the ganache a silky, melting texture during tasting (provided the fat content is at least 35%)
◽️ in some cases, e.g. when preparing whipped ganache, cream is a necessary element for increasing the volume, as well as trapping and stabilizing air bubbles in the finished ganache

🟣 MOISTURE CONTENT
◽️ liquid with a water content of about 60% dissolves sugar and makes the chocolate less dense

🟣 TASTE
▫️ due to the fat molecules present in the composition, it has aroma-retaining properties and is a carrier of taste to our receptors

**Notes:** B3's version (used here) is complete and self-contained. Batch 4's B4-01 captured only the tail of this same content (dosage + effects, lines 3250-3266) without the opening paragraphs — B3's fuller version is used; B4-01 is redundant and not counted separately.

## Tip 089 — Everything You Need to Know About Ganaches, Part 4: Butter as an Additive

**Source:** merged B3-33 + B4-02 (identical content in both; B3's fuller title/heading context kept)

Part 4

So, did you miss this series of posts?

Today we continue talking about ganaches and as usual, I'm ready to share with you a concentrate of the best information from top pastry chefs and chocolatiers, seen through the prism of my experience and critical thinking.

Check out the beginning here #marusya_about_ganache

We've already analyzed the chocolate and liquid in the composition of ganache in detail, now let's move on to possible additives.

➕ Butter
🔺fat content — 82% and more — the more fat contained in butter, the longer the shelf life of the finished ganache will be
🔺 the butter is added when the ganache is at 32–34ºС

💎 dose — 5–15%

📌recommended dose of fats (butter + cocoa butter) — 25–40% — too much fats will destabilize the ganache (the emulsion will break), while insufficient amount of fats will result in a ganache that's too thick

When added to a ganache, the butter influences its:

🔴 TEXTURE

▪️when the butter is heated, the consistency of the ganache becomes runnier, and when it cools, the ganache becomes thicker
▪️makes the ganache smooth and creamy, adds it a glossy shine, as well as a delicate, melt-in-the mouth texture (provided the butter is added at the end of cooking, at 30-35ºС, without prior melting).

🔴 TASTE

▪️ has aroma-retaining properties and is a carrier of taste to our receptors due to fat molecules in the composition
▪️ reduces the bitterness of chocolate with a high content of cocoa products, e.g., >70%.

🔴SHELF LIFE

▪️ adding butter to cream before boiling it prolongs the shelf life of the ganache, and also contributes to the even distribution of fat molecules in the mixture.

## Tip 090 — Everything You Need to Know About Ganaches, Part 5: Sugar, Sorbitol & Aromatic Additives

**Source:** merged B3-34 + B4-03 (identical content in both)

Part 5

#marusya_about_ganache

In the previous part we discussed all the nuances of adding butter to a ganache. But apart from it, the following ingredients can be added to the ganache, namely:

➕ Sugar
🔺sugar in ganaches can be represented by: sucrose (=sugar), invert sugar or glucose syrup (high in dextrose)
💎 dose — at least 25% of the total weight

When added to a ganache, sugar influences its:

🔴 TASTE

▪️makes the ganache sweeter

🔴 TEXTURE

▪️makes it softer and more elastic
▪️increases the viscosity of the ganache, making it more stable due to its hygroscopic properties

🔴 SHELF LIFE

▪️ prolongs shelf life — sugar helps to "bind" water present in cream, making it unavailable for the microorganisms (molds) to multiply.

➕ Sorbitol or food supplement E420
It is an odorless white crystalline powder with a pleasant taste. It is two times less sweet than regular sugar and is very hygroscopic (absorbs and retains moisture).
💎 dose — 1.5–2.5% of the total weight

The way sorbitol affects the ganache is similar to sugar, however, E420 is used primarily as a stabilizer, and not as a sweetener.

➕ Aromatic additives
🔺 can be represented by various extracts, essential oils, alcohol, as well as flavored cream or milk
🔺 all additives that are sensitive to microflora (cocoa powder, spices, etc.) should be boiled for several minutes along with the cream

## Tip 091 — Everything You Need to Know About Ganaches, Part 6 (Last): Why Ganache Splits & How to Store It

**Source:** merged B3-35 + B4-04 (identical content in both)

Part 6 and last

Guys, have you missed us?

'cause we missed you immensely!

Inspired after a short vacation, we are preparing new wow-materials for you!

Today I'd like to share with you the last piece of information about ganaches in order you could round up all the information you already learnt and arrange it into a coherent picture.

We have already discussed with you what ganache is, what it can consist of and what are the possible additives, and we also examined in detail each basic component separately: the recommended dosage of the ingredient and its influence on the texture, taste and shelf life of the ganache.

Check out the beginning here #marusya_about_ganache

Today I will share with you a couple more useful tips and tricks.

🤔 Why has your ganache split?

1. It doesn't contain enough liquid
2. Poor dispersion of fats in water
3. Indoor temperature is too high

🆘 How to fix split ganache?

1. Add heavy cream/milk/puree
2. Process the mixture with a hand blender
3. Process the mixture with a hand blender and allow it to cool

↘️↙️
⠀⬇️

And here comes another interesting nuance:

📍 Ganache should be processed with a hand blender while it is hot to avoid air bubbles getting into the mixture! This is due to the fact that:

🔺 air is a favorable environment for microorganisms
🔺 fats that are in contact with air eventually turn rancid

♻️ How and how long can ganache be stored?

✔️ Before storing and cooling the ganache, it should be covered with cling film that comes in contact with the surface of the ganache. This way, you will protect your ganache from air, microorganisms and evaporation of aromatic substances.

✔️ Ganaches can be stored in the fridge at 4ºС for no more than 2 days. You can freeze them only in finished products.
