## Tip 092 — A Crash Course in Chocolate, Part 1: What Is Chocolate & Composition

**Source:** from B3-18

A CRASH COURSE IN CHOCOLATE
part 1

Hello everyone! I have great news for you - today we are launching a series of articles about chocolate!

Even not being a chocolatier, almost every confectioner encounters chocolate when making cake decorating, ganaches, frostings, mousses, cremeux and other fillings. Therefore, acquaintance and building friendly relationships with a product such as chocolate will be relevant for many of you.

Let's start with the basics.

WHAT IS CHOCOLATE?

Chocolate is a confectionery product based on cocoa butter, which is a product of processing cocoa beans - the seeds of the chocolate tree, which are rich in theobromine and caffeine.

TYPES OF CHOCOLATE AND ITS COMPOSITION

1. Black
2. Milk
3. White

The division is based on differences in composition, which determines the appearance of the product and its taste.

COMPOSITION OF BLACK CHOCOLATE

- cocoa mass
- cocoa butter
- sugar

COMPOSITION OF MILK CHOCOLATE

- cocoa mass
- cocoa butter
- sugar
- milk powder

COMPOSITION OF WHITE CHOCOLATE

- cocoa butter
- sugar
- milk powder

*In fact, white chocolate isn't really chocolate, as it doesn't contain cocoa mass (cocoa beans). On this basis, the debate about whether to consider it real chocolate is still ongoing. I work with it all the time and refer it to chocolate, as due to the presence of cocoa butter in its composition, it has all the properties of chocolate.

WHAT ELSE CAN BE IN THE COMPOSITION OF CHOCOLATE:

In order for all the components to combine into a smooth emulsion, a small percentage of an emulsifier, such as soy lecithin, is often added to chocolate.

WHAT YOU SHOULD NOT HAVE IN CHOCOLATE:

Good chocolate should be free of vegetable oils, vanillin and any other by-products.

And now the SECRET INFORMATION which is in open access 😁

🟤 The percentage indicated on the chocolate packaging (70%, 54.5%, 33%) is the percentage of cocoa mass.

❗️BUT❗️

⚪️ The percentage indicated on the WHITE chocolate packaging is the percentage of cocoa butter content.

## Tip 094 — A Crash Course in Chocolate, Part 3: Tempering Definition & Classic Method

**Source:** from B3-20

part 3

In the previous posts — #marusya_about_chocolate — we have covered the basics: definition of chocolate, its types, composition and brands.

With this knowledge, we can proceed to the next step — tempering.

WHAT IS TEMPERING?

Tempering, or crystallization, is the process of heating and cooling chocolate to certain temperatures in order to obtain stable cocoa butter crystals, which gives the chocolate a number of important properties:

✔️gives chocolate a glossy finish and make it snap cleanly
✔️allows you to avoid undesirable imperfections on the surface of chocolate:
▪️ sugar bloom — a whitish film what affects the tasting
▪️ fat bloom — greyish or whitish streaks or spots of cocoa butter
✔️chocolate hardens much faster
✔️ stays solid in warmer temperatures, as it has a higher melting point.

METHODS FOR TEMPERING CHOCOLATE:

✔️classic (tabling)
✔️seeding (callets)
✔️Mycryo — powdered cocoa butter

CLASSIC TEMPERING METHOD

The method requires you to use a marble or granite slab/countertop. Since not everyone has an access to it, I won't dwell on it in detail. But the technique itself is important for understanding the processes:

Destroy the solid form of chocolate by bringing it to the melting point:
▪️dark — 50-55°C
▪️milk — 45-50°C
▪️white — 40-45°C

✔️Bring it down to the crystallization temperature of cocoa butter — 27°C

✔️Bring the chocolate to its working temperature:
▪️dark — 31-32°C
▪️milk — 30-31°C
▪️white — 29-30°C

✔️Give liquid chocolate the desired shape.

✔️Let the chocolate stabilize and become solid again.

SEEDING METHOD OR CALLET TEMPERING

The trick is that the callets themselves are already tempered. Cocoa butter crystals are already crystallized, that is, step 2 from the classic method is omitted. All you need to do is melt the chocolate and bring it to working temperature.

---

## Tip 095 — A Crash Course in Chocolate, Part 4: Seeding Method Detail

**Source:** from B3-21

part 4

We continue a detailed analysis of the methods for tempering chocolate.

As you remember from the previous post — #marusya_about_chocolate — the classic method is not the easiest one, since it requires lots of effort as well as marble or granite slab/countertop. But at the same time, this is the only way to temper chocolate in large quantities (over 1 kg).

In large scale production where it's often necessary to temper 1-2 kg of chocolate or even more, they use special tempering machines that maintain the required temperature of chocolate.

For home bakers, one of the best methods is the seeding method or callet tempering. Let's learn how it works.

SEEDING METHOD OR CALLET TEMPERING

Destroy the solid form of chocolate by bringing it to the melting point:
▪️dark — 50–55 °C
▪️milk — 45–50 °C
▪️white — 40–45 °C

✔️Add chocolate callets and stir to bring the chocolate to its working temperature:
▪️dark — 31–32 °C
▪️milk — 30–31 °C
▪️white — 29–30 °C

And voilà — you can give liquid chocolate the desired shape👌

With this method there is no need to first bring chocolate down to the crystallization temperature of cocoa butter (27 °C), and then bring it up to the working temperature, as with the classic method, since the callets themselves are already tempered and have a stable crystalline structure.

❓If I use chocolate callets only, then I just melt them and that's it?

❌ No!

Even if you use only chocolate in callets, bringing it to working temperature is not enough — heat treatment destroys the bonds between cocoa butter molecules, the crystalline structure breaks up and collapses.

That's why it is necessary to add cold callets with a stable crystalline structure to melted chocolate, as they prompt the chain reaction resulting in properly crystallized chocolate.

---

## Tip 096 — A Crash Course in Chocolate, Part 5: Secrets of Seeding Method (Melting Tips)

**Source:** from B3-22

part 5

Secrets of tempering chocolate using seeding method (callets)

1️⃣ It is best to melt chocolate in the microwave. Chocolate is very hygroscopic, meaning it absorbs moisture from the air. Therefore, a water bath will not be the best option for melting chocolate.

2️⃣ To avoid overheating, melt the chocolate in the microwave in 20 to 30 second intervals, thoroughly stirring it with a spatula between each interval.

📍 melt the white chocolate on the lowest power level and in very short, 10-20 second intervals as it can be easily overheated

3️⃣ I do not recommend melting chocolate in glassware — glass retains temperature for a long time and gives it to chocolate even after we've already brought it to 50-55 °C (45-50 °C milk and 40-45 °C white chocolate). Opt for plastic vessels instead.

4️⃣ Chocolate must be constantly stirred! This is necessary in order to ensure the same temperature of chocolate at the bottom, sides and in the middle of the bowl, as well as to correctly measure the temperature. It is most convenient to measure the temperature of chocolate with a pyrometer.

5️⃣ In order to bring the chocolate to its working temperature (dark — 31-32 °C, milk — 30-31 °C, white — 29-30 °C), add callets to the melted chocolate (20-25% of the total volume). With this amount, stable crystals will prompt the chain reaction resulting in properly crystallized chocolate.

This ratio is considered optimal, but since there are many variables in this equation, I would call it only approximate. You can be guided by it, but make sure to always keep an eye on the temperature of the chocolate.

6️⃣ When you get down to work, please remember that:

🔺 You need to work with chocolate quickly — if the chocolate is properly tempered, it will set in 3 minutes at a temperature of about 20 °C
🔺 white chocolate is much thicker than dark or milk chocolate — to make it easier to work with, add a little cocoa butter along with the callets to it

---

## Tip 097 — A Crash Course in Chocolate, Part 6: What Is Mycryo?

**Source:** from B3-23a

part 6

#marusya_about_chocolate

Today I suggest talking about a very interesting product, which has no analogues on the market at the moment — Mycryo.

🟤 WHAT IS MYCRYO?

Mycryo® is the brand name of the Barry Callebaut group of companies, which specializes in the production of a unique product — pure cocoa butter in powder form.

This 100% vegetable fat is an absolutely healthy product, mostly thanks to cocoa beans: it contains no cholesterol, has low calorie content, and you need less fat for frying.

If you look at the original packaging of Mycryo, you will see a photo of a delicious steak. And there's a reason for that. Mycryo has a number of unique properties that make the range of its application very diverse:

🔺 it resists frying temperatures better than butter or oils, and doesn't burn
🔺brings out the natural flavors of even the most delicate ingredients, and unlike other fats, it does not dominate over them
🔺 and, of course, facilitates the process of chocolate tempering

🟤 HOW IS MYCRYO OBTAINED?

Cocoa butter, obtained through roasting and grinding cocoa beans, is then frozen at very low temperatures to produce a dry powder. In other words, atomization occurs — dry powder is cooled to approximately -250 °C.

As a result, cocoa butter takes the form of granules, which are pure beta crystals, which are essential for proper chocolate tempering.

Let's try to figure it out.

🟤 TYPES OF COCOA BUTTER CRYSTALS

So, there are six types of cocoa butter crystals, each with a name: γ‎ (gamma), α (alpha), β'' (beta 2), β' (beta prime), β (beta) and super-β (super-beta).

They differ from each other by the strength of the bonds between the molecules. The stronger the bonds are, the more energy (higher temperature) is needed to break these bonds.

Of all six crystals, only one fits for tempering — beta (β) — they are the most stable and form the correct crystal lattice.

---

## Tip 098 — A Crash Course in Chocolate, Part 7: Mycryo Tempering — Conventional vs. My Method

**Source:** from B3-23b

part 7

#marusya_about_chocolate

Chocolate tempering with Mycryo — the conventional technique VS method I use.

You asked me for the details, so there you are.

🟤 CONVENTIONAL TECHNIQUE FOR CHOCOLATE TEMPERING WITH MYCRYO

1️⃣ Melt the chocolate to 40-45 °C in a microwave oven or over a water bath.
2️⃣ Leave the chocolate to cool (to 34-35 °C for dark chocolate or 33-34 °C for milk, white or colored chocolate) at room temperature.
3️⃣ Add Mycryo (1% of the chocolate mass), mix thoroughly.
4️⃣ Once the chocolate has reached an ideal temperature (31-32 °C for dark chocolate or 29-30 °C for milk, white or colored chocolate), use the product.
5️⃣ To use the chocolate for a longer time, keep it at 31-32 °C in the case of dark chocolate or 29-30 °C in the case of milk, white or colored chocolate.

🟤 MY CHOCOLATE TEMPERING METHOD USING MYCRYO

1️⃣ Melt the chocolate to 40-45 °C in the microwave
❌ It is better not to use a water bath — chocolate is very hygroscopic

2️⃣ Add ~15-20% callets of the total chocolate mass and mix thoroughly until they have completely dissolved, lowering the temperature to 35 °C.
❌Cooling chocolate at room temperature takes a long time and it must be constantly stirred, otherwise the crystallization will be uneven — chocolate cools much faster on the walls of the bowl.

❗️3️⃣ Sift Mycryo through a fine sieve to get rid of large pieces — when adding Mycryo, the temperature of the chocolate drops rapidly, so you need to combine the chocolate with cocoa butter into a homogeneous mass very quickly, which makes it difficult for large pieces of Mycryo to get in.

4️⃣ Add Mycryo (2% of the chocolate mass), stir thoroughly.
❌ Earlier on the official website it was indicated that 1% Mycryo is enough, but recently the manufacturer has changed the proportion to 2% — that is, 2 g of Mycryo per 100 g of chocolate.

✅5️⃣ see step 4 of the conventional technique
✅6️⃣ see step 5 of the conventional technique

## Tip 099 — All You Need to Know About Starch

**Source:** from B3-24

ALL YOU NEED TO KNOW ABOUT STARCH

#marusya_about _starch

We've already talked about pectin, agar-agar and gelatin. So today I suggest talking about the most popular thickening agent — starch.

In fact, there are lots of types of starch: potato, rice, wheat, corn, tapioca, sorghum, etc. However, the most common types are potato and cornstarch.

It is preferable to use cornstarch for making desserts, and it's not recommended to replace it with potato starch. Cornstarch ensures a more delicate texture, while adding potato starch results in a much thicker texture.

Potato starch
🔸 ensures a more fluid texture
🔸 has a characteristic flavor
🔸 the starch mixture is transparent
🔸 may impart a bluish color to some dishes

Cornstarch
🔸 ensures a more viscous texture
🔸 has no aftertaste
🔸 the starch mixture is turbid
🔸 does not change the color of the dish

Types of cornstarch
✔️regular cornstarch — does not contain impurities and has a white color
✔️modified starch — a safe product, additionally processed for better absorption

Take into account that❗️starch as a thickener activates properly only after bringing the mixture to a boil❗️

The boiling point of mixtures with starch added is 82 °C.

Bring the filling or custard with cornstarch to a boil, then, stirring vigorously with a spatula, allow it to cook over low heat for several minutes so that the starch has completely denatured — subjected to a heat treatment, the starch granules should swell and change their structure.

If the starch has not completely swollen, after cooling, the filling won't be stable and may leak water, acquiring a liquid, runny structure.

The dosage of starch may be different depending on:

✔️ density of the product to be thickened: liquid with a high percentage of water or a thicker puree with a high content of natural pectin
✔️ consistency of the filling you want to get

## Tip 100 — How to Correctly Make and Serve a Cake with Buttercream

**Source:** from B3-25

HOW TO CORRECTLY MAKE AND SERVE A CAKE WITH BUTTERCREAM

#marusya_theory_tips

It hasn't been long since a new Napoleon cake recipe set was released, and I've already received lots of similar feedback: "The cake is delicious, but I didn't like the buttercream — it's heavy and too greasy."

❓Can Napoleons and other cakes with buttercream taste like this?
✅ Yes, they can

❓Is there some mistake in the recipe?
❌ No, there isn't

There are 2 reasons why your buttercream can turn out to be heavy and greasy:

1️⃣ You didn't follow the buttercream preparation instructions properly
2️⃣ You served the cake with buttercream inappropriately

So, let's learn how to get the best results.

HOW TO MAKE THE PERFECT BUTTERCREAM:

🔸 make sure your butter is soft
🔸 whip the butter on maximum speed of your mixer
🔸 the whipping time should be from 3–5 to 10–15 minutes, depending on the power of your mixer and the amount of butter (it's impossible to over whip the buttercream)
🔸 if you're making a cake for yourself, I advise you to try using 72% butter instead of 82% — this way you will get a lighter version of the buttercream

➡️At a result, properly whipped butter should increase in volume by 2 times and become lighter. Well whipped butter will allow you to get a light, airy and not greasy buttercream.

HOW SHOULD THE CAKE WITH BUTTERCREAM BE SERVED:

🔸 in no case do we serve the cake immediately from the fridge, as the fat molecules harden at this temperature
🔸 you should take a well stabilized cake out of the fridge and slice it into portions
🔸 keep the slices at room temperature for 10 to 30 minutes, depending on the temperature in the room, so that the buttercream softens
🔸 and only then serve the cake to the client, guest or enjoy the cake yourself

➡️ A properly served cake keeps its shape well, and has a light, airy buttercream

FLAVOR PAIRING. SOUR CHERRY 🍒

🌿 Flavor description

## Tip 102 — Honey: Myths vs. Reality, Part 1: Is Heated Honey Toxic? (HMF Intro)

**Source:** from B3-27

HONEY. MYTHS VS REALITY

part 1

Since without honey a honey cake is no longer a honey cake, I've been working with this product a lot for at least several years.

Therefore, I've faced a wide range of questions regarding this ingredient. The most popular of them are the following:

🌿 Can honey be heated? Doesn't it become toxic after this?

🌿 What are the substitutes for honey in recipes and how to use them correctly?

Honey is a healthy and natural product. It contains glucose, fructose, a small amount of sucrose, flavonoids, microelements and enzymes. It has bactericidal and medicinal properties.

📌 So, the first question and the most common myth at the same time.

❗️ When heated, honey becomes toxic.

Honey won't become toxic, no matter how long you heat it: it does not initially contain any toxic components that are dangerous to humans.

In fact, an organic compound known as 5-hydroxymethylfurfural (HMF) is formed when honey is heated. There is an opinion that this compound is highly toxic, that's why it's dangerous to heat honey.

Let's see how toxic this compound is, and whether we risk to get food poisoning from eating a piece of honey cake or adding honey to hot tea.

HMF is an intermediate product of the breakdown of sugars in an acidic medium, which increases with heating, that is, HMF appears in absolutely all products where sugars have undergone heat treatment in the presence of acid.

Therefore, HMF is found in any baked goods, jams, roasted apples and beets, roasted coffee, soda, and often in quantities ten times greater than in honey.

Why, then, is there GOST (State Standard) that regulates the norm of HMF in honey? - let's figure it out in the continuation of the article 😉

P.S. The photos show a honey cheesecake "Baklava". And yes, there is a lot of honey in it and it was baked at 170-180 °С / 338-356 °F 😅

---

## Tip 103 — Honey: Myths vs. Reality, Part 2: The GOST HMF Standard Explained

**Source:** from B3-28

part 2

So, we already know that an organic compound 5-hydroxymethylfurfural (HMF) is formed when honey is heated and it's neither toxic, nor carcinogenic.

So why are there GOST (State Standard) recommendations according to which the HMF concentration in honey should not exceed 25 mg/kg ?!

The limit for oxy methyl furfural in honey was set not to protect people from a "toxic" product, but to ensure that the product has not undergone overheating during processing.

This indicator is mainly used by honey processors when purchasing lots of honey and helps to determine its age, as well as whether this honey has been overheated during presale processing.

Deviation from the norm characterizes not the danger of heated honey, but rather its uselessness, since enzymes and proteins denaturation takes place much earlier than HMF appears. In this sense, those who like to put honey into hot tea have nothing to worry about, except that honey will lose its medicinal properties.

In any case, after putting a teaspoon of honey into your hot drink, all antioxidants won't disappear at once - they simply won't have time to do it, because your mug is not an autoclave.

Perhaps no one has studied the effect of HMF on human health and its long-term consequences. But taking into account our daily use of a wide variety of products with HMF throughout our lives, this organic compound should not cause serious concerns.

When heated, other organic compounds can form in honey that are harmful to the body (or affect it ambiguously), however, they do not make heated honey noticeably more toxic than almost any other food that you fry or bake.

Moreover, due to the acidic environment (pH 3.5), HMF also appears in raw honey.

In the continuation of the article, we'll analyze why citric acid is sometimes added to the dough for a honey cake and what can be used instead of honey.

---

## Tip 104 — Honey: Myths vs. Reality, Part 3: Honey Substitutes & Sugar-to-Honey Ratio

**Source:** from B3-29

part 3

While I was reparing materials for the articles on my site, I found out that one very interesting topic was not fully disclosed.

Almost a year and a half ago I started writing an article about honey, and in the first two parts I gave a detailed answer to such a tricky question as to whether it's possible to heat honey and whether it becomes harmful after that.

If you are not familiar with this information or want to refresh it in your memory, follow the hashtag #marusya_about_honey.

Today I'd like to analyze another important question:

🌿 What are honey substitutes and how to use them in the recipe?

Allergy prevents some people from consuming this product, that's why we need to find a suitable replacement.

❗️ATTENTION❗️

The allergens present in honey are subjected to heat treatment during baking, after which most of them break down, and in most cases, cease to cause an allergic reaction!

However, if you want to be 100% sure or cook to order for an allergic client, there are 3 main replacement options:
🔸 glucose-fructose or glucose syrup
🔸 trimoline
🔸 artificial honey

You can read more about these ingredients here #marusya_about_sugar .

💎 Approximate ratios of replacing honey with glucose / glucose-fructose syrup / artificial honey — 1:1; trimoline — 1:0.75

Also, sometimes honey is replaced with maple or rice syrup, molasses, agave nectar. As you can see, there are lots of options.

Many people often ask another question:

🌿Can I replace sugar with honey?

When replacing sugar with honey, remember that it's sweeter than sugar. In addition, sugar and honey have different densities, so a 1:1 substitution will make your baked goods not only very sweet, but also sticky, heavy, and overly moist.

💎 Ratios of replacing sugar with honey

For every 100 g sugar indicated in the recipe, use 75 g honey.

---

## Tip 105 — Honey: Myths vs. Reality, Part 4: Adjustments When Substituting & Why Add Citric Acid

**Source:** from B3-30

part 4

#marusya_about_honey

Guys, thank you for being so active under the previous post — I see that the topic of honey is really interesting for you, and therefore I decided not to put off writing the continuation.

🌿 Things to remember when replacing sugar with honey:

🔸 increase baking time as the dough becomes more "heavy" and needs to be baked longer
🔸 lower the temperature by 15 °C, as honey gives the dough a darker color
🔸 reduce the amount of liquid in the recipe; reduce or increase the amount of flour

Summing up, it's definitely possible to replace sugar with honey. However, be sure to stick to the right ratio and, if necessary, make adjustments based on the thickness, consistency and sweetness of the honey used.

🌿Why add citric acid or lemon juice to honey dough?

Adding citric acid is an optional step in the preparation of honey dough and it's just to be on the safe side in case baking soda fails to react properly. If it hasn't activated, you may face several issues:

🔸 smell of baking soda during baking
🔸 bitter aftertaste in cakes
🔸 cake with frosting / berry or fruit filling can be streaked blue — frosting or filling contains acid that reacts with not activated baking soda in the cake layers
🔸 flat, thin crepe-like cake layers

I advise you not to neglect this step, since there is always a possibility that the soda won't react properly for the following reasons:

🔸 different composition of natural honey (more/less acidic, more or less acid in the composition of different types of honey)
🔸 different composition of alkalized cocoa (more/less acidic, more or less acid in the composition of cocoa with varying degrees of alkalization)
🔸 wrong temperature that doesn't ensure a proper reaction between baking soda and acid

💎 Ratio of adding lemon juice to honey dough

1-1.5 g per every 100 g of dough

## Tip 106 — Why Do You Need to Sift the Flour?

**Source:** merged B3-36 + B4-05 (identical content in both)

WHY DO YOU NEED TO SIFT THE FLOUR?

Almost every pastry recipe calls for sifted flour. Have you ever thought about why you should sift it?

Let's start with what we have actually moved away from for a long time, thanks to modern production technologies.

In the past, it was always necessary to sift the flour in order to:

▪️ get rid of weevils and other bugs
⚠️Nowadays contaminated foods should be disposed immediately.⚠️
▪️ get rid of the smallest pieces of stone for grinding grain — quern. The saddle quern consisted of a flat stone bed and a rounded stone to be operated manually against it; later, millstones developed from those hand-operated querns
▪️ get rid of debris — threads from packing bags, cereals from a neighboring box, pieces of paper or plastic bags, etc.

Nowadays, here are a few times you should sift your flour for baking:

▪️ to eliminate and prevent lumps — when your flour has been sitting around for a while, seems to be tightly packed, or has been stored at high humidity, lumps form even in high-quality flour.
▪️ to get a homogeneous, uniform base for the dough — sifted flour mixes better with other ingredients, and the dough behaves more predictably
▪️ to get much lighter and airier flour — it's especially important when baking sponges
▪️ to guarantee you get the right amount when the recipe uses cups instead of grams as a unit of measure (American recipes) — sifted flour will have a larger volume than not sifted

💡 Lifehack from Marusya

If the flour you are using is fairly fresh and does not contain any lumps, in most cases you can skip sifting, and make your flour airier simply by mixing it with a whisk.

## Tip 107 — Honey Cake Layers: Factors That Affect the Intensity of Honey Taste and Aroma

**Source:** merged B3-37 + B4-06 (identical content in both)

HONEY CAKE LAYERS: FACTORS THAT AFFECT THE INTENSITY OF HONEY TASTE AND AROMA

Honey cake layers can have a different intensity of honey taste and aroma: from delicate, subtle honey notes to strong, pronounced ones. What does it depend on?

The very first and seemingly obvious answer that comes to mind is the amount of honey added. But in fact, this is far from the primary factor.

1️⃣ The intensity of the honey taste and aroma in the cake layers depends primarily on the heat treatment the batter undergoes before baking — the more you heat the honey mixture, the more pronounced taste will have the cake layers:

🔸subtle honey taste and aroma can be obtained by bringing the mixture for the honey dough to 60–70 °C over a water bath or in a saucepan
🔸 moderately pronounced taste is obtained by bringing the honey or the honey mixture to a boil (100–116 °C)
🔸 rich, pronounced honey taste and aroma of the cake layers is achieved by boiling the honey mixture or by using the dough made with burnt honey (> 116 °C)

2️⃣ Secondly, the intensity of the honey taste and aroma of the cake layers depends on the type of honey. If you want to get cakes with a light, delicate honey taste, opt for light types of honey, for example, acacia or linden. Buckwheat honey and other dark types should be preferred if you like cakes with a rich, pronounced honey taste.

3️⃣ The amount of honey added to the dough makes less difference to the intensity of honey taste and aroma of cake layers compared to the type of honey and its heat treatment. Therefore, do not be surprised that the amount of honey in honey recipes can vary from 60 to 450 g, depending on the recipe.

P.S. The photos show the Pear–Gorgonzola–Pine Nut honey cake, which drives fans of the sweet fragrant pear and noble blue cheese crazy.

Do you think the dough for this honey cake is cooked over a water bath or is brought to a boil? Those who vote for the water bath, type "1" in the comments, for boiling — type "2".

## Tip 108 — Brown vs. White Eggs — Is There a Difference?

**Source:** merged B3-38 + B4-07 (identical content in both)

BROWN VS. WHITE EGGS — IS THERE A DIFFERENCE?

#marusya_theory_tips

Guys, you probably noticed that in some countries in supermarkets the prices for brown and white eggs differ? Is there any reason for that? Or is it just a marketing ploy?

Let's figure it out. But first things first.

Is there really a difference between brown and white eggs?

✔️ Yes — in color

Eggs come in many colors, depending on the breed of the hen. Chickens have different pigment genes that determine the color of the egg shell. Generally speaking, white shell eggs come from hens with white feathers and white earlobes, while brown shell eggs are produced by hens with brown feathers and red earlobes.
📍some chickens have a unique pigment gene and lay multi-colored eggs — light blue, green and even pink

✖️ No — in terms of nutritional value and taste

The color of the eggshell is not indicative of the best quality of the egg. In fact, there is no distinguishable difference between brown and white eggs when it comes to taste and nutritional value.
📍The nutritional value and palatability of an egg depend solely on the diet and environment of the chicken. The food eaten by the hen can directly affect how an egg tastes, which means that pasture-raised hens usually lay eggs that have a richer taste and higher concentration of useful macro- and microelements

❓ Is the higher price for brown eggs a marketing ploy?

✖️No — hens with brown feathers tend to be larger than hens with white feathers, meaning they consume more feed. Therefore, brown eggs need to be sold at a higher price to make up for the extra costs

✔️ Yes — if you have faced an unscrupulous dealer speculating on their more "natural" appearance

---

## Tip 110 — 8 Types of Butter and How to Use Them — Part 2 (Cultured, European-style, Whipped, Brown Butter)

**Source:** from B4-09

8 TYPES OF BUTTER AND HOW TO USE THEM

start by tag #marusya_about_butter

Well, the start of a new week! Wish you all the best and a productive Monday!

Today I've prepared a continuation about the types of butter. Learn, save it to your bookmarks and don't forget to leave your ❤️ and any comment under the post — I will be very pleased!

So, we have already talked about sweet cream, salted, compound and ghee butter. There are 4 more types left.

🧈Cultured butter

This is the secret ingredient in the best bakery products! This type of butter is obtained by adding pure cultures of lactic acid bacteria to cream (hence the European name cultured butter) and fermentation of the mixture for at least 24 hours. In the process of the fermentation we obtain a thick product with a pronounced cultured milk aroma that enhances the flavor of dishes with the addition of butter, such as pancakes, pies or mashed potatoes.

🧈European-style butter

European butter can boast about its high fat content of 82% to 90%. The high fat content with longer-lasting whipping and live cultures gives European butter a bright yellow color and rich flavor that is instantly transferred to bakery products.

🧈Whipped butter

This fresh look at a traditional butter involves adding air to it to create an easy-to-butter ingredient. Using a hand or planetary mixer make creamy whipped butter that keeps its smooth texture even after cooling. You can also add milk for a softer consistency. Put whipped butter on a crispy piece of baguette and enjoy a restaurant-worthy snack!

🧈Brown butter (beurre noisette)

Brown butter is an ultra-luxury ingredient that adds a nice nutty flavor to everything from bread and cake to Brussels sprouts. Heating of diced sweet cream butter until the milk solids begin to turn brown gives a toasted and delicious butter that is incredibly fragrant in both liquid and solid form.

**Notes:** This is "part 2" of the butter-types series (covers 4 of 8 types); the other 4 types (sweet cream, salted, ghee, compound) appear in Tip 112 below, published as a separate post under an identical title.

## Tip 112 — 8 Types of Butter and How to Use Them — Part 1 (Sweet Cream, Salted, Ghee, Compound)

**Source:** from B4-12

8 TYPES OF BUTTER AND HOW TO USE THEM

#marusya_about_butter

Any butter is made from cream. But depending on the origin of the cream, the composition of the butter and ways of preparation, there are several varieties that ensure different tastes of meals and serve for different purposes:

🧈Sweet Cream Butter (Unsalted Butter)

It is the most popular variety due to its versatility: it can be used both for frying vegetables, meat, and for making desserts and pastries. It is made only from cream of animal origin.

🧈Salted Butter

Unsalted butter is all cream, while the salted variety has some salt added. Salt acts as a preservative, so this variety of butter lasts longer than the unsalted one. Most often, it is used for spreading on bread, topping veggies and pasta.

🧈Ghee butter

Gee is nothing more than the pure butterfat! It is cooked over low heat to remove the water molecules until the milk solids brown lightly, creating a slightly nutty, caramelized aroma. Ghee butter is shelf-stable and has a high smoke point. It is most commonly used for sautéing vegetables, meat, but it's also used in baked goods thanks to its rich flavor.

🧈 Compound butter

This is a special variety of butter that has been softened to a pliable state, and then has herbs, spices, and/or sweeteners (fruit or berry purees) added to it. Usually chilled and then sliced, compound butter can add herby richness to a steak, enhance the flavor of a baked good, or add creaminess to a pasta sauce.

**Notes:** This is the introductory/first post of the "8 types of butter" series (despite appearing textually after Tip 110 in the source, which is explicitly its sequel — "we have already talked about sweet cream, salted, compound and ghee butter"). Kept as a separate tip from Tip 110 since these are two distinct source posts sharing one identical title — a source ordering quirk, not a duplication.

## Tip 113 — Alt. Pastry Chef's Notes — Varieties of Salt: Table Salt [Part 1]

**Source:** from B4-13

ALT. PASTRY CHEF'S NOTES
part 1

I'd like to thank everyone who took part in yesterday's charity fundraising in support of Ukraine. You are amazing! It was very nice to see how many people are ready to help those who are now in a difficult situation.

If you have a desire to join the fundraising campaign, follow the link in the profile header — it will be active until the end of the day, after which the fundraise will be closed, and the proceeds will be transferred to humanitarian aid to Ukrainians.

So, today we continue to analyze the topic of salt.

Earlier, we talked about why add salt to desserts — #marusya_about_salt. Having understood that salt performs many important functions, from enhancing flavor to extending the shelf life of desserts, the question arises: how much salt to add?

First of all, the amount of salt added to a dessert will depend on the variety of salt you are using.

VARIETIES OF SALT

There are 4 types of food salt:

▫️table salt
▫️rock salt
▫️sea salt
▫️lake salt

They differ from each other in the methods of harvesting, chemical composition — and, accordingly, taste, as well as varying degrees of salinity, depending on the content of sodium chloride in the composition.

▪️TABLE SALT

The most commonly used variety of salt. Most table salt is mined, then put into water, purified of other trace minerals and re-dehydrated (evaporated). The content of NaCl is no less than 97% on a dry matter basis.

There are different grades of table salt:

🔸 second
🔸 first
🔸 top
🔸 "Extra"

The main feature of "Extra" grade is that it is fine-grained (almost like a powder) and often comes in "iodized" form which means it includes a small amount of potassium iodide or sodium iodide. The addition of iodine does not change the taste of the salt itself, but when heated above 100 °C, iodized salt decomposes and gives a dish a metallic aftertaste.

"Extra" grade salt has a distinctive taste. Such salt is convenient to use in food production: its high degree of salinity and low cost reduce the cost of purchasing and storing the product.

**Notes:** This "ALT. PASTRY CHEF'S NOTES / part 1" title is unique to this post, but the body content is about salt varieties, matching the surrounding "part 2"/"part 3" salt posts (Tips 114–115). This is a different sub-series from "Why Add Salt to Desserts?" (Tips 117, 118, 120) even though both use hashtag #marusya_about_salt — this one covers salt TYPES/dosage, the other covers salt FUNCTIONS. **The post opens with a real-world reference to a Ukraine war-relief charity fundraiser** — see consolidated Flagged Anomalies section at the end of this document.

## Tip 114 — Salt Varieties — Rock Salt and Sea Salt (incl. Fleur de Sel) [Part 2]

**Source:** from B4-14

part 2

Judging by the number of comments under yesterday's post, we should definitely create a cupcake recipe book! I'm glad you're so excited about this idea. The team and I will make sure to bring it to life. As soon as the recipe book is available on the site, I'll notify you in a post and story.

Today, as promised, I will continue talking about food salt.

#marusya_about_salt

▪️ROCK SALT

It is considered natural: it is obtained by mining, then ground and it's ready to be used. It has a less intense taste than that of table salt.

▪️SEA SALT

A type of salt of natural (marine) origin, extracted from seawater through the use of a vacuum evaporation process after which it is sun and wind dried and then distilled into an edible, solid form. The purity of sea salt depends on the composition and purity of the sea water from which it is harvested.

Sea salt not only enhances the taste of foods, but can also add different flavors depending on the additives in its composition. It is much less salty than table or rock salt, so when cooking, you need much more of it to make the dish salty.

▫️Fleur de Sel ("flower of salt" in French)
The most expensive among sea salts. It has an exceptional taste and is able to give dishes a special delicacy and sophistication. This salt has snowflake-like crystals which add a crunchy texture to dishes.

▪️LAKE SALT

It is extracted from the bottom of salt lakes, where it settles naturally. Then it undergoes purification processes, after which the salt crystals are converted into food salt. Lake salt has a low sodium chloride content, so it is considered less salty.

Classification of salt on the basis of salinity levels from higher to lower:

1. Table salt "Extra" grade
2. Table salt of the first, second and top grades
3. Rock salt
4. Sea salt
5. Lake salt

In all my recipes I use rock salt. Therefore, if you use any other type of salt, make sure to reduce / increase its amount based on the degree of salinity.

## Tip 115 — How Much Salt to Add, and Which Salt to Use in Pastry [Part 3 and last, salt-varieties sub-series]

**Source:** from B4-15

part 3 and last

Today we will figure out how much salt should be added to desserts and pastries, and we will also talk about what kind of salt is used in confectionery.

How much salt to add? This usually depends on:

◾️ variety of salt
We have already figured out that, first and foremost, the amount of salt added to dessert depends on the type of salt you use, since each of them has a different degree of salinity. You can read about it in detail by following the hashtag #marusya_about_salt

◾️ goal you want to achieve
If you want just to enhance the taste of the dessert, then you will need less salt than to eliminate excessive sweetness, for example.

◾️ your taste preferences
Depending on taste buds, a dessert with the same amount of salt will be moderately salty for some, and slightly over-salted for others.

✅ Basic ratios of salt to flour and sugar:
1/4 tsp. salt per cup (130 g) flour
1/4 tsp. salt per cup (180 g) sugar

If the dessert you are making contains both flour and sugar, then first use salt in ratio only to the ingredient that weighs more, check the result, and then add more salt if necessary.

⚠️Which variety of salt to use in pastry?
When choosing the type of salt to add, think of the goal you want to achieve:

◾️ if you need salt to decorate your dessert, then opt for Fleur de Sel — it has snowflake-like crystals and ennobles the dessert with its crispy texture. Other varieties of sea salt that have a crystalline structure are also suitable for decoration.

◾️ to enhance the taste of the dessert, I advise you to use rock or sea salt. They dissolve well in the dough and don't add bitterness, which is typical of iodized and table salt.

◾️ if you use salt as a bitterness neutralizer, why add an extra bitter taste along with table salt?

**Source:** from B4-16

FLAVOR PAIRING. BILBERRY

#marusya_flavor_pairing

Have you been missing the food pairing posts?

Ready, steady, go! It will be interesting, as today we're going to scan the BILBERRY🔍

🌿 Flavor description
🔸 mild
🔸 top floral notes
🔸 slight sourness of the skin
🔸 creamy and fruity aftertaste

Bilberries do not have a strong taste — it is quite neutral and often the berries have no taste at all. Although their skin is thin, with a slight sourness.

BUT!

The faint taste of bilberries can be revealed with the help of heat treatment — then they shine and add to the flavor profile of the dessert!

🌿 Aroma profile
🔸 floral rose aroma
🔸 spicy vanilla notes

🔻 dairy products — mascarpone, ricotta and cream cheese

## Tip 117 — Why Add Salt to Desserts? — Functions 1-4 [Part 1]

**Source:** from B4-17

WHY ADD SALT TO DESSERTS?
part 1

When I began to study this question, it immediately became clear that for this topic I'll need more than one post. You may wonder what salt has to do with dessert. Let's have a look.

Firstly, in some cases it's really impossible to make a dessert without salt. Secondly, without it, most of sweet pastries become insipid.

Let's figure it out.

FUNCTIONS OF SALT IN BAKING AND MAKING DESSERTS

1. Tightens the gluten structure

Salt has a binding or strengthening effect on gluten — this allows the dough to rise and expand during baking. It's indispensable, for example, when baking bread, pastries, croissants or sponges.

📍It's impossible to get a tall sponge without adding salt to the batter. Salted batter will turn out more voluminous and stronger than unsalted.

2. Enhances the taste

Salt brings out the characteristic taste of pastry it is used in. When used in moderate amounts, it does not make the product taste salty, but simply enhances its best qualities. It turns insipid two-dimensional tastes into rich, three-dimensional ones.

Such an effect is achieved thanks to the right combination of different tastes. For example, when salt is added, sweet flavors become less sweet and more complex, while sour flavors become less prominent.

3. Neutralize bitterness

Salt neutralizes bitterness, so any bitter foods (e.g. coffee, dark chocolate, grapefruit) become more appetizing and tasty if you add a little salt.

📍 Grapefruit remains slightly bitter both fresh and baked even after removing the albedo (the whitish, spongy inner part of the rind of a citrus fruit). But salting the pulp helps to get rid of this specific aftertaste.

4. Stabilizes the egg white foam

It's thanks to the addition of that tiny pinch of salt that the egg whites, when whipped, start to increase in volume and become lush. Adding salt to your meringue will ensure that all of the protein chains have safely bonded together.

A similar effect can be obtained by adding acid (e.g. lemon juice).

**Notes:** First installment of the salt-FUNCTIONS sub-series (distinct from the salt-VARIETIES sub-series, Tips 113–115), sharing hashtag #marusya_about_salt. Covers functions 1–4 of 8 total; functions 5–8 appear in Tip 120.

## Tip 119 — Infusion — Hot Method and Its Secrets [Part 3 of infusion series]

**Source:** from B4-19

Continuing to delve into the topic of infusion, today we take into account the hot method, that I use quite often when developing recipes.

HOT METHOD

Many of you, probably, have guessed that the hot method involves heat treatment. You're on the right track. Hot infusion consists in bringing the liquid together with spices, herbs or other flavorings to a boil, followed by infusion for 30-60 minutes.

The infused liquid can be cream, milk or berry puree. For example, you can infuse the cream with dry lavender, as I did in Blueberry-Lavender Cheesecake (photo 3-6), or you can give a mouthwatering coffee aroma by hot infusion the cream with ground cofee (Coffee-Baileys Cheesecake, photo 7-8).

Everything is very simple, but there are secrets that I will gladly share with you.

HOT METHOD SECRETS

💎 The infused liquid must be heated over medium or low heat so that the heating occurs gradually.
💎 In the infusion process, I advise you to use as few dishes as possible to prepare ingredients. For example, it is better to remove the zest immediately into cream. Don't remove the zest on the board, and then transfer it to cream from the board, as all spices, herbs and citrus fruits contain a large amount of essential oils which evaporate easily.
💎 After boiling, you must immediately remove the saucepan from the heat - at too high temperatures, flavors can release substances that will negatively affect the flavor and aroma.
💎 After heating, it is better to cover the liquid with a cling film in contact. If you cover it with a lid, excess moisture from evaporation will form on it, which will get into the liquid. Furthermore, skin which you will need to remove will be formed.
💎 After infusion, don't forget to strain it through a sieve or cheesecloth.
💎 The infused liquid can be stored in the refrigerator after complete cooling during the shelf life of the main product. Keep in mind that the resulting flavor will evaporate with each day of storage!

Did you learn something new for yourself today? If yes, let me know in the comments ⬇️

**Notes:** Clearly labeled "part 3" (per the preceding fragment, Tip 118) of the INFUSION series, mislabeled/misplaced right after a "salt part 2" intro in the raw text — a likely source-document ordering quirk. This is INFUSION series part 3; parts 1 and 2 appear later at Tips 148–149, and part 4 appears at Tip 121. This content substantially overlaps with Tip 001's "HOT METHOD" section (from Batch 1), which covers the same hot-infusion technique nearly verbatim — kept as a separate tip since it is an independent posting captured directly from Batch 4's source range, per the instruction not to consult or merge against site data or earlier per-batch numbering beyond the seams being reconciled in this task.

## Tip 120 — Why Add Salt to Desserts — Functions 5-8

**Source:** from B4-20

5. Balances sweetness

Salt helps to balance the taste of products with a pronounced sweetness, such as caramel, chocolate or meringue. Such products are usually treacly, and it's almost impossible to add other flavor accents to them, even if you think they will work well in your pastry. Salt added in low concentrations balances the sweetness and adds depth to other flavors, so that our taste buds perceive them as moderately sweet, although the amount of sugar remains unchanged.

6. Helps to release juices

Salt changes the balance of liquid in foods. It makes juicy berries and fruits release juices, leaving the fibrous material behind. If you are making a fruit dessert and you need to get the maximum juice, just add a pinch of salt to your fruits.

7. Increases the shelf life

Salt is a natural preservative. It dehydrates harmful microorganisms. Due to dehydration, bacteria become inactive, their growth stops, the metabolism of their cells becomes impossible, which is why they die. Different types of microorganisms become harmless at different salt concentrations. But even in a small amount, salt increases the shelf life of pastry.

8. Helps to maintain the recommended daily sodium intake

Our body needs a certain amount of salt (NaCl) to function properly, so it's natural that we tend to like the taste of salt. This does not mean that we should eat tons of salt a day, but using it in reasonable amounts in cooking and making desserts will help us stay healthy and enjoy food.

**Notes:** Direct continuation of the salt-functions series (functions 1-4 in Tip 117), picking back up right after the misplaced infusion "part 3" content (Tip 119) with no new heading — confirms the salt part-2 body was physically separated from its intro in the source document (see Tip 118's note).

## Tip 121 — Infusion — Decoction and Vacuuming [Part 4, the last one]

**Source:** from B4-21

INFUSION: WHAT, BY WHAT AND HOW

#marusya_infusion

part 4 (the last one)

We have already talked with you about the cold and hot method of infusion. Today I will tell you about decoction and vacuuming.

DECOCTION

This method, of course, is very similar to hot method — the liquid is also subjected to heat treatment. However, there is one significant difference — with hot method, we only heat the liquid or bring it to a boil, but as for decoction, we boil for several minutes or even cook for up to 60 minutes to get dense aromatic decoctions.
Infusions are steeped and decoctions are simmered.

If we talk about confectionery, this method is often used in the preparation of flavored sugar syrups for coating muffins or, for example, soaking sponge cake.

For the preparation of fillings for honey cakes "Fig-Parmesan-Gorgonzola-Red Wine" and "Quince-Goat Cheese-Medium Sweet White Wine" we also use this method — juicy fleshy figs and fragrant quince are stewed in wine from 25 to 60 minutes until the liquid has almost completely evaporated. Due to the prolonged exposure to high temperature, we get incredibly tasty, rich and fragrant fillings!

VACUUMING

This is a method for infusion of solid ingredients (bananas, mangoes, coconut, pineapple, apples, etc.), which are subsequently used as whole or cut.

Thanks to vacuuming, we can give the main product a lot of flavors!

There are several ways:

1️⃣ Take a dense main ingredient and pour it with juice of the infusing one, then leave to infuse in the refrigerator.
2️⃣ Mix the main and infusing ingredients — you can even wrap apple pieces with lemon slices or thin mango strips, sprinkled with cinnamon or other spices. After that, it is necessary to place them in a bag for vacuuming and pump out the air with a vacuum cleaner.

As a result, you can get an orange-flavored apple, for example, or a lemon-flavored banana.

## Tip 122 — Everything You Need to Know About Agar — Part 1 (Overview, Quality, Gel Strength, vs. Gelatin)

**Source:** from B4-22

EVERYTHING YOU NEED TO KNOW ABOUT AGAR
part 1

In previous posts, we have already talked with you about gelatin - #marusya_about_gelatin and pectin - #marusya_about_pectin. Today I suggest analyzing the next, no less interesting thickener - agar, aka agar-agar, aka seaweed food thickener.

Agar means "seaweed" in Malaysian. Its gelling properties are said to be accidentally discovered in Japan in the 17th century by Mino Tarōzaemon. He found out that the chilled seaweed soup had a jelly-like consistency the next day.

Agar is extracted from red and brown algae, which undergo several stages of rinsing, drying and grinding.

Nowadays, this natural thickener is widely used in confectionery art. It's used to make zephyr, marmalade, fruit pastes (pastila), sweets, jelly and mousse desserts.

Agar can be classified by its quality and gel strength.

🌿Quality

🔸 High grade agar - has a white or light yellow color, although it can have a slightly grayish tint
🔸 First grade agar - varies from yellow to dark yellow color

🌿Gel strength

🔸 600-1200 g/cm2

The higher the number it has, the thicker gel can be obtained. The greater the strength of agar is, the less amount of it is needed to gel the mixture.

📎 You can check out the table that shows how to convert between different amounts of agar-agar depending on the gel strength in the carousel.

Agar is often referred to as a natural substitute for gelatin. However, these two thickeners have a number of differences.

AGAR VS GELATIN

1️⃣ Unlike gelatin, agar does not impart any taste, smell, or color to desserts.
2️⃣ In terms of gelling properties, agar is 10 times superior to gelatin
3️⃣ Unlike gelatin, agar activates in an acidic environment. Gelatin is not a good choice when it comes to preparing, for example, desserts with pineapple or kiwi - they contain enzymes that destroy proteins (87-92% of the composition of gelatin). But agar-agar perfectly gels such mixtures.
4️⃣ Unlike gelatin, agar is thermoreversible - it can be heated and frozen as many times as you like.

---

## Tip 123 — Agar — Rules of Working With Agar [Part 2]

**Source:** from B4-23

part 2

Please support this useful post with your like ❤️ ー this is extremely important for us now!

For those interested in the answer to the question from the previous post about using agar to make the orange confit for the "Orange-Dark Chocolate" honey cake: I chose it because the filling made with gelatin began to leak out after defrosting.

This is due to the fact that ice crystals formed in the orange confit during freezing, which broke the texture of the jelly. As a result, the filling lost its original properties and began to leak out after defrosting.

Will gelatin-based filling always leak out after defrosting? No, it won't. Moreover, sometimes after defrosting, a filling made with agar or pectin may also leak out. What does it depend on? First of all, it depends on the amount of unbound water in the composition, but this should be discussed in another post.

Today we'll continue examining the agar. So, here are the RULES OF WORKING WITH AGAR:

1️⃣ Agar does not dissolve in cold water ー it should be brought to 95-100 °C to completely dissolve, at this temperature it forms a viscous and transparent solution, which when cooled to 35-40 °C, turns into a thick and pure gel.

2️⃣ Agar does not require pre-soaking in water or mixing with sugar. In order to introduce agar into the mixture, it is enough just to add it in there! The mixture can be hot, warm or cold ー it does not matter.

3️⃣ Agar is a thermoreversible thickener. You can heat and freeze fillings made with it as many times as you like ー it won't lose its gelling properties.

4️⃣ One of the main advantages of agar over gelatin is the ability to activate in an acidic environment. The advantage over pectin is that agar gels well even in the absence of sugar in the mixture.

5️⃣ The thicker the desired consistency of the gel you want to obtain is, the stronger agar should be used and / or the more agar you need.

---

## Tip 124 — Agar — How to Calculate the Required Amount (Dosage by Acidity and Texture) [Part 3]

**Source:** from B4-24

part 3

Today we'll talk about how to correctly calculate the required amount of agar and what it depends on.

If you are making a dessert with agar using a recipe from an eminent chef, in order to get a stable result, be sure to pay attention to:

🔸 amount of agar — agar is a very strong thickener (10 times stronger than gelatin), so even a slight deviation of 0.1-0.2 g can change the texture of the gel
🔸 strength of agar — if the recipe says "5 g of agar 1200", to get a dessert "like from a chef" you need to either use agar of the same strength or use the one that is available and convert between its strength.

⬆️ you can find the table that shows how to convert between different amounts of agar-agar depending on the gel strength in the 1st post from the series #marusya_about_agar.

🔸 agar brand (if specified) — as a rule, despite the same strength, agar of different brands can slightly differ in the opacity / transparency of the gel obtained and gelling properties, so it is best to stick to a brand recommended by chef.

If you use a recipe from the Internet, where there's no information about the strength of the agar used, or you want to create a dessert yourself, take into account the following tips:

🌿 Recommended dosage

2-4 g of agar per 150-180 ml of liquid

The exact amount depends on:

🔺 liquid being gelled and its acidity

The higher the acidity of the liquid is, the more agar must be added. This is due to the fact that acid lowers the gelling properties of thickeners, including agar. For example, to gel 100 ml of a neutral liquid (e.g. water, milk) you'll need 0.9-1 g of agar, while for 100 ml of an acidic liquid (e.g. juice) — 1.3-1.5 g of agar.

🔺 desired texture

runny texture — 0.8 g / 500 ml
soft texture — 1.5 g / 500 ml
thick texture — 5 g / 500 ml
very thick texture — 7 g / 500 ml

If this post was useful for you, leave any comment or emoticon under it — I'll be very pleased ⬇️
#marusya_about_agar

---

## Tip 125 — Agar — Storage and Substitutes (Gelatin, Pectin, Corn Starch) [Part 4]

**Source:** from B4-25

part 4

Today will be the final part of a series of articles about agar. I'll tell you about the terms and conditions of storage, as well as possible agar substitutes.

🌿 TERMS AND CONDITIONS OF STORAGE

🔸Agar

The shelf life of agar varies from 2 to 3 years since the date of packaging. It is indicated on the packaging and may change depending on the formula and production technology of agar by a specific manufacturer.

Agar should be stored in a cool, dry place, protected from sunlight, in airtight packaging.

🔸 Products with agar

1️⃣ Agar-based confectionery products are stored about 24 to 36 hours at a temperature between +4 to -8°C
2️⃣ Fruit mixtures — up to 7 days in the refrigerator
3️⃣ Jam and other preservation — up to 1 month in a cool, dry place, protected from sunlight

🌿 AGAR SUBSTITUTES

🔸 Gelatin
Gelatin has a lower density after cooling than agar, so it is suitable for making more delicate desserts. If you want to get a dense and smooth texture, it is better to use agar.

In general, these thickeners can be considered interchangeable, but it should be kept in mind that the gelling ability of agar is much higher than that of gelatin.

💎 Substitute: 1 tsp. agar = 2-3 tsp. gelatin

ℹ️ It's not suitable for vegetarians, as gelatin is made from the bones and tendons of animals

🔸 Pectin

Pectin is the most beneficial and equivalent substitute for agar, but there are exceptions. Both products have the vegetal nature and the only difference is that pectin contains carbohydrates, and its calorie content is higher than that of agar.

A prerequisite for the "activation" of pectin is the presence of sugar. Accordingly, substitute agar for pectin in the making of sugar-free desserts is impossible.

💎 Substitute: 1 tsp. agar = 2-3 tsp. pectin

🔸 Corn Starch

Agar-agar can be substituted for starch. However, starch makes the filling opaque, when dissolved, that significantly narrows the range of its possibilities.

💎 Substitute: 1 tsp. agar = 2 tsp. starch

## Tip 126 — Flavor Pairing: Lemon

**Source:** from B4-26

FLAVOR PAIRING. LEMON 🍋

In the pastry art, lemon can be considered a versatile citrus fruit that not only serves as the basis for creating desserts with a characteristic lemon sourness, but also for balancing sweet and savory tastes.

Let's have a look at the flavor profile of LEMON

🌿 Flavor description

🔸 sour
🔸 pure, refreshing flavor dominated by citric acid

We feel the pronounced sourness of lemon precisely thanks to the lemon juice. But the rich bouquet of aromas that a lemon possesses is revealed when the zest is peeled.

🌿 Aroma profile

🔸 dominant citrus notes
🔸 waxy and oily accents in essential oils and volatile compounds contained in the peel
🔸 notes of rose, lavender and pine
🔸 light grass, camphor, woody, pine tree aroma

Lemon is a natural flavor enhancer. For example, thanks to it, the taste of an orange becomes more expressive. It matches perfectly with:

🔻 other fruits and berries such as orange, grapefruit, raspberry, blackberry, blueberry, mango, peach.

Lemon-blueberry is one of the most interesting combinations: the floral notes of blueberries are masked by its sour juice, and the acidity of the lemon doesn't add to its floral aroma. But! When enough sugar or honey is added, both flavors reveal and create an incredible, aromatic combination in desserts.

🔻 alcoholic drinks - red wine, gin, rum and orange liqueur. It stands out the most when paired with the Italian Limoncello liqueur.

🔻 nuts - pecans, cashews, almonds, pistachios and walnuts

🔻 herbs and spices - basil, cayenne pepper, poppy seeds, mint, thyme, rosemary

Lemon-ginger is one of the most successful and harmonious flavor combinations. This pair has very similar flavor profiles. And only your sense of smell will help you find the subtle differences between lemon flavor of ginger and ginger flavor of lemon.

That is why the Lemon-Ginger honey cake recipe gained such popularity. You can find this free recipe by following the hashtag #marusya_recipe

## Tip 127 — Flavor Pairing: Mango

#marusya_flavor_pairing

Fresh lime juice squeezed over a ripe mango is one of the world's greatest flavor combinations. The distinct zesty sour flavor of lime submits to the taste of mango with its floral notes.

## Tip 128 — Brown Butter (Beurre Noisette) — 6 Tips for the Perfect Result

**Source:** from B4-28

BROWN BUTTER OR BEURRE NOISETTE

#marusya_theory_tips

In the comments under yesterday's post, I noticed that for some people the technique of making brown butter was new and it raised questions and doubts.

Therefore, today I decided to share with you my tips and tricks in order to dispel your doubts and inspire you to make incredibly delicious blackberry, bilberry and star anise cupcakes while the berry season is in full swing.

Brown or browned butter (fr. beurre noisette) are all different names for the same product obtained by heating regular butter - it melts, separates into butterfat and milk solids, the latter sink to the bottom of the pan and turn a deep brown color.

The browning (caramelization) of butter is the result of the milk proteins and sugars going through the Maillard reaction upon heating to 130 °C.

The recipe for brown butter can be found in the previous post. But I'd like to give you some tips so that everything goes perfectly, and you don't get nervous.

6 TIPS FOR GETTING THE PERFECT BEURRE NOISETTE

1️⃣ Use a thick-bottomed saucepan to ensure even heating of the butter.

2️⃣ The saucepan should be of a light color - it's more difficult to notice the change in the color of the butter against a dark background, and it can easily burn.

3️⃣ To get the perfect brown butter, I advise you to heat no more than 200 g of butter at a time in a small saucepan (d = 14-16 cm). If you need a larger amount, use a saucepan d = 22-26 cm.

4️⃣ When the butter gets frothy, start to constantly stir it with a whisk so that the milk solids do not burn.

5️⃣ After bringing the butter to 128 °C, remove the saucepan from the heat, while continuing to stir - within 30-40 seconds the residual heat of the saucepan will bring the butter to the desired slightly brownish color (it will heat up to 130-138 °C).

6️⃣ Immediately after this, pour the butter into a separate container to stop the cooking process. Otherwise, the temperature will continue to rise, and as soon as it exceeds 140 °C, the butter will burn.

## Tip 129 — Infusion: The Ins and Outs — What Can Be Infused [Part 1]

**Source:** from B4-29

NFUSION. THE INS AND OUTS

#marusya_infusion
part 1

At first glance, the topic is very simple and there is nothing to talk about. But! You will be surprised when you find out that even when I was giving a master class at a large-scale production, the guys were perplexed by my advice to change the method of infusion to cold.

In fact, aroma and taste are inextricably linked, and they should be considered as a whole.

You can read more about tastes and how they are created by the hashtag #marusya_flavours

Flavoring is an amazing thing that you encounter when diving into the world of confectionery. After baking ordinary shortbread cookies and charlotte, you get a bit bored and you feel a desire to play with new or unusual flavor combinations, add a subtle aroma.

The right aroma and taste of a dessert can calm a person or, on the contrary, energize, make them happy or make them think a little. For example, vanilla - brings warmth and tranquility, cinnamon - reminds of a warm autumn. Lemongrass and lavender have soothing effect.

In order to transfer the flavor of one ingredient to another, they must be combined under suitable conditions:

🔸 mixed or blended together
🔸 chilled together
🔸 heated together
🔸 boiled together

WHAT CAN ONE INFUSE?

In mousses:

🔺 liquid base (cream/milk)
🔺 syrup for cooking the egg whites (for Italian meringue-based mousse)
🔺 mousses can also be flavored with pastes

📍only thick pastes which don't change, but rather improve the texture of the dessert, are used: nut, praline and some fruit pastes

In ganaches:

🔺 cream
🔺 fruit puree
🔺 water

In berry fillings:

🔺 berries or berry puree can undergo cold or vacuum infusion process

In sponges and other desserts that are baked at high temperatures:

🔺 the flavoring is added directly to the composition of the product

## Tip 130 — Infusion — Popular Flavorings and the Cold Method [Part 2]

**Source:** from B4-30

part 2

In the previous part, we discussed with you what can be infused. Today I'll tell you about the most popular flavorings.

Most likely, the first thing that comes to mind is flavors. Both natural and artificial flavors will help to improve or change the taste of a dessert. This method is good for 3 reasons:

1️⃣ it doesn't require much time and effort - add a drop, mix and it's done!
2️⃣ it's helpful when you don't want to add extra ingredients to the texture in order not to spoil it
3️⃣ it's convenient if you need an unusual flavor (bubble gum, California strawberry, Tiramisu)

Apart from flavors, the following ingredients can be used to create an infusion:

🔸 Vanilla (pod/paste/extract)
🔸 Spices and seasoning (pepper, cinnamon, cardamom, anise, coriander, star anise, cloves, ginger)
🔸 Herbs and flowers (tarragon, basil, dill, thyme, rosemary, lavender)
🔸 Tea (black, matcha, red, green)
🔸 Fruit pastes
🔸 Citrus zest
🔸 Freeze-dried berries
🔸 Tonka beans
🔸 Coffee beans
🔸 Alcoholic drinks (rum, brandy, liqueurs)
🔸 Syrups
🔸 Natural essential extracts and oils

And this is not all!

Now let's proceed to the most interesting part - infusion methods. They are:

🔺 Cold
🔺 Hot
🔺 Decoction
🔺 Vacuum

COLD METHOD

Cold method consists in adding a certain flavor (spices, herbs, zest, etc.) to a liquid (cream, milk, berry puree), and then placing it in the fridge for at least 12 hours.

This is the longest method of infusion - the aromatization of the liquid occurs slowly and not as intensively as with the hot method.
It will take 3-5 days to almost completely extract flavor from fresh herbs into a liquid, and about 20 days for dry ones. In addition, a MORE flavoring component will be required.

But if you want to get an exquisite, sophisticated flavor and avoid heat treatment, this method will be the best option.

## Tip 132 — Pectin NH Nappage — Q&A

**Source:** from B4-32

Pectin NH Nappage

There are plenty of articles about pectins, but this topic doesn't seem to lose its relevance and the number of questions regarding this thickener and how to work with it is still huge:

❓ Why do I prefer pectin when I need to stabilize any berry or fruit filling?

✅ because pectin gives the most natural texture and, at the same time, it doesn't affect the taste, smell and color of the finished product

❓Why do I use pectin NH Nappage and not any other pectin, what's special about it?

✅ pectin NH Nappage is used for the preparation of a neutral gel and glazes, as it allows to obtain a more transparent gel
✅ it's thermoreversible - it can be reheated and frozen as many times as you need
✅ it doesn't require a lot of sugar and / or acid in the mixture
✅ thanks to the presence of additional stabilizers in the composition, it perfectly stabilizes juices

❓ What is the composition of pectin NH Nappage, apart from E440 pectin itself?
✅ diphosphate E450 - stabilizer, acidity regulator, emulsifier, leavening agent, water-retaining agent
✅ calcium phosphate E341 - stabilizer, acidity regulator, leavening agent, color fixer
✅ dextrose - pure glucose - a monosaccharide, one hundred percent carbohydrate

❓Which brands of pectin NH Nappage do I prefer?

✅ Spanish Sosa
✅ French Louis Francois

❓How much pectin NH Nappage do you need to perfectly thicken the filling and how to introduce it correctly?

✅ the recommended dose is 0.8-2.0 g per 100 g of the mixture, depending on the content of pectin in the puree, the amount of water and the desired thickness of the filling
✅ pectin should be mixed with sugar in 1:5 ratio and then gradually sprinkled into the warm mixture. While doing it, stir the mixture constantly to avoid lumps
✅ it fully dissolves within 15 minutes

❓Are there cases of pectin NH Nappage not setting the mixture?

✅ yes, this pectin practically doesn't activate in mixtures containing alcohol

Which thickener do you prefer to use and why?
