## Tip 133 — Crème Anglaise From A to Z — Composition and Basic Rules [Part 1]

**Source:** from B4-33

CRÈME ANGLAISE FROM A TO Z
As it turned out, not everyone can properly make the crème anglaise (French for "English cream") or custard. There's nothing complicated about its preparation, but at the same time, to obtain a stable result, you need to follow a number of rules, as well as understand the processes that occur during its preparation.

So in order to avoid difficulties in working with the custard, you'll need to know some tips and tricks which I'm going to share with you today.

Let's start with the concept of crème anglaise and custard.

Custard is a combination of water, fats and sugars which becomes possible thanks to an emulsifying agent.

Under normal conditions, fats and water don't mix, but with the presence of an emulsifier it becomes possible.

Crème anglaise is a type of custard in which the emulsifying agent is the lecithin present in egg yolk.

Crème anglaise can be used as a standalone product, as well as a sauce (French version) or a base for making mousses, ice creams and creams (English version).

✅ Composition of crème anglaise:

🔸 70% - liquid (milk / milk + cream 1:1 / milk + fruit puree 1:1)
🔸 15% - egg yolks
🔸 15% - sugar

To make crème anglaise, mix egg yolks with sugar and boil the milk and/or cream. Pour 1/3 of milk into the yolk-sugar mixture, then pour everything back into a saucepan with the remaining milk. Bring to 82°C and remove from the stove.

Basically, you can use this recipe and get down to cooking. However, there's a huge possibility that something will go wrong.

‼️ To properly make crème anglaise, you should follow a number of rules:

🔸 all ingredients must be fresh, as the enzymes contained in milk catalyze many biochemical processes
🔸 all tools used to cook the custard must be perfectly clean
🔸 use an appropriate tool at each stage of preparation: a whisk - for beating the yolks with sugar, a silicone spatula - for cooking the cream
🔸 stick to the right temperatures - bring the cream to 82-83 °C to pasteurize it and obtain the desired consistency

---

## Tip 134 — Crème Anglaise — Why It Can Curdle at 82°C (Yolk and Sugar Chemistry) [Part 2]

**Source:** from B4-34

part 2

I received an interesting question:

How can one cook crème anglaise to 82°C and not get an "omelette"?

To answer this question, let's analyze the composition and properties of some ingredients that are used to make the custard.

🌿 Yolk

Composition:

🔸 50-54% - water
🔸 30-33% - fats and lipids
🔸 16-17 - proteins (including lecithin)
🔸 1% - carbohydrates and minerals

It is thanks to lecithin that fats form an amalgam (emulsion) with a liquid. That is, an egg is, in fact, a natural emulsion.

‼️ Lecithin must not be heated above 70°C

🌿 Sugar

1️⃣ Sugar is very hygroscopic, that is, it has a high ability to absorb moisture.

I suggest doing a little experiment. Place one yolk in a bowl, add a couple of tbsp of sugar and leave it like that without stirring.

After a while, you'll find that the mixture turned grainy - the sugar absorbed all the liquid from the yolk.

Therefore, when you start making crème anglaise, as soon as you add sugar to the yolks, IMMEDIATELY start to stir the mixture vigorously with a whisk! If during this process you decide to set it aside and answer a phone call, for example, the custard won't be of the right texture anymore - the emulsion will break, which is IMPOSSIBLE TO FIX! If you continue to cook the custard using this mixture, you'll get a lumpy mixture in your saucepan.

Next, we face another interesting task:

❌ As you remember, lecithin, which is found in the yolks, must not be heated above 70°C.

✅ But crème anglaise must be brought to 82-83°C in order to achieve the desired consistency and, most importantly, to pasteurize the product.

Here the 2nd property of sugar comes to the rescue:

2️⃣ Sugar prevents lecithin and proteins from overheating.

By adding sugar to the yolks, we thereby delay the destruction of lecithin, which allows us to bring the crème anglaise to 82°C and not cook it into an "omelette".

📌 Even if you thoroughly stirred the yolks with sugar, there still is a risk of curdling (coagulation) of proteins.

---

## Tip 135 — Crème Anglaise — Correct Temperature Measurement Technique [Part 3]

**Source:** from B4-35

part 3

As promised, today I'll tell you why, when making crème anglaise, you still might cook it into an "omelette" after bringing it to 82 °C, even if you thoroughly whisked the sugar with yolks, protecting the lecithin from overheating.

The reason for this is quite obvious - the custard was brought to a higher temperature.

To measure the temperature correctly, follow guidelines:

🔸 When cooking the custard, stir it with a spatula and not with a whisk to avoid air getting into the custard. When dealing with the aerated mixture it is almost impossible to measure the temperature correctly!

📌 Cooking crème anglaise with a whisk is considered a gross mistake!
❌ Firstly, you compromise the very concept of the custard by letting the air penetrate into it - thus you'll get a mousse, not a custard.
❌ Secondly, you won't be able to accurately measure the temperature of the mousse.

🔸 Stir the custard carefully, making figure-eight motion with spatula - this will ensure uniform heating of the entire mixture and prevent it from scorching.

🔸 Do not use a pyrometer to measure the temperature of the custard - it is designed to read the temperature on the surface! Use a probe thermometer istead, immersing it into the mixture (avoid touching the bottom of the pan!).

🔸 However, if you are cooking on an induction stove, I do not recommend using a probe thermometer to check the temperature of the custard - no matter how hard you try, the device won't read the temperature correctly, because:

❌ the bottom of the pans for induction stoves is metal, the probe is also metal, that is the induction heats it along with the pan - to put it another way, the probe thermometer will read not the temperature of the mixture, but the temperature the metal is heated to.

✅ Solution: If you have an induction stove, use a glass deep fry/candy thermometer

---

## Tip 136 — Crème Anglaise — Complete Cooking Instructions (Step by Step) [Part 4]

**Source:** merged B4-36 + B5-01 (B5's version is the tail-only fragment of the same content, from the neighboring batch's boundary; B4's version is the complete post and is used here)

Part 4
Complete cooking instructions

1️⃣ Whisk the egg yolks and sugar.

📌 Whisk thoroughly and quickly to avoid lumps. You need to ensure a good emulsion so that the yolks don't separate.

2️⃣ Heat the milk.

If you're using pasteurized or UHT milk, bring it to 85°C.

If you're using raw milk, bring it to a boil (100°C) to pasteurize.

📌 When the milk has boiled, a film forms on its surface - these are proteins that have coagulated. To prevent skin formation, add some sugar to the milk.
📌 It is best to use pasteurized dairy products to achieve a balanced taste in the final product.

3️⃣ Temper the egg yolks - gradually pour a third of the hot milk into the yolk mixture in a thin stream and mix well with a silicone spatula.

❌ If you pour the milk too quickly, the yolks can cook, while you only need to heat up the yolk mixture.

4️⃣ Pour in the remaining milk, put the saucepan on low heat and pasteurize the mixture at 82-83°C, stirring continuously with a spatula.

❌ Do not cook it on high heat - the custard may burn!

5️⃣ Remove the custard from the heat and pour it into a wide plastic vessel.

📌 If you used flavors (vanilla pod / zest / herbs), strain the custard first.

6️⃣ Place the vessel in a shock freezer or over an ice bath for quick cooling.

It is vital to quickly cool the custard to room temperature, as it is made from milk and eggs, which spoil quickly.

7️⃣ Cooled custard:

a) if you're going to use crème anglaise as a standalone product or as a sauce, cover it with cling film in contact and place it in the fridge for further stabilization

b) if you're going to use it as a base for frosting - add it to the remaining products (e.g. melted chocolate), cover with cling film in contact and place in the fridge

c) if you're going to use it as a base for crémeux - first add melted gelatin mass, then quickly cool it, cover with cling film and place in the fridge

**Notes:** Source typo "radually" corrected to "gradually" in step 3 (present in both B4 and B5's identical duplicate text) for readability; original spelling preserved as a known source typo.

---

## Tip 137 — 5 Problems When Making Crème Anglaise and How to Solve Them [Part 5, final]

**Source:** merged B4-37 + B5-02 (identical content in both)

5 PROBLEMS WHEN MAKING CRÈME ANGLAISE AND HOW TO SOLVE THEM

This is a final post about crème anglaise, in which I'll tell you what problems you may face in the process of making custard, and how to solve them.

1️⃣ Runny custard

❌ Reason
The custard hasn't been brought to 82-83 °C.

Crème anglaise contains the alpha-amylase enzyme which can break down protein.

If the custard has been brought to less than 74 °C, the alpha-amylase won't get destroyed and will digest the proteins. In other words, the custard will be runny. Such custard can't be fixed.

✅ Solution
If the custard has been brought to a temperature above 75 °C, the product has been pasteurized (alpha-amylase is destroyed), it can be fixed - just add bloomed gelatin in order to make the custard more stable.

2️⃣ Custard has curdled

❌ Reason
The custard was brought to a temperature above 82-83 °C.

✅ Solution
In the future, be sure to measure the temperature correctly following the rules described in the previous post (P3). You can find them by the hashtag #marusya_about_crèmeanglaise

Someone advises blending curdled custard. Thus, you will eliminate the "lump effect", but won't get rid of the omelette taste. Therefore, if your crème anglaise has split, I advise you to remake it so as not to compromise the whole cake.

3️⃣ Custard foams during cooking

❌ Reason
See what's in your hand. I'm sure it's a whisk.

✅ Solution
After beating the yolks with sugar, put the whisk aside and switch to a silicone spatula. This is the appropriate tool to cook the custard with.

4️⃣ There are yolk pieces in custard

❌ Reason
Poorly beaten yolks with sugar

✅ Solution
Remake the custard

I don't advise you to strain it. Yes, of course, you'll get rid of pieces of yolk floating in the custard, but this won't help you to get rid of the yolk taste.

5️⃣ Skin forms on the custard surface

❌ Reason
The custard wasn't covered with cling film or wasn't covered tightly enough.

✅ Solution
Strain the custard or carefully remove the skin. Then cover the crème anglaise with cling film touching the surface.

## Tip 138 — How to Differentiate Vegetable Cream from Dairy?

**Source:** merged B4-38 + B5-03 (identical content in both)

HOW TO DIFFERENTIATE VEGETABLE CREAM FROM DAIRY?

#marusya_theory_tips

The previous post got more than 20 comments, so, as promised, I will tell you how to differentiate vegetable cream (or cream containing a mixture of non-dairy and dairy ingredients) from dairy cream at home.

Unfortunately, sometimes the name of the product or even its composition provided on the packaging is not really helpful in finding out whether it's a dairy, vegetable or mixed imitation cream.

If you want to get quality pastry products with a rich taste, I advise you to carry out a small test before giving preference to a particular brand.

Put a small amount of cream in a saucepan and bring it to a boil.

❌ You're dealing with vegetable / mixed imitation cream which contains a huge amount of thickeners and stabilizers, if after boiling it:

🔸 has become even thicker than it was (sometimes it may look like low-fat sour cream)
🔸 has not changed its original snow-white color

If you observe this with your cream, I can assume that it contains only vegetable fats or, at best, it has a mixed composition of milk and vegetable fats.

✅ You're dealing with dairy cream, if after boiling it:

🔸 has become liquid, the consistency resembles full-fat milk
🔸 has acquired a yellowish tint, characteristic of dairy products (after heating, organic heavy cream acquires a well-defined milky or ivory color)

Similar processes are typical for cream of animal origin. You can trust a brand that has passed such a test.

I can share with you the brands that have passed this test in my kitchen.

The quality brands I trust:

🥛 Mlekovita, 30/36%
🥛 President, 33%
🥛 Elle & Vire, 35%
🥛 Debic, 32%

If you have read the post to the end, leave any emoji in the comments ⬇️ and bookmark the post 📎

P.S. Be sure to test the origin of the cream you use. You'll get valuable experience in a matter of minutes!

---

## Tip 139 — Dairy and Non-Dairy Whipping Cream

**Source:** merged B4-39 + B5-04 (identical content in both)

DAIRY AND NON-DAIRY WHIPPING CREAM

#marusya_theory_tips

My students often ask me which cream is better to use.

First of all, I'd like to say that in my recipes I only use cream of animal origin. I don't work with plant-based creams for a number of reasons:

🔻 they contain a huge amount of stabilizers, flavors, preservatives and other synthetic components

🔻 contain palm or palm kernel oil. During the preparation of vegetable cream, this oil is partially hydrogenated, that is, the liquid emulsion turns into solid fatty acids.

This is done for the sake of extending the expiration date to ❗️12❗️months. But at the same time, during hydrogenation trans fats are formed, which might affect our health:

❌ raise of cholesterol levels, negative effect on the immune and cardiovascular systems

❌ a higher risk of developing diabetes, liver, kidneys, heart and nervous system diseases. Trans fats have also been hypothesized to be carcinogenic.

🔻 non-dairy cream cannot be heated as after that it may have an unpleasant taste. Such creams are usually used cold, which significantly narrows the range of their use in pastry

🔻and the last reason I don't work with vegetable cream is the low fat content

✅ The number one goal that I pursue when developing new recipes is to get the perfectly balanced taste. And these are the FAT MOLECULES that contribute to the FLAVOR! Therefore, I prefer to use dairy products with a high fat content.

It seems that to get the perfect dessert it is enough to pick any dairy cream in the supermarket and that's it. But it's not! Unfortunately, quite often the information provided by manufacturers on packaging cannot be trusted, believe my own experience.

**Notes:** Its closing line sets up Tip 138 as its logical sequel/payoff — but Tip 138 physically appears BEFORE this one in the source, meaning source order is reversed relative to logical reading order. Kept as two separate tips matching the two separate source posts.

---

## Tip 140 — How to Choose the Right Chocolate Fluidity

**Source:** merged B4-40 + B5-05 (identical content in both)

HOW TO CHOOSE THE RIGHT CHOCOLATE FLUIDITY

#marusya_theory_tips

Fluidity depends on the content of cocoa butter in chocolate - the more cocoa butter in its composition, the more fluid it is.

Chocolate with a higher fluidity contains more cocoa butter and when tempered properly, it allows you to create a hard, thin and crunchy chocolate coating with a glossy sheen.

Such chocolate contracts better during cooling, which makes it easier to remove the chocolate shell from the mold.

🌿 Chocolate with a lower fluidity contains less cocoa butter, which produces a less hard, less shiny and thicker chocolate coating.

Determining the fluidity of chocolate is not difficult, as manufacturers indicate it on the packaging with a drop symbol system. The more drops are drawn on the packaging that you hold in your hands, the more fluid the chocolate is in front of you.

There is a wide range of fluidity levels marked with 1-5 drops:

🔸 1 drop
✅ ideal for mousse and frosting

🔸 2 drops
✅ perfect for making large molded chocolate figures

🔸 3 drops
✅ versatile chocolate suitable for making any texture: frosting, ganache for coating, icing, chocolates

🔸 4-5 drops
✅ ideal for molded chocolates with a thin chocolate shell

That is, depending on the application, you should choose a chocolate with the right fluidity.

In my work, I most often use milk or dark chocolate with a fluidity of 3 drops - its properties are most suitable for making ganaches for coating, crémeux, as well as mousse and baked chocolate cheesecakes.

---

## Tip 141 — Several Thickeners in a Filling. Why?

**Source:** merged B4-41 + B5-06 (identical content in both)

SEVERAL THICKENERS IN A FILLING. WHY?

In online courses and during live master classes, which I miss so much, students often ask me why they need to add gelatin if they have already added pectin to the preparation.

The question is really good and very interesting. Let's find the answer now!

The thing is that each thickener has different properties:

🌿 Agar-agar
🔸 gives a delicate, "glassy", not elastic texture
🔸 activates at high temperatures (90-100°C). If added to a cold mixture, it starts to set at 40°C and form lumps
🔸 must not be frozen as it may lose its properties
🔸 if you process the gelled mixture with a blender, you'll get a gel

🌿Gelatin
🔸 gives good elasticity and makes the fillings a bit "rubbery"
🔸 activates at low temperatures (can be added at 25-30°C)
🔸 retains its properties when frozen
🔸 it takes at least 6-7 hours for the filling to set
🔸 contains a large amount of protein, which allows one to whip it and get a thick foam

🌿 Pectin (NH, NH Nappage)
🔸gives a stable, jam-like texture and doesn't affect the taste of the mixture
🔸 activates at high temperatures (80-100°C)
🔸 retains its properties when frozen
🔸 fillings made with pectin can be reheated

Sometimes when we want our filling to get the properties of 2 different thickeners at once, we add gelatin or agar-agar to the pectin.

For example, when making the Kiwi-Lime-Bergamot cheesecake, the photo of which you can see in the carousel, I used both pectin (to give light elasticity and natural taste) and agar-agar (for stability) to prepare the Mojito marmalade made from vermouth, lime juice and bergamot.

In this case, the gelatin can't be used to stabilize (gel) the mixture. Can you guess why?

## Tip 142 — What You Need to Know About Pectin, Part 1 (What Is Pectin)

**Source:** from B5-07

part 1

We've already talked with you about gelatin, and today I'd like to start a new series of posts about the super-popular thickener - pectin.

So, let's start with finding out what pectin is.

Pectin (from the Greek "pektos" - 'firm and hard') is a polysaccharide (high-molecular weight carbohydrate) that is present in foods and has an important function, which is to ensure their quality and texture.

In the culinary arts, pectin acts as a gelling agent, stabilizer, thickener, and water-retaining agent.

Like some other thickeners (cornstarch, agar-agar, gelatin), pectin in small quantities thickens the mixture, and in large quantities - gellies it.

It is used to make jams, confits, jellies, marmalades, fruit pastes (pastila) and much more.

The main advantage of pectin compared to other thickeners is that it is able to form a sufficiently thick texture WITHOUT AFFECTING THE TASTE, SMELL AND COLOR OF THE FINISHED PRODUCT.

Pectin is obtained by aqueous extraction of the appropriate edible plant materials, mainly from citrus peel and apple pomace, sugar-beet pulp or sunflower heads, unlike, for example, gelatin, which is obtained by boiling skin, tendons, ligaments, and/or bones with water.

Interestingly, some fruits (apple, plum, currant) contain so much pectin that one can make jelly or jam from them without adding pectin.

📌I added a table which shows the content of pectin in fruits and berries to the carousel.

🌱The strength of pectin

To standardize pectins, the USA-SAG method is used: a gel is prepared from the pectin under study, sugar, water and acid. While hot, this pectin solution is bottled in standard cups and set aside until set. The single gels are removed carefully from the cups and transferred on a plate which allows one to measure the percentage of sagging of the gel cone under its own weight with the help of a ridgelimeter. From this value the °SAG can be calculated.

The majority of pectins are standardized to 150 grade USA-SAG (the strength of almost any pectin you find in pastry stores).

---

## Tip 143 — What You Need to Know About Pectin, Part 2 (Powdered vs Liquid; Chemical Properties)

**Source:** from B5-08

part 2

Thanks to your comments I decided not to put writing the continuation of the post on pectins on the back-burner. Today, as promised, I will tell you about the types of pectin.

There is no unified world classification of pectins. Each manufacturer creates its own unique product! However, pectins can be classified according to a number of properties.

So, here are the classifications of pectin:

🌱 According to the form it comes in

📌 POWDERED

Should be mixed with sugar and gradually sprinkled into the mixture at a temperature of 40-45°C.

📌 LIQUID (EXTRACT)

Should be diluted in hot water and only then added to the mixture. This type of pectin is less common as it can only be used if the mixture can hold the extra liquid. It is more suitable for large productions.

🌱 By chemical properties

During the production, pectin undergoes an esterification process — it interacts with alcohol and acid, which gives it various properties.

📌 Low ester (the degree of esterification (DE) is less than 50%) — is able to gel regardless of the acidity of fruits/berries or amount of sugar, but in the presence of calcium ions.

This type of pectin works with dairy-based and low sugar mixtures.

📌 High ester (DE is 50% or more) — is able to gel at high acidity (low pH) and high sugar content of the mixture. This is the most popular type of pectin — it dissolves faster in water and gels mixtures faster.

In the pastry business, high ester pectin is used to make zephyr, fruit pastes (pastila) and marmalade.

📌 Amidated pectin is a low ester pectin which undergoes ammonia treatment during the manufacturing process. Gelation requires less calcium in neutral pH mixtures and is less prone to setting (gelling) at high calcium concentrations.

Amidated pectin is often used to make fruit fillings for yogurts and thermostable fillings.

---

## Tip 144 — What You Need to Know About Pectin, Part 3 (Thermal Reversibility; Setting Speed; Apple/Citrus Pectin)

**Source:** from B5-09

part 3

So, guys, today is Sunday, and it means that we'll have a theory today. When our brain, body and soul are relaxed, they are best able to absorb information.

Today we continue to talk about the classifications of pectin. The pectin can also be classified according to:

🌱 Thermal reversibility:

📌Thermoreversible - such pectin becomes liquid when heated and then solidifies when cooled, that is, the mixture can be reheated and cooled again until set.

📌 Regular - once it gels, it cannot be reheated because pectin loses its properties at temperatures above 90-100 °C.

🌱Time of setting (gelling)

📌 Rapid set pectin
🔸 degree of esterification - above 70-76%
🔸 gelling temperature - 75-85°С
🔸 gelling time — 10-15 minutes

📌 Medium rapid set pectin
🔸 degree of esterification - 70-72%
🔸 gelling temperature - 60-70°С
🔸 gelling time — 15-20 minutes

📌 Slow set pectin
🔸 degree of esterification - 56-68%
🔸 gelling temperature - 45-60°С
🔸 gelling time - 20-25 minutes

If we try to analyze these numbers, we can notice a consistent pattern:

✅ The higher the degree of esterification is, the faster the gelling happens, and the higher the temperature is needed to form gel.

The speed of gelation is regulated by chemical compounds in the composition of pectin.

Each manufacturer "plays" with pectin compositions and forms its own assortment. On the one hand, this allows you to choose pectin for absolutely any confectionery purposes, on the other hand, it complicates the issue of their interchangeability.

Let's look at the main types of pectins:

🌱 Apple or citrus pectin

This is basic, regular pectin. It is obtained from the peels, seeds and pulp of citrus fruits. Has nothing but pectin in its composition. This pectin is classified as high ester, that is, to form a stable gel, this type of pectin needs a large amount of sugar in the composition, the presence of acid and high temperature.

**Notes:** "Slow set pectin" appears twice across the series (here as a category, and again in Tip 146 as a distinct product) — kept as-is per source, not an error to fix.

## Tip 145 — What You Need to Know About Pectin, Part 4 (Pectin NH, Nappage, Yellow, NH Plus)

**Source:** from B5-10

part 4

I'm super happy that you're so active in stories reacting to new flavors of cheesecakes

My photographer and one of the first tasters won't let me lie to you - they taste like heaven! And I got so much pleasure from the process of creating these flavors! I wish I could spend all my time doing this!

Guys, I also ask you to support the new post about pectins as warmly🔥 as you did with cheesecakes. If you want to create new flavor combinations or just love to bake using recipes from famous chefs, this knowledge will definitely come in handy.

Today we continue to analyze the main types of pectins. In addition to the regular basic pectin, you also can find:

🌱 Pectin NH

Low ester thermo-reversible pectin that does not require a high content of sugar and acid in the mixture. It is also suitable for making fillings for mousse cakes - it tolerates freezing well and does not let the liquid leak out when thawed.

🌱 Nappage

Low ester thermo-reversible pectin that activates in mixtures with 60-70% sugar content and sour fruits and berries. Most often it is used to prepare a neutral gel and glazes.

📍 The manufacturers often treat NH pectin and pectin Nappage as the same product. You can even come across the name "Pectin NH Nappage". It's good for making glazes in particular.

The main difference is that pectin Nappage gives a more transparent gel than pectin NH, which is extremely important for the preparation of a neutral glaze.

🌱 Yellow pectin

In Europe, the term "yellow" refers to regular apple or citrus pectin, which can lead to confusion. It all depends on the specific manufacturer, so be sure to read the ingredients on a product label.

PatisFrance yellow pectin is more like nappage, while Sosa yellow pectin is regular apple pectin.

🌱 Pectin NH Plus

Medium ester pectin, which does not form a dense marmalade texture, but thickens well, producing an elastic texture. With this pectin mixtures set faster than with NH pectin. It is used to prepare all kinds of fillings and glazes.

## Tip 146 — What You Need to Know About Pectin, Part 5 (Pectin FX, Acid-Free, Slow Set)

**Source:** from B5-11

part 5

In the previous posts we've already discussed such main types of pectin as regular pectin (apple / citrus / yellow), pectin NH, NH Plus and pectin Nappage. Today I suggest analyzing the remaining three types:

🌱 Pectin FX

Low ester pectin, which only works with calcium-containing foods; the level of acidity does not matter. Most often it is used to make milk and cream fillings, marmalade or confiture.

📍It also works with dairy products regardless of the amount of sugar in the mixture, which allows you to get a non-sweet, balanced taste of creamy fillings.

🌱 Acid-free pectin

In fact, this is an analogue of regular pectin, for which the level of acidity does not matter since it contains calcium sulfate which has good hygroscopic, stabilizing and emulsifying properties.

It is used when working with fruits and berries that do not contain acid, e.g. bananas, apples, coconuts, dates, watermelon, etc. It also works well with nut butters. For instance, the pistachio paste marmalade is made using acid-free pectin.

🌱 Slow set pectin

Another analogue of regular pectin. The key difference between them is that slow set pectin gels mixtures much more slowly. This is very convenient if, for example, you need to pour a filling or marmalade into small molds - it gives you 3-4 minutes for this, while any other pectin sets almost instantly.

Knowing the properties of each type of pectin, you can easily choose the right one for any of your confectionery experiments and thus, obtain the best result.

If this series of posts was useful for you, leave any smiley in the comments, and next time I'll tell you how to decide how much pectin to use😉

## Tip 147 — What You Need to Know About Pectin, Part 6 (Methods of Adding Pectin to a Mixture)

**Source:** from B5-12

part 6

Today we'll talk about ways of adding pectin to the mixture being gelled and will choose the best of them.

1️⃣Pre-dissolve pectin in boiling water

Add pectin powder to boiling water, carefully processing it with a hand blender. Then pour it in a thin stream into the hot mixture being gelled, stirring it with a whisk.

This method is used if the mixture can "hold" additional liquid. It's more suitable for large productions.

📍Proportions:

Pectin - 5-8%
Water - 92-95%

2️⃣ Pre-dissolve pectin in sugar syrup

Add pectin to the sugar syrup and stir thoroughly until the pectin has completely dissolved. Then add it to the mixture being gelled.

3️⃣ Mix pectin and sugar

This is the most common and convenient way. Vigorously stirring the warm mixture (35-50 °C) with a whisk, gradually sprinkle in the sugar, pre-mixed with pectin.

📍Bear in mind that at temperatures below 35 °C, pectin may start to set, form lumps, and thus, it will be difficult to stir the mixture and get a good texture.

❓ Why is it necessary to mix sugar and pectin, and not add them separately?

The whole trick is the interaction of pectin with water.

A particle of pectin powder, once in water, absorbs it like a sponge, increasing in size several times, and only after reaching a certain size starts to dissolve.

If pectin powder particles, when in contact with water, are close to each other, then, in the process of absorbing water and swelling, they stick together, forming a sticky lump that slowly dissolves in water. To avoid this, pectin should be pre-mixed with sugar in a ratio of 1:10 or more.

#marusya_about_pectin

## Tip 148 — What You Need to Know About Pectin, Part 7 (How Much Pectin to Use)

**Source:** from B5-13

part 7

You can know everything about pectins, but when it comes to creating some kind of filling or dessert using it, the question arises: how to decide how much pectin to use?

Among the huge number of questions about pectin, this is perhaps one of the most important.

Let's find the answer😉

To start the gelling process, you need 3 components:

1️⃣ sugar
2️⃣ pectin
3️⃣ acid or calcium

Scientists consider the ratio 60 : 1 : 1 to be optimal

🔸 60 parts of sugar
🔸 1 part of pectin
🔸 1 part of acid (citric, tartaric, lactic, etc.)

However, this ratio may vary depending on:

🔺 the type of pectin you are using (yellow, NH, FX);
🔺 pectin formula, which each manufacturer keeps in strict confidence;
🔺 the initial content of pectin in berries or fruits that you are going to use;

📍 the more pectin the fruit you work with contains, the less pectin you need to introduce additionally

⬅️ photo 3 shows a table with pectin content in fruits, berries and vegetables

🔺 the type and density of the filling you want to get: runny sauce (coulis), stable and soft cake filling, dense marmalade, thick confiture...
🔺 the type of sugar you are using (sucrose, glucose or fructose).

📍 You can obtain the most dense texture by using sucrose (regular sugar), and the least dense - by using fructose.

⬅️ 4th photo shows the thickness of gel depending on the type of sugar used

Taking into account all the above factors, which the amount of added pectin depends on, it becomes clear that, unfortunately, it is impossible to create a single table with universal proportions of pectin.

But there is good news - most often it is enough just to read the recommendations on the ratio on the packaging, if you have one available.

If not, use the ratio 60 : 1 : 1, take into account all the influencing factors and experiment✨❤️

## Tip 149 — Things We Need to Know About Eggs, Part 1 (Egg Anatomy: Shell, Cuticle, Membranes)

**Source:** from B5-14

part 1

#marusya_likbez

I haven't posted theory for a while, so let's revive this tradition again.

I'm planning to post some useful information on thickeners - pectin, gelatin, agar-agar, starch and much more. I feel that I need to speak out to eliminate your doubts concerning their interchangeability.

Feel free to suggest your topic for the next useful post in the comments. And meanwhile, let's talk about ... EGGS.

We all know that an egg consists of a shell, and inside there is a yolk and white. But apart from them, there are many more other components inside the egg.

In fact, the anatomy of an egg is the following:
🥚 shell (+ cuticle)
🥚 outer and inner membrane
🥚 air chamber (air cell)
🥚 chalaza
🥚 vitelline membrane
🥚 yolk
🥚 egg white

SHELL is the first line of defense against air and bacteria. The shell weight makes up approximately 10% of the total egg weight.

Eggshell contains 7 to 17 thousand tiny pores. These pores are responsible for the movement of air and the penetration of bacteria into the egg, and let the carbon dioxide and moisture escape the egg.

You may ask where the extra water and carbon dioxide come from in the egg. We will definitely talk about this when it comes to egg aging and shelf life

CUTICLE is a natural coating that covers the surface of the egg and helps to fill the pores in order to protect the egg against air and bacteria.

❗️ Egg washing reduces this protection, leading to a more quick egg spoilage. The likelihood of bacteria getting into the egg increases.

OUTER AND INNER MEMBRANE - these are the layers that separate the shell from the egg white.

As the egg ages, the protective shell dries up and begins to flake off, allowing the outside air to enter the egg shell through the small pores and be stored in the AIR CHAMBER for chick to breathe

Why are fresh eggs so hard to peel? Until a few weeks have passed, the amount of air accumulated between the membranes won't allow them to easily detach and peel the shell from the hard-boiled egg white.

❗️Therefore, by putting a hard-boiled egg into cold water, we do not make it easier to peel it, but significantly reduce its shelf life.

---

## Tip 150 — Things We Need to Know About Eggs, Part 2 (Chalazes, Vitelline Membrane, Yolk)

**Source:** from B5-15

part 2

Let's continue on the useful topic of egg constituents. Today we'll talk about chalazes, the yolk membrane and, in fact, the yolk itself. We will devote a separate post to the topic of egg whites because this component is essential in the confectionery industry.

Please, support the release of the useful post with ❤️ and any emoticon in the comments 😉

When we break a fresh egg, we can see thin white ropes stretching from the yolk — these are called CHALAZES — twisted threads of egg white that anchor the yolk in the center of the egg white. Chalazes are usually only seen in very fresh eggs.

By the way, quite often I hear that someone does not like custards and curds because of the rather pronounced egg amber. The cause of the unpleasant odor is precisely the chalazes. Therefore, in order to get rid of the unwanted aroma of eggs, it is enough to simply strain the egg melange before use.

Chalazes can also cause breaks in eclairs. Here's another good reason for straining.

The YOLK MEMBRANE or vitelline membrane is a thin coating that helps to retain the yolk. It has as many as 4 layers.

In stale eggs, the membrane becomes thinner, since it for 97% consists of water, which, during storage, gradually evaporates through tiny pores in the eggshell.

The yolk does not spread when a fresh egg is broken due to the strong vitelline membrane, but every week the yolk membrane becomes thinner and weaker.

The yolk accounts for about 39% of the eggs, of which as much as 46% are dry matter. The yolk contains fat and cholesterol, as well as most of the calories in an egg. Most vitamins and nutrients are found in the yolk.

The yolk is an emulsion that contains the natural emulsifier lecithin, which performs an extremely important for pastry chefs function — retaining moisture in the product. That is why baked goods with only yolks come out more tender and do not stale much longer than with whole eggs.

---

## Tip 151 — Things We Need to Know About Eggs, Part 3 (Egg White / Albumin Structure and Proteins)

**Source:** from B5-16

part 3

#marusya_likbez

Finally, my hands reached the point of writing to you about the most intriguing component of the egg for us, confectioners — egg white.

Egg white (albumin) is actually consist of four parts: an internal thick white, an internal thin white, an external thick white and an external thin white. These layers are designed to protect and cushion the chick embryo as it develops.

The white of a freshly laid egg will be turbid, not quite transparent. This is because it contains carbon dioxide, which slowly escapes through the shell as the egg ages, turning the white turbid color to transparent.

❗️The fresher the egg, the thicker and turbidier the protein.

All layers of the egg white become more watery as the egg ages. Therefore, the old egg will spread in a thin layer on the pan, while the fresh one will be fried in quite thick layer. Noticed?

In general, during storage of eggs, all nutrients gradually decompose into water and carbon dioxide, which eventually come out through the pores in the shell.

❗️This is why old eggs always weigh less than fresh ones.

Egg white contains proteins, as well as niacin, riboflavin, magnesium, potassium and sodium. It is not as nutritious as the yolk, but it has only about 17 calories and does not contain fat and cholesterol.

Proteins found in eggs:

✅ Ovomucin

🔸 Responsible for the elasticity of the egg — accordingly, it has a direct impact on the volume and stability of the meringue. It is ovomucin that helps to beat the egg whites into a lush mass.

🔸 In fresh protein, the structure of ovomucin is dense. However, during storage, it becomes less stable, as a result the meringues on stale eggs are not so elastic with a less lush structure.

✅Ovotransferrin and ovalbumin

🔸 Responsible for coagulation and heat resistance of the egg, so the older the egg, the more time it takes to cook it. If you use too old eggs, the meringue may not bake properly at all.

Now egg white powder — albumin becomes more and more popular. There are 3 types of albumin, but only one of them is ideal for confectionery purposes.

---

## Tip 152 — Things We Need to Know About Eggs, Part 4 (Shelf Life / Storage of Eggs, Melange, Powder)

**Source:** from B5-17

part 4

We had a look at the anatomy of an egg, but did not talk about the egg as a whole. If I'm not mistaken, I promised to dwell upon the shelf life of the eggs.

So, let's start!

❗️ Fresh eggs storage

🔺Commercial industry
🔸 Eggs are stored at room temperature as the use of refrigeration equipment is impractical (space, electricity, depreciation)
🔸 Shelf life - up to 25 days
🔸 After disinfection, eggs are stored for no more than a day

🔺 Storage at home
🔸 At room temperature - up to 25 days
🔸 Storage in the fridge at 4-6°C extends the shelf life to 90 days, at 0-2°C - up to 125 days

⚠️ Types of eggs by freshness

🔸 with a shelf life of no more than 7 days after the egg was laid
🔸 with a shelf life of more than 7 days after the egg was laid

❗️Egg white and egg mixture storage

🔸 in the fridge at 2-6°C for making custard - no more than 8 hours
🔸 in the fridge at 2-6°C for making baked goods and semi-finished products - no more than 24 hours

❗️Egg melange storage

🔸In the fridge at 2-6°C - no more than 8 hours
🔸 Egg melange can be frozen - it can be put in a blast freezer and then stored in a low-temperature chamber at -18°C for up to 15 months
🔸 Storage of the defrosted melange - no more than 4 hours in the fridge
🔸 Before using, is should be strained through a tinned metal or stainless steel sieve

❗️ Pasteurized eggs storage

🔸 Once opened, they should be stored in the fridge for no more than 3 days

❗️Powdered eggs storage

🔸 Shelf life - up to 6 months
🔸 Once diluted with water + 20°C, the shelf life is no more than 24 hours

❓If you still have questions about eggs, ask them in the comments, I'll be happy to answer them.

## Tip 153 — Things We Need to Know About Eggs, Part 5 (Egg Disinfection: Production vs Home)

**Source:** from B5-18

part 5

The beginning can be found by hashtag
#marusya_likbez

Today we will talk about the disinfection of eggs. This topic is important and interesting for all pastry professionals: both home bakers and those who work in production.

First of all, it is worth noting that the disinfection of eggs at home and in production is very different.

✅ Egg disinfection in production

In production, eggs are disinfected in special rooms. First, the eggs are sorted, randomly ovoscoped (using a special device with a beam of light passing through the egg so that one is able to see what happens inside), and then transferred to 4-section baths for disinfection. In each section, the egg goes through a certain stage:

🔸 1st section - the eggs are placed in warm water for 5-10 minutes
🔸 2d section - disinfection with a 0.5% sodium carbonate solution (at 40-45 °C) for 5-10 minutes
🔸 3d section - disinfection with a 2% bleach solution or 0.5% chloramine solution for 5 minutes
🔸 4th section - the eggs are rinsed with running water for 5 minutes.

✅ Egg disinfection at home

There are a large number of disinfectants that are suitable for disinfecting eggs at home. For example, Nettex, Battles, Defra and others.

Regular washing with baking soda will not fully protect against the risk of salmonella infection. Moreover, such a procedure will destroy the protective shell of the egg, and the risk of pathogens entering will increase significantly.

In addition, when cooking curds, custards and egg white creams made from raw eggs, the temperature is not high enough to sterilize the eggs. Therefore, there is always a small, but still possible risk of getting infected with salmonella.

However, if you are very meticulous in this matter, there are 3 ways:

1. Do not cook homemade custards and curds made from raw eggs.
2. Always use disinfectants.
3. Switch to pasteurized eggs or dried powder (dried egg white or egg powder).

## Tip 154 — Things We Need to Know About Eggs, Part 6 (Substituting Fresh Eggs with Dried Egg Powder — Formula)

**Source:** from B5-19

part 6

#marusya_likbez

Today I'd like to explain to you how to work with dried egg powders.

Most often they are used in mass production, but some home bakers also tend to use powders in order to zero out the risk of salmonella infection when preparing frostings made from raw eggs.

In order to understand how to substitute a regular fresh egg for dried egg powder, you need to figure out how much dry matter the egg itself contains.

Standard dry matter content:

🔸 Whole egg (egg melange) - 27% dry matter
🔸 Egg yolk - 46% dry matter
🔸 Egg white - 12% dry matter

There is a formula by which you can calculate the required amount of dried egg powder (G₂):

G₂ = (G₁ × a₁) ÷ a₂

G₁ is the mass of the product being substituted - for example, you need to substitute 2000 g of fresh eggs for dried egg powder

a₁ is the dry matter content of the product being substituted - as you now know, the whole egg contains 27% dry matter

a₂ - dry matter of the substitute product, that is, the powder itself - as a rule, the percentage of dry matter content is indicated on the packaging, ~ 94%

Thus, you get:

G₂ = (2000 g × 27%) ÷ 94% = 574 g

That is, in order to substitute 2 kg of fresh eggs, you need 574 g of dried egg powder.

Lastly, it's necessary to find out how much water is needed to dilute the powder:

2000 g - 574 g = 1426 g

Easy as ABC 😉

If you need to substitute only the egg white or egg yolk, then use the percentage of dry matter in each of them (46% and 12%, correspondingly) in the formula and get the required amount of dried egg white or egg yolk.

## Tip 155 — About Food Colorings, Part 1 (Classification Overview)

**Source:** from B5-20

part 1

Do you use food colorings?

I'm not a big fan of using synthetic food colorings in large quantities for two reasons.

The first is harm to health due to questionable substances of chemical origin in the composition. No matter how manufacturers convince us of 100% safety, this, alas, is far from always the case.

The second reason is less obvious, but no less important for me — the psychological aspect of the perception of colors by our brain — dividing them into edible and inedible.

Our brain is designed in such a way that subconsciously we will avoid eating food that is too bright and flashy, especially of cool inedible shades.

Despite the above reasons, in general I have nothing against the wise use of colorings in pastry. Especially when the addition of a coloring of a certain hue allows the client to identify the dessert with the fruit used for its preparation.

For example, one can use a small amount of orange coloring for orange desserts or yellow for banana desserts.

Let's try together to sort out the huge number of different food colorings present in the market, in particular let's figure out how and where they can be used.

So, colorings are usually classified according to:

🔺 composition: synthetic and natural;
🔺 form of production: powdered, gel and liquid;
🔺 chemical basis: water-soluble and fat-soluble.

## Tip 156 — About Food Colorings, Part 2 (Natural vs Synthetic)

**Source:** from B5-21

PART 2

check out the beginning by the hashtag #marusya_foodcolorings

Today we'll talk about the classification of food colorings by their composition. As I've said, they are natural and synthetic.

🌿 Natural

Originate from a wide range of natural sources, mainly wild and cultivated plants, as well as waste from their processing.

In the pastry art, a wide variety of natural food colorings is used:

🔸 Teas: green or blue matcha, hibiscus
🔸 Freeze-dried berry, fruit or vegetable powders
🔸 Powdered plant, vegetable or fruit juices
🔸 Algae and spice extracts
🔸 Vegetable charcoal
🔸 Fermented plant foods (e.g red rice)

Natural colorings are suitable for coloring dough, frosting, culinary mastic, sugar flowers, meringue and zephyr.

Pros:
➕ Contain only natural ingredients
➕ a confectionery product gains additional useful properties

Cons:
➖ relatively short shelf life
➖ may be subject to microbiological deterioration (formation of mold, fungus)
➖to obtain a rich color, a large amount of coloring is required
➖ the color is quite difficult to predict (if the same plants grow on different soils, different shades can be obtained)

🌿 Synthetic

Synthetic colorings are chemical compounds that are synthesized in laboratories and used to color various confectionery products, such as frostings, syrups, culinary mastic, sponge cakes, macarons and chocolate.

Pros:
➕ stable and predictable result
➕ long shelf life
➕ a wide range of colors, that allows you to create very bright, saturated shades with a small amount of coloring
➕ resistance to technological processing (the color won't fade in the oven and retain its initial shade in frostings of various properties)
➕ neutral taste

Cons:
➖ deprived of useful properties
➖ contain substances of chemical origin in the composition
➖ stain the oral cavity

Personally, I use both synthetic and natural coloring, depending on the situation, but I try to give preference to natural ones.

---

## Tip 157 — About Food Colorings, Part 3 (Water-Soluble vs Fat-Soluble)

**Source:** from B5-22

PART 3

#marusya_foodcolorings

Speaking about colorings we've come to a very important classification into water- and fat-soluble: I will tell you how they differ and how to choose the right type.

🌿 Water-soluble

This type of colorings is used to color foods with a high water content.

🔺liquid water-soluble clorings are suitable for water-based glazes, marshmallows, zephyr or fruit pastes (pastila);

🔺 gel water-soluble colorings, in which the percentage of moisture is lower than that of liquid ones, can be used to color sponges, low-fat frostings, as well as to create all kinds of patterns on confectionery products by applying the colorings to a pastry bag;

🔺powder (dry) water-soluble colorings are suitable for coloring products that are highly sensitive to moisture: for example, meringues or macarons.

🌿 Fat-soluble

Fat-soluble colorings do an excellent job of coloring high-fat mixtures without forming lumps and curdling, giving the desired color to products.

Are a good choice for coloring chocolate used for decoration, creating velour mixtures, chocolates design, etc. Everything containing cocoa butter and chocolate requires the use of fat-soluble colorings.

🔺 Liquid / gel fat-soluble colorings are represented by a very poor color range. Therefore, compatible colorings were created (when mixed with a water-soluble coloring, they turn it into a fat-soluble one).

🔺 dry (powder) fat-soluble colorings are added to chocolate in dry form and should be thoroughly mixed or processed with an emulsion blender.

📍 Which coloring is better for chocolate: liquid or powder?

Definitely powder! The liquid will inevitably break the structure of cocoa butter, and you might encounter issues when tempering the mixture.

The powder coloring will not affect the mixture in any way, but the weight of the coloring cannot exceed 0.02% of the weight of the product being colored in order to avoid staining the oral cavity.

---

## Tip 158 — Interchangeability of Glucose, Glucose-Fructose Syrup and Trimoline

**Source:** from B5-23

#marusya_theory_tips

Almost every day I receive DMs with your requests for a piece of advice on this issue. Let's figure it out in detail❤️

The main component in the carbohydrate composition of glucose syrup is glucose. Glucose-fructose syrup usually contains 50% glucose and 50% fructose. These syrups are almost 100% interchangeable.

The sweetness value of syrups is much lower than that of sugar. Just to compare: sugar has a coefficient of 1, while glucose-fructose syrup has a coefficient of 0.6-0.8 (depending on the amount of water in the composition).

📍 Glucose-fructose syrup is also an artificial substitute for honey. The only difference between honey and glucose-fructose syrup is that honey contains pollen and other substances carried by bees that can cause an allergic reaction.

Trimoline (inverted sugar) has a pasty consistency and looks like candied white honey. Its carbohydrate composition is also represented by glucose and fructose, but the percentage of water is significantly lower than that of glucose-fructose syrup.

Thus, the sweetness value of trimoline is much higher than that of sugar. As I've already mentioned, sugar has a coefficient of 1, while trimoline has a coefficient of 1.2-1.4 (depending on the amount of water in the composition).

Glucose-fructose syrup can not always be substituted for trimoline.

You can safely replace one with another if you are making ganache, frosting or chocolates filling. But if you are making various kinds of caramels, syrups or zephyr, meaning the products exposed to high temperatures during cooking, then the glucose cannot be substituted for trimoline due to its sensitivity to high temperatures - at temperatures above 100 °C it can have an unpleasant burnt smell and taste.

📍 When adding trimoline to baked goods (puffs, croissants, etc.), the baking temperature is usually reduced by 10 °C to avoid excessive browning.

## Tip 159 — About Tastes and How They Are Created (Introduction: Taste Components)

**Source:** from B5-24

A basic knowledge of primary flavors and how they work is essential in the culinary arts.

Unfortunately, among amateurs, few people have this knowledge and even less consciously use it, combining ingredients and adding spices rather intuitively.

For example, when asked why I always add salt to honey dough, none of my students answered correctly.

Why do French chefs teach to cook bitter caramel, why salted caramel is tastier than unsalted, how to reduce the sweetness of a dessert without reducing the amount of sugar at the same time? — answers to all these, as well as many other questions, can be obtained with basic knowledge of flavors.

So, I propose to sort out the basic flavors, as well as talk in detail about how we perceive them and what influences our perception.

Our taste buds are able to perceive only 4 basic flavors: sweet, salty, sour and bitter. The art of culinary is to bring these 4 components into a balanced harmony and create a delicious taste.

It is equally simple and complex. After all, not only taste buds are responsible for the perception of taste, but also smell, touch, sight and hearing, including our emotions, thoughts and mood.

TASTE = TASTE QUALITY + TASTE IMPRESSION + AROMA + UNKNOWN "FACTOR X"

Taste quality is what is perceived by the taste buds.

A taste impression is what is perceived by the rest of the mouth.

Aroma is what is perceived by the sense of smell.

The unknown "X factor" is what is perceived by other senses, as well as by the heart, mind and spirit.

Liked the article? — do not forget to thank the author with your ❤️

Please rate the usefulness of this article on a 5-point scale, where 5 is 👍🏻, 1 is 👎🏻. The more 5 marks there are, the faster I will write a sequel for you with an analysis of 4 main tastes.

In writing the article, materials from books are used:
✔️ The Flavor Bible by Andrew Dornenburg & Karen Page
✔️The Flavor Matrix by James Brinscione
✔️The Art & Science of Foodpairing by Peter Concquyt, Bernard Lahousse, Johan Langenbick

**Notes:** First post of the "About Tastes" series (parts 2–7 follow as Tips 160–165), though not explicitly numbered "part 1" in source.

---

## Tip 160 — About Tastes, Part 2 (The 4 Main Tastes: Sweet, Salty, Sour, Bitter)

**Source:** from B5-25

PART 2

#marusya_flavours

Judging by the number of "5" under the previous post, the topic turned out to be interesting for you. Knowing tastes and having the ability to understand and combine them skillfully is incredibly important both in creating desserts and meals in your kitchen!

As promised, today I will dwell upon the 4 main tastes.

⚠️ Sweet taste

To act on our receptors, a large amount of a sweet substance is needed, unlike other tastes. Nevertheless, we can appreciate the harmony and pleasant taste that even a subtle sweet taste gives to spicy dishes.

Sweet taste is combined with bitter (chocolate), sour (berries, lemon) and even salty (caramel). Sweetness can also bring out the flavors of other ingredients, like fruit or mint.

⚠️ Salty taste

Salt is a natural flavor enhancer and the most important ingredient for making spicy food delicious. By the way, sugar plays this role in desserts.

⚠️ Sour taste

As flavor enhancers, acidic components are second in importance only to salt in spicy dishes and sugar in sweet ones. Sour notes - whether it be a little lemon juice or vinegar - add brightness to the dish. The harmonious combination of sourness with other flavors is crucial to the finished dish.

⚠️ Bitter taste

Humans are most sensitive to bitter taste, and our body's defense mechanisms allow it to be recognized even in small amounts.

The bitter taste is harmoniously combined with the sweet, and sometimes can significantly reduce the richness of the dish. For some chefs, the bitter taste is more important than others, but some see it as a necessary savory flavor - one that makes you take another bite, and then another.

Every delicious dish you've ever tasted has been the result of these 4 tastes acting on your taste buds. We distinguish them both individually and in combination.

---

## Tip 161 — About Tastes, Part 3 (Umami and Taste Perception: Temperature, Texture)

**Source:** from B5-26

part 3

the beginning can be found by the hashtag #marusya_flavours

We continue to talk about tastes. Today I will tell you about the fifth little-known taste of umami, and also we will deal with such a concept as "taste perception".

⚠️ Umami (found in high-protein foods)

In addition to the four basic tastes, a growing body of evidence points to the existence of a fifth taste, umami.

It gives dishes a salty or meaty "rich" taste. It can be felt in ingredients such as anchovies, cheese, mushrooms, green tea, and flavorings such as monosodium glutamate, which is the main component of ready-made seasonings.

✔️TASTE PERCEPTION
Temperature, texture, piquancy, astringent taste.

In the oral cavity, in addition to taste, there are tactile receptors sensitive to temperature and texture, which are also very important in determining taste. Such perception of food is usually called taste perception.

They help our body digest food, get the most nutrients and pleasure from it. A crunchy dish should be appealing in both sound and texture.

⚠️ Temperature

Temperature is the main sensation perceived by the oral cavity. The temperature of food even affects how we perceive its taste. For example, cool temperatures suppress the appeal of sweetness.

The temperature of food can affect both how it is perceived and how much you enjoy it. The menu is always dominated by hot dishes in winter and chilled ones on hot summer days, precisely because they balance the temperature of our body and the environment.

⚠️ Texture

The texture of food plays a crucial role in its ability to attract and please. We consider mashed and/or creamy foods (soup, mashed potatoes) traditional while crunchy food is considered fun.

We like texture because it activates the senses of touch, sight, and sound.

---

## Tip 162 — About Tastes, Part 4 (Piquancy, Astringency, Aroma, Chemical Senses)

**Source:** from B5-27

part 4

the beginning can be found by the hashtag #marusya_flavours

Thank you for supporting new posts about tastes with likes and comments! I see that you're involved and interested and it inspires me to write a continuation.

So, in addition to temperature and texture, the following 2 factors also affect the TASTE PERCEPTION:

⚠️ Piquancy

Our taste buds can also perceive what we mistakenly call spicy, meaning piquant taste, that is more pronounced in chili peppers and barely noticeable in cayenne pepper powder. Some prefer the piquant taste to others.

⚠️ Astringent taste

Our taste buds also recognize astringency, a dry mouth sensation caused by the tannin content in tea or wine, and sometimes cranberries, unripe persimmons, and certain types of nuts.

✔️ AROMA

Taste balance largely depends on the aroma. It is believed that it is the aroma that mainly determines the taste of the dish. This explains the popularity of such flavoring ingredients as fresh herbs, spices or lemon zest.

Adding flavoring ingredients will enhance the aroma of the dish and improve its taste.

Some foods are perceived with both our taste buds and our sense of smell.

⚠️ Strong spicy taste

Ingredients such as horseradish and mustard have a strong spicy taste and aroma. However, despite their piquancy, their taste and aroma irritate the receptors of both the nasal and oral cavities.

⚠️ Chemical senses

The term "chemical senses" refers to taste sensations that are pleasant to us (for example, the tingling sensation when drinking sparkling drinks) and at the same time irritate or deceive the taste buds (for example, chili pepper creates a false sensation of burning, while peppermint has a cooling effect).

## Tip 163 — About Tastes, Part 5 (The "X Factor": Emotional, Mental, Spiritual Perception)

**Source:** from B5-28

PART 5

the beginning can be found by the hashtag #marusya_flavours

The unknown "X factor" is what is perceived by the heart, mind and spirit.

Food has an effect on our entire body. We perceive it not only with the help of the 5 senses, but also emotionally, mentally and even spiritually.

Among the 5 senses, the first we resort to is sight. The visual representation of a meal can greatly enhance the enjoyment we derive from it.

The appearance of the dessert directly affects our perception of its taste. For example, the richer the color of a berry sorbet is, the more intensely we will perceive its berry flavor.

The stronger the association between a particular food and color is, the stronger the flavor impact. For example, a combination of strawberry with red, lemon with yellow, lime with green.

It is for this reason that, despite the disfavor for the use of colorings, I allow myself to add a little yellow dye to banana and lemon zephyr, purple dye to the lavender, for example, or color the honey cake frosting.

Emotional perception is the perception of taste by the heart. How else can you explain the fact that an adult very often prefers a dish prepared according to their mother's recipe. This also explains the attraction of traditional dishes and national cuisines as a manifestation of our love for the culture.

Mental perception - the perception of eating food by the mind as a process that not only allows for maintaining our vital functions and health, but also for getting pleasure.

Spiritual perception - the perception of cooking and eating as a sacrament that can improve the quality of our daily lives.

The world's leading chefs have worked to improve every aspect of the meal experience - from products to atmosphere and service - in order to take this process to a new level, filled not only with pleasure, comfort and interest, but also with meaning.

## Tip 164 — About Tastes, Part 6 (Flavor = Taste + Smell; Maillard Reaction; Sous-Vide/Airtight Cooking)

**Source:** from B5-29

PART 6

the beginning can be found by the hashtag #marusya_flavours

Let's talk more about FLAVOR today.

Why is flavor so important when it comes to pairing ingredients?

Flavor is a combination of taste that is perceived by taste buds and smell. It is assumed that in 90% of cases the taste we perceive is actually flavor!

For example, when you eat a pineapple, the flavor is actually perceived through the sense of smell. But if the pineapple is not ripe, it won't be as flavorful. It may taste sweet, but it will no longer taste like pineapple.

There are two ways to add flavor to a dish: through aroma or a chemical reaction. In fact, culinary art is nothing more than a laboratory experiment: the moment a raw product is cooked, its state changes.

For example, when roasting meat, carbohydrates and amino acids interact with each other, which leads to caramelization of the product. This chemical reaction is called the Maillard reaction, and it contributes to the manifestation of flavor.

The aroma tends to disappear. If you enter the kitchen and you feel that it smells good, then the dish itself has already lost most of its flavor. The aroma has dispersed in the air.

To give the dish some flavor and keep it, it is best to use the low-temperature cooking method (products are packed in vacuum bags and cooked at a low temperature).
This method helps to preserve the flavor without letting it fade, but unfortunately it's not suitable for home cooking.

Airtight cooking may do better for home conditions - put the product you want to cook in a ziploc bag filled with some water (if it is supposed to be cooked in the oven) or without it (if you're going to cook it in a pot with water).

Meat cooked in this way, especially oven-baked ham, is very tasty and flavorful.

---

## Tip 165 — About Tastes, Part 7 (Scientific Foodpairing: The Kiwi/Oyster Story)

**Source:** from B5-30

part 7

the beginning can be found by the hashtag #marusya_flavours

Why do some ingredients pair especially well together and others don't? This is a question that no doubt preoccupied many in the food industry to the point of keeping them awake at night.

A scientific explanation for this phenomenon was given by Bernard Lahousse in 2005. At that time, he actively collaborated with Michelin-starred chefs Korean Sang-Hoon Degeimbre and Belgian Kobe Desramaults.

They met regularly and discussed potential menu items they had in works. At one such meeting, Sang-Hoon asked, 'Bernard, why is it that when I smell the kiwis, I also smell the sea? Is that possible?'

This question interested Bernard so much that he borrowed a mass spectrometer and a gas chromatograph from his scientist colleague and began to analyze the volatile substances that kiwis release into the air, thereby creating the recognizable aroma of an exotic fruit.

As it turns out, in addition to the fruity esters, kiwis also contain concentrations of green, grassy, ​​and fatty-scented aldehydes, which have a marine-like scent similar to that of oysters and other types of shellfish.

The discovery of an aromatic link between these two seemingly completely unrelated ingredients formed the basis for the very first scientific foodpairing, thus revealing to the world kiwitre, Chef Degeimbre's creation, which has since become a signature dish on the menu of L'Air du Temps.

Kiwitre is a dish in which a raw oyster is served over diced kiwi with croutons and lime-infused coconut cream.

Thus, the scientific foodpairing is based on the fact that the ingredients in order to combine well must contain the same aroma molecules, like kiwi and oysters, for example, share the same marine-like aroma note.

If you have read the article to the end, please, leave any emoji in the comments ❤️

Want to learn more about aroma molecules, key aromas, the main aroma types and how it all works?

**Notes:** Final part of the tastes series. Ends with a teaser question rather than a full close — likely a continuation existed but is not present in this source range.

## Tip 166 — What You Need to Know About Gelatin (Can Gelatin Be Boiled?)

**Source:** from B5-31

#marusya_about_gelatin

The question of whether it is possible to boil gelatin provokes a lot of controversy and contradictions: some say that it can be done, others - by no means. Let's look into this issue.

So, first of all, I'd like to say right away that GELATIN CAN BE BOILED! However, not in all cases it is necessary to do this - it all depends on how and what you're using it for.

Let's have a look at a few cases.

1️⃣ High concentration of gelatin in liquid

Let's take a no-bake cheesecake as an example. For its preparation, gelatin is diluted in a small amount of water, and then mixed with cream cheese or whipping cream. The gelatin mass turns out to be thick and viscous, and, accordingly, if heated for a long time and strongly, it:

🔸 may set too quickly
🔸 may clump up

Therefore, with a high concentration of gelatin in the liquid, it is not recommended to heat the mass for longer than it takes the gelatine to dissolve completely.

‼️ATTENTION‼️ If you still have brought the gelatin mass to a boil, do not throw it away - just let it cool down a little.

2️⃣ Low concentration of gelatin in liquid

If you need to jellify the juice or fruit puree, you can safely bring the gelatin to a boil. The jellified mass will turn out to be quite runny, so there's almost no risk of rapid setting and the formation of lumps.

However, do not forget that the liquid will boil down and the concentration of gelatin will increase, so keep the mass boiling for literally 1-2 minutes, no more. Excessive concentration of gelatin can negatively affect the taste, making the jelly too thick, and the gelatin flavor more pronounced.

3️⃣ Making the jelly from raw materials of animal origin

If the jelly is made from fish or meat broth, it must be simmered over low heat so that there are no large bubbles, otherwise it won't be clear. But it is not the gelatin that becomes cloudy, but the broth itself!

You can safely use all of the above rules, but it should be remembered that the strength of gelatin is significantly reduced in an acidic environment (pH < 5) and in the presence of protease enzymes.

**Notes:** Distinct from the other "WHAT YOU NEED TO KNOW ABOUT GELATIN"-titled posts in this document (Tips 167 and 169/B6-11) — the source reuses this exact title across at least 3 separate postings on different sub-topics (boiling gelatin here; basic definition/sourcing/powdered-gelatin prep in Tip 167; gelatin mass/sheet gelatin/Bloom strength in Tip 169). Flagged in the consolidated Flagged Anomalies section as a title-reuse pattern worth human attention.

## Tip 167 — What You Need to Know About Gelatin (What Gelatin Is; Sources; Powdered Gelatin Prep)

**Source:** from B5-38

At first glance, the topic is very simple and there is not much to learn about, but, in fact, it's not that easy.

Gelatin (French gélatine, from Latin gelātus "frozen") is a mixture of peptides and proteins produced by partial hydrolysis of collagen extracted from the skin, bones and connective tissues of animals. The collagen (protein) in these tissues is converted to gluten.

Gelatin is used in many areas. If we talk about cooking, then it is used to make jelly, confit, mousse, soufflé, marshmallows and so on.

Thanks to it, the mousse cakes keep their shape and tender creams in eclairs are not runny. Gelatin is also used for the production of ice cream to prevent sugar crystallization and reduce protein coagulation.

Edible gelatin used to be a product of animal origin, but with the development of the technologies there have appeared different variations of gelatin that no one could think about.

Notice, now gelatin can be made using a by-products of:

📌beef
📌pork
📌fish
📌vegetables❗️

You will also find halal and kosher gelatin.

🌱 Types of gelatin

There are two types of gelatin – powdered and sheet.

📌 POWDERED

It is always soaked in cold water in a ratio of 1: 5 or, depending on the strength of the gelatin, 1: 6.

Mostly, it's "instant gelatin" – it takes just a few minutes for it to swell. Then it is heated until it is completely dissolved.

It is important that the liquid to which the gelatin is added is warm, so that the gelatin disperses well in it, and does not form a huge lump.

Sometimes it is difficult and irrational to heat up the entire mousse or cream. For example, if it's a mousse made with cold sour cream, then a part of it, no more than 1/3, is taken, slightly heated over a water bath or in a microwave, then the gelatin is added to it, mixed well, and then both mixtures – the warm with gelatin and the cold one – are combined.

**Notes:** This is the actual FIRST installment in the gelatin educational series (basic definition, sourcing, powdered-gelatin prep), distinct from Tip 166 (a later, more advanced/narrow post — "can gelatin be boiled") from the same ongoing #marusya_about_gelatin hashtag campaign. See Tip 166's note.

---

## Tip 168 — Gelatin: Converting Between Different Strengths (Bloom Conversion Formula)

**Source:** from B5-32

We've already talked about what gelatin is, its usage, types, as well as forms of gelatin and its strength. The beginning of the article can be found by the hashtag #marusya_about_gelatin

Today I suggest talking about conversion between different strengths of gelatin.

When you're going to prepare any pastry product that contains gelatin, it is important to understand that its consistency and texture depends not only on the QUANTITY of the gelatin added, but also on its STRENGTH.

And if the strength of the gelatin in the recipe is different from the strength of the gelatin you use, there is a very high risk of ending up with an unstable confit, which flows out of the cake, or a too thick cheesecake ...

There are 2 ways of getting a stable, predictable result:

1️⃣ Use gelatin with the same strength as indicated in the recipe
2️⃣ Use any gelatin that you have on hand, converting between different strengths

‼️HERE'S THE FORMULA:

In order to convert between different strengths of gelatin, you need to divide the strength of the gelatin used in the recipe by the strength of your gelatin.

G1 ÷ G2

You will get the coefficient by which you need to multiply the weight of gelatin, indicated in the recipe.

For example, a recipe uses 10 g of gelatin Dr. Oetker with a strength of 220 bloom. And you have gelatin with a strength of 180 bloom.

Calculate the coefficient:

220/180 = 1.22

Then multiply this coefficient by the weight of the gelatin in the recipe: 10 g x 1.22 = 12.2 g

If the strength of your gelatin is greater than in the recipe, you will get a coefficient which is less than one. This means that you will need to use a smaller amount of gelatin.

As you can see, conversion is quite simple to do, and at the same time, it helps to avoid undesirable difficulties when working with the textures of your fillings, cakes and desserts.

---

## Tip 169 — Shelf Life of Frosting

**Source:** from B5-33

I hear this question all the time: both at live master classes, and at my online courses, as well as from clients who contact me in DM. The question is relevant, interesting and, without a doubt, extremely important for any pastry chef and everyone who just loves to bake.

✅ Making the frosting

🔸 I always tell my students that making the frosting is an integral part of assembling a cake.

Such frostings as buttercream, cream cheese frosting, custard and frosting made from egg whites, should be used immediately after their preparation.

‼️ THEY CANNOT BE STORED AND MUST NOT BE PREPARED IN ADVANCE.

🔸 Other types of frosting can be stored in the fridge at 2-6 °C for no more than 5 hours. After that, they must be used for coating and decorating a cake.

✅ Storage of frosting during coating and decorating

It should be remembered that:

🔸 From the moment you start applying the frosting to a dessert, you have no more than 1.5 hours - this is the period set for working with frosting at the workplace.
🔸 If you take breaks during this process, the piping bags filled with frosting should be put in a clean container and placed in the fridge till the end of the break.

✅ Storage of finished desserts with frosting

🔸 The container in which you place the pastry product must be clean, dry and without any foreign odors.
🔸 All finished pastry products should be stored in the fridge at 2-6 °C.
🔸 Shelf life of finished products according to governmental standard:

🔺 with frosting made from egg whites - no more than 72 hours;
🔺 with buttercream - no more than 36 hours;
🔺 with cream cheese - no more than 24 hours;
🔺 with custard - no more than 12 hours;
🔺 with crème fraîche or sour cream - no more than 6 hours.

**Notes:** This "Shelf Life of Frosting" post covers similar ground to Tip 006 (from Batch 1) but with different numeric ranges and structure — presented and kept as an independent, separately-derived tip per this task's continuation-only scope; a human reconciler comparing this master against the earlier Tip 006 should note the discrepancy (Tip 006 gives ranges like 24–72 hours for buttercream depending on ingredients; this tip gives a flat "no more than 36 hours" governmental-standard figure) as the two appear to be genuinely different source posts on the same general topic, not duplicates.

## Tip 170 — Conversion of Recipes for Any Size Cake Molds

**Source:** from B5-34 (tail fragment also appears as B6-01, see note)

Every day I receive a lot of photo reviews about cheesecakes prepared using my recipes: you comment on their mind-blowing tastes, their beautiful appearance, the balance of flavors and textures....

But as soon as it comes to the slightest change in the recipe, such as using a mold of a different diameter, people often get confused and ask me to recalculate the ingredients to fit their mold.

I always try to answer all the questions you have. But I would really like each of you to understand how this can be done on your own, because in fact, if you know the conversion formula, it will be easy as pie.

‼️SO, HERE'S THE FORMULA:

In order to convert the recipe for another size mold, you need to divide the square of the diameter of the larger mold by the square of the diameter of the smaller mold.

d1² ÷ d2²

And you will get a number by which we need to multiply or divide all the ingredients that appear in the recipe.

For example, the recipe requires a mold of 18 cm in diameter. But you only have a mold of 20 cm in diameter at hand.

You calculate the coefficient:

20*20 = 400
18*18=324

400/324 = 1.23

Since you have a larger mold than indicated in the recipe, you MULTIPLY the amount of all ingredients by 1.23.

If, on the contrary, the mold in the recipe is larger, and yours is smaller, then you DIVIDE by this coefficient.

As you can see, the conversion is not difficult at all.

If this post was helpful to you, be sure to let me know with a ❤️ or a comment.

P.S. For those to whom all this simple mathematics still seems complicated for some reason, I've added a table with ready-made coefficients in the carousel, which is available on the Internet 🥰

➡️ Horizontally - the diameter of the form in the recipe, cm
⬆️ Vertical - the diameter of your form, cm

**Notes:** Batch 6's B6-01 independently captured only the closing sign-off/P.S. fragment of this same post (a boundary artifact at the start of its assigned range); Batch 5's B5-34 has the complete post and is used here in full — B6-01 is redundant and not counted separately.
