## Tip 171 — Cake Coating, Common Problems: Leaking Liquid — Whey (Yogurt, Sour Cream, Cream Cheese Cases)

**Source:** merged B5-35 + B6-02 (identical content in both)

CAKE COATING. COMMON PROBLEMS

The leaking liquid from the assembled cake. Where does it come from?

In fact, in 98% of cases, the reason is very simple. It's all about whey - the liquid residue of cheese, casein and yoghurt production.

It appears especially if dairy products do not contain thickeners and stabilizers, in the event of a violation of the storage temperature and a number of other factors.

❗️ My first leaking cake was a honey cake. In frosting recipe I replaced the sour cream with Greek yogurt. The whey flooded the entire cake base and part of the fridge. Can you imagine how wet the cake was inside?

Therefore, when asked whether it is possible to use yogurt instead of sour cream for frosting, I say "NO"! ❌

The frosting with the addition of even a small amount of yogurt (Greek or Turkish 10-12%) seems to be absolutely stable at first, but over time it liquefies a lot and becomes almost runny. It might be used in sponge cakes if they are not soaked at all, but for honey cakes this option won't work at all.

❗️ The second cake leaked because of the poor quality sour cream - either the storage conditions or the process of preparation were violated.

The remains of sour cream and frosting having spent some time in the fridge released a huge amount of whey. It's logical that the same thing happened with the frosting in the cake - the sponge layers absorbed most of it, but some of it leaked onto the cake base.

❗️ In the third case, the cream cheese was to blame. I usually use Cremette, which contains thickeners and stabilizers, so it releases a small amount of whey.

But one day, for lack of Cremette, I assembled the cake with another cream cheese, which contained neither thickeners nor stabilizers. Also, most likely, the technology of its preparation was also different. Now I understand why it cost almost half the price of Cremette.

That cream cheese released 3-4 times more whey than Cremette. As a result, in the morning there were several tablespoons of sweet liquid on the cake base.

---

## Tip 172 — Cake Coating, Common Problems: The Cake Press (Pros/Cons of Weighting a Cake)

**Source:** merged B5-36 + B6-03 (identical content in both)

CAKE COATING. COMMON PROBLEMS

Last couple of words about putting the press on the cake and we'll proceed to the last part of the article - leaking liquid from the assembled cake.

The cake won't become less airy because of the press, believe me. A properly baked sponge should bounce well, that is, if you press on it, it perfectly returns to its original shape, so a press placed on top of a thick cake base does not affect it.

The same can be said about the fluffiness of the frosting. Micro air bubbles inside the frosting won't be squeezed out of the cake in any way. The press only helps to get rid of large hollows inside the cake.

By the way, getting an ideal cut of a honey cake without a press can be a challenging task - the edges of the cake layers will always be slightly bent downwards.

Do I put weight on my cakes? Very rarely, to be honest. I simplified this process and quickly tamp the cakes tightly immediately after assembly.

❌ The last part of the article is leaking liquid from the assembled cake.

To my mind, very few of you have faced this problem. For example, in my entire practice, the cakes leaked only three times.

I really wanted to show you a photo that my student once sent me, but I had to give up this idea, since it is impossible to find the right one among the thousands of photos.

Therefore, at this point, imagine a coated smoothed cake with a pool of liquid leaked from it onto the cake base.

Why does this happen? Logically, due to the large amount of liquid in the cake. But where does it come from?

## Tip 173 — How to Make a Perfect Cheesecake (Myths, Baking, Cooling, Decorating, Storage — Q&A Format)

**Source:** merged B5-37 + B6-04 (identical content in both)

HOW TO MAKE A PERFECT CHEESECAKE

ℹ️ If you open the oven, the cheesecake will collapse.

Stupidity! The cheesecake shouldn't rise at all. If it rises, it means that the temperature that you set for it is too high.

ℹ️ Never open the oven while the cheesecake is baking.

Also wrong. If the cheesecake is baking at low temperatures, then nothing bad will happen if you open the oven.

ℹ️ On average, any cheesecake should be baked for at least 1.5-2.0 hours.

In fact, the baking time will depend on many factors — the characteristics of your oven, the temperature you set (90-150 °C / 194-302 °F), the height of the cheesecake (4-6 cm), taste (chocolate, caramel, berry or fruit), and also the number of cheesecakes (two cheesecakes in the oven will bake 20-30 minutes longer, for example).

That's why there is only one way out — to check its readiness using a piece of advice from the next paragraph. Usually, a cheesecake with a diameter of 16-18 cm and a height of 5-6 cm is baked for about 100 minutes.

ℹ️ How to check the doneness of a cheesecake?

By touching the cheesecake's top with your finger. It should remain clean, while the middle of the cheese should slightly jiggle, like jelly.

✅ Do not worry if some butter leaks onto the baking tray during baking. You used it to grease the ring, and it's natural that it melts at high temperatures. It doesn't affect the texture and taste of the base at all.

✅ Turn off the oven, slightly open the door and leave the cheesecake to cool down inside for about 30-40 minutes. Then take the cheesecake out of the oven and leave it at room temperature until it cools down completely. After that, put it in the fridge for 6-12 hours.

⚠️ If you put a still warm cheesecake in the fridge, the condensation may form and the base will soak from moisture.

✅ Coat the cheesecake with a variety of fillings and décor only after it has completely cooled down (at least 6 hours).

Now there are only a few questions left to deal with:

ℹ️ What can be used to decorate the cheesecake?

✔️ fresh berries, fruits
✔️ coulis, confit, berry / fruit sauces, jellies, etc., in short, everything that can be made from fruits / berries and their puree with the addition of a thickener
✔️ regular or whipped ganache
✔️ regular or whipped caramel
✔️ nuts, meringue, creams, namelaka, cremeux, gels, glazes, etc.

ℹ️ How long does a cheesecake last?

The shelf life of any product depends on the way it was made, the products used in it and the storage temperature. That's why the only thing that I can say is that on average, the shelf life of baked cheesecakes is no more than 3-4 days in the fridge. Then, how long you will be able to resist this sweet temptation is up to you.

ℹ️ Can cheesecakes be frozen?

If it is dry and there are no foreign odors in the freezer, you can freeze a cheesecake and store it for not more than 20-30 days.

✅ I don't advise freezing cheesecakes if you are unsure about your freezer, but if you need to freeze a cheesecake without a cake base, make sure to wrap it in plastic wrap.

✅ You can turn any large cheesecake into individual cakes, and vice versa. Just keep in mind that the baking time will change. For individual cheesecakes it's 50-60 minutes maximum at the temperature that is 10-20 °C / 50-68 °F lower than usual.

ℹ️ What texture does a perfect cheesecake have?

It's like with zefir – everyone has their own preferences. First of all, YOU should like the cheesecake! It all depends solely on your taste and nothing else!

## Tip 175 — Cake Coating, Common Problems: Air Bubbles (Why They Form, the Press Pros/Cons)

**Source:** merged B5-39 + B6-06 (identical content in both)

CAKE COATING. COMMON PROBLEMS

How to avoid air bubbles?

I'm sure you sometimes come across photos of assembled cakes with packs of butter or cream on top.

I hope you've already guessed why this is necessary, but in a few words I'll explain what happens.

Being pressed down by some weight, all the air comes out of the cake, and in this way you let the layers tightly stick together for a more secure transportation of the product.

So let's see whether it is obligatory to put a weight on a cake, or such an idea is completely meaningless?

Let's start with where we started.

What to do if air bubbles appear on the side of the cake over time? Answer: put on a press!

There are some simple rules for placing a cake under the press:

📌 the press does not have to be put on for long - several hours will be quite enough, depending on the thickness of your cake and the weight of the press itself

📌 the press is never put directly on the cake, but always on a thick cake base to evenly distribute pressure over the entire surface

📌 the press should be put only on the cake which is tightly tightened with the acetate film, or, better with a cake ring for assembling.

📌 It's better not to use a press for cakes with very delicate and moist fillings.

➕ Pros of putting a cake under the press:

✔️ no air inside the cake and, as a result, no bubbles on coating
✔️ perfectly flat cake surface, which greatly facilitates the smoothing process
✔️ perfectly even layers in the cut, which guarantees a wow effect when cutting

➖ Cons of putting a cake under the press:

✔️ if you put a too heavy press / leave the press for a long time / put the press on a very delicate filling, then you can end up with a too tamped cake.

**Notes:** Both check-marks (✔️) are used for pros AND cons in the source, a source inconsistency, not a transcription error — kept as-is.

## Tip 176 — Cake Coating, Common Problems: Where Air Bubbles Come From (Mechanism)

**Source:** merged B5-40 + B6-07 (identical content in both)

CAKE COATING. COMMON PROBLEMS

Yesterday we discussed the possible reasons why the filling may get out of the cake in the form of bubbles on the coating:

❌ not verified, inaccurate recipe
❌ you deviated from the cooking instructions
❌ incorrect assembly of the cake.

But what if you are 200% confident in your filling and, nevertheless, a tricky bubble still has appeared on your finished cake? Or, let's say, you have pierced the bubble and found that ... there is nothing there! 😳

But it cannot be empty, can it? There should have been something inside that pushed the buttercream and formed the bubble! And if this is not a filling, then, of course, it is ... air!

Let's figure out where this air comes from, what it does inside the cake and why it starts to get out at the most inopportune moment.

Air gets inside the cake, naturally, during the assembly process. When we assemble a cake, especially without a cake ring, we very carefully lower each sponge layer on top of the filling with frosting, trying not to press too much, otherwise we risk squeezing the filling with the frosting out.

It is quite natural that many hollows, filled with air, remain inside the cake. What happens next? In the process of maturing, thanks to the gravity, our cake "shrinks", pushing all the air out.

But it often happens that not all the air has time to get out and some of it remains inside the cake, looking for a perfect moment to put a pastry chef on edge. Usually, because it tries to get out when the chef is about to give the cake to the client.

A similar problem often happens with sponge cakes, especially if we don't give the assembled cake enough time to rest before applying the coating.

---

## Tip 177 — Cake Coating, Common Problems: Deviating from Instructions & Incorrect Assembly (Liquid Fillings)

**Source:** merged B5-41 + B6-08 (identical content in both)

CAKE COATING. COMMON PROBLEMS

In the previous part of the article, we began to analyze the reasons why the filling may begin to get out in the form of bubbles on the side surface of the coating.

So let's continue.

❌ You deviated from the cooking instructions. In this case you are to be blamed, if the first point has not been violated, that is, everything is alright with the recipe.

Probably, you didn't reach the desired cooking temperature or didn't give the filling enough time to stabilize. By the way, some chefs do not mention the latter in their recipes, taking it for granted.

Remember, most fillings require not only complete cooling, but also at least 6-8 extra hours for full stabilization. In some cases (with whipped ganache, for example), it takes at least 12 hours to stabilize in the fridge.

❌ incorrect assembly of the cake. Some recipes call for liquid fillings in the form of sauces, which flow seductively out of the cake when it's cut.

How to ensure that such a filling remains inside the cake without leaking from it before the cake is cut?

Only by assembling the cake in the right way. Firstly, you should make a border of frosting for such a filling at least 2.0-3.0 cm thick. Secondly, it is better to use the same buttercream for the border with which you will coat the cake, that is, more stable, made with butter or chocolate ...

In the continuation of the article, I will tell you about what else can cause bubbles, as well as why this happens and how to avoid such incidents. 🥰

## Tip 178 — Cake Coating, Common Problems: Root Cause — Not Following a New Recipe Exactly (Pectin/Gelatin Types)

**Source:** merged B5-42 + B6-09 (identical content in both)

CAKE COATING. COMMON PROBLEMS

... Quite often, by the way, it is the filling itself that tries to get out and this can happen for several reasons:

❌ not verified, inaccurate or simply violated recipe. This could be a recipe author's mistake, or your own.

In the first case, I advise you to never ❗️ cook to order using a new recipe for the first time, no matter how much you trust its author. Think about your nerves.

Prepare at least half of the portion first. See how the ingredients behave in the process of cooking. Because the same, even seemingly most common products, such as butter and flour of different brands, can have completely different properties and characteristics.

You may make a mistake by allowing yourself small deviations from the recipe, in some cases, without even noticing it at all. How is this possible? I'll tell you.

If you are a novice pastry chef, an amateaur, then it is likely that you do not understand much about the power of gel, collagen, or different types of pectin.

For example, in the recipe after the word "pectin" there are two capital letters NH, which tell you absolutely nothing. And you naively think that there is no difference between types of pectin and you put the one that you have at hand. The same may happen with agar-agar and gelatin. Then don't be surprised at the fillings that want to get out of your cake.

**Notes:** This text begins mid-sentence ("... Quite often, by the way...") — a direct continuation of Tip 177's promised follow-up.

## Tip 179 — Cake Coating, Common Problems: Cracked Coating — Causes and Fixes

**Source:** merged B5-43 + B6-10 (identical content in both)

CAKE COATING. COMMON PROBLEMS

❌ You deviated from the cooking instructions. It's your fault if...
So, the first problem which we've already discussed was cracked coating.

How can you avoid this?

✅ do not violate the technological process and coat the cake with buttercream no earlier than 10-12 hours after assembly, especially if it's a sponge cake

✅ do not make the buttercream layer too thin, unless it is required by the design

✅ use foolproof recipes in which the precise amount of frosting and soaking syrup sufficient to soak the layers are indicated

The second common problem that many pastry chefs might have faced is bubbles on the coated cake.

What's that, what does it look like, why does it happen and how to deal with it?

It happens that you make a cake, keep it in the fridge, everything seems to be as it should be. You coat it with buttercream, decorate, and are waiting for the customer. And then 15-20 minutes before their arrival, you open the fridge, and, gosh, you see a large "tumor" that has formed on the perfectly smooth surface of the cake, as if something is trying to get out of the filling.

Well, it is. Something is trying to get out, but what?

The first problem is a cracked coating. Why does this happen?

Cracks in the buttercream appear due to the fact that the cake layers have absorbed a lot of moisture from the buttercream, it became too dry and cracked.

The most common causes of cracking are:

📌 coating the cake a few hours after assembly

Remember, the technological process of making cakes requires keeping the cake after assembly for at least 10-12 hours in the fridge before proceeding with the coating.

Why? First, the fillings and the frosting need to stabilize and second, which is more important, the cake needs to mature – the cake layers need to soak with frosting.

I prefer to leave the cake in the fridge overnight after assembly and coat it with buttercream only the next day. Thus, the layers will be perfectly soaked and will no longer absorb moisture from the buttercream.

📌 too thin layer of coating

Even if you left the cake in the fridge overnight and it soaked well, parts of the layers that do not come into direct contact with the frosting inside the cake (sides of the layers and the top layer) can still absorb some moisture from the buttercream.

If the coating is very thin and the layers are not sufficiently soaked, it results in cracks on the coating.

📌 insufficient amount of frosting or soaking syrup in the recipe

Finally, the third option that may cause cracks on coating is lack of frosting or syrup inside the cake. Consequently, you get too dry cake layers which actively absorb moisture from the buttercream.

In this case, even though you kept the cake in the fridge overnight after assembly and covered it with a proper layer of buttercream, cracks may appear on the second day after smoothing.

If the article was helpful, leave 👍🏻 in the comments and I'll write a continuation.

**Notes:** This post has an odd internal structure: it opens with a one-line fix-list for "cracked coating" (already introduced in an earlier, not-fully-visible post), THEN re-introduces and fully explains cracked coating and its 3 causes from scratch, THEN teases a "second common problem" (bubbles) already covered in Tips 175–176. This strongly suggests the underlying original Instagram carousel/thread posted these topics in a different order than they appear in this text extraction. Kept as one tip (single opening, single closing "leave 👍🏻... I'll write a continuation") despite the internal redundancy — a content-fidelity call, not an attempt to fix the source's apparent disorder.

## Tip 180 — What You Need to Know About Gelatin, Part 2 (Gelatin Mass, Sheet Gelatin, Bloom Strength, Dosage)

**Source:** merged B5-44 (truncated at batch boundary) + B6-11 (complete version used)

Judging by the number of questions that I often receive, articles with an analysis of the ingredients are very relevant for many of you.

So here is the continuation about gelatin and I kindly ask you to support it with any comment.

A bloomed mixture of water and powdered gelatin is called a gelatin mass.

This term is often found in the recipes of famous chefs. It's very convenient: you make gelatin mass in advance and put it in the fridge, and when you need it, you take it out, weigh the required amount and use it.

The gelatin mass can be stored for up to a week. We'll talk about it in more detail a little later.

📌 SHEET

The gelatin sheets are soaked in a fairly large amount of very cold water – so that the sheets are completely covered with water.

This type of gelatin will absorb just as much water as it needs. Usually it takes about 5-10 minutes.

Before use, squeeze the excess liquid and add the gelatin to the hot mass that you want to gell.

🌱 Gelatin strength

The strength of gelatin is measured according to 2 main classifications:

📌 in Bloom (in honor of Oscar Bloom). The most common gelatin is 125-265 Bloom.

📌 in categories - silver, gold, bronze and platinum (this system is used in America and Australia).

Bronze - 125-135 Bloom
Silver - 160-170 Bloom
Gold - 190-220 Bloom
Platinum - 235-265 Bloom

As for calculating the amount of gelatin, this is an ambiguous question, because it all depends on the density and properties of the mass that is being gellyfied (chocolate in the contents or juice), on the effect you want to get – thick, slightly jiggling jelly or delicate mousse, and on the strength of the gelatin itself.

But, on average, it is assumed that for 500 g of liquid, 10 g of gelatin with a strength of 180-200 Bloom is used.

And then you should decide whether the gelatin is enough, the product is too tender or, on the contrary, thick. Draw conclusions, make adjustments and adapt to the properties of your products.

**Notes:** Batch 5's version of this post (B5-44) was cut off mid-topic exactly at its batch boundary ("SHEET / The gelatin sheets are soaked in a fairly large amount of very cold water..."); Batch 6's independently-captured version (B6-11) is complete, and is used here in full.

## Tip 181 — Cream Cheese vs Mascarpone — What's the Difference

**Source:** from B6-12

LET'S FIND OUT MORE ABOUT CREAM CHEESES

I often receive questions about replacing Philadelphia with Mascarpone in recipes, difference between them and the reasons the frosting suddenly breaks after adding cream cheese to it.

In order to achieve a harmonious combination of ingredients, and sometimes even for the recipe to work, it is extremely important to add the cheese specified in the recipe. Soft cheeses are not always interchangeable!

Let's see what difference is between cheeses and why the interchange doesn't always work.

Cream cheese is classified as fresh cheese. Depending on the technology, there are acid, acid and heat, and acid and enzyme coagulated cheeses.

The most common fresh cheese is cream cheese.

An important feature of classic cream cheeses is their high fat content, which is 60-80%, as well as a pronounced characteristic creamy taste.

📌 What is the difference between Mascarpone and cream cheese?

The fat content of Mascarpone reaches 80%, the taste of the product is slightly sweet and resembles thick sour cream or butter.

❗ Classic Italian Mascarpone, in fact, is not cheese, since special enzymes and lactic acid bacteria are not used to make Mascarpone. Therefore, the name "cheese" is nominal.

📌 How is Mascarpone different from Philadelphia?

✔ Initially, Mascarpone was made in Italy. And it appeared much earlier. Philadelphia, in its turn, was made in the USA.

✔ The fat content of Mascarpone is up to 80%. In Philadelphia it's up to 69%.

✔ Vinegar, tartaric acid or lemon juice are used to make Mascarpone. To make Philadelphia traditional ferments are used.

Before proceeding to the analysis of the interchangeability of soft fresh cheeses Cream Cheese and Mascarpone, let's have a closer look at the process of their manufacturing.

✔ Cream cheese (Philadelphia) is made from two types of milk: skim and pasteurized. In order to denature the protein, a special ferment and heavy cream are added. As a result, this soft fresh cheese acquires a delicate and creamy aroma.

✔ Mascarpone has one of the highest fat contents among soft fresh cheeses. For its manufacture, pasteurized buffalo milk or heavy cream is used, to which the required amount of tartaric acid and white wine vinegar is added. After that, the process of fermentation and coagulation begins. The last stage is cooling. After that, the cheese is packed in tight bags and hung to remove the excess whey.

As you can see, the raw materials for making these two cheeses (skim and pasteurized milk vs heavy cream or buffalo milk), as well as the technologies (ferment vs tartaric acid and vinegar) are completely different.

❗ That's why let me express this thought one more time - Mascarpone and Cream Cheese are not the same !!! ❌

Now let's see if they are interchangeable. Well, to some extent yes, and to some extent - no. How is it possible?

Let's say, if it is really important for you to keep the texture and the taste of the frosting originally suggested by the author of the recipe, then these cheeses cannot be called interchangeable at all.

In fact, Mascarpone cannot be replaced by anything without affecting the taste of the product, as it has a unique viscous and creamy texture.

But what if keeping certain texture and taste of the frosting becomes a low priority, since now you have only Mascarpone / Cream Cheese at hand and you need to assemble the cake right away?

Let's have a look at exactly why the cream cheeses and mascarpone are relatively interchangeable.

**Notes:** Opens the cream-cheese-vs-mascarpone series with definitions and manufacturing comparison; ends on a cliffhanger answered in Tip 182.

---

## Tip 182 — Cream Cheese vs Mascarpone — When They ARE and ARE NOT Interchangeable

**Source:** from B6-13

I kindly ask you to support the post with ❤ and any emoticon in the comments, since you won't find this information anywhere. I share with you my own experience, which has been accumulated over the years.

📌 So, what are the cases in which it is possible to replace cream cheese with mascarpone and vice versa?

In principle, in any frosting or sour cream frosting that is used as filling of a sponge or honey cake.

If in your recipe, for example, there are 2 parts of cream cheese and 1 part of cream (2:1) or 1 part of cream, 1 part of cream cheese and 1 part of sour cream (1:1:1) you may easily replace the cheeses with each other and nothing bad will happen.

❗ Important!
Consider the different texture and fat content of the cheeses. It might affect the taste of the pastry to some extent.

❗❗ Much more important!
Work with mascarpone extremely carefully! Unlike frosting with cream cheese, frosting with mascarpone is very easy to overwhip - it may break in the blink of an eye and you'll end up with lumps and curdled texture.

All this happens due to the capricious mascarpone.

Therefore, when working with the latter, keep an eye on the process of whipping the frosting, do not get distracted.

📌 When are these cheeses completely not interchangeable?

In any buttercream, most often for coating the cake.

I can't count how many times students have sent me photos of broken buttercream with a request to help them fix it! After a look into the issue, it turned out that mascarpone was used instead of cream cheese. Please, remember, you should not do that!

Why?

Mascarpone does not tolerate temperature differences. If you add cold cream cheese to room temperature butter, nothing bad will happen. But if you add cold mascarpone, the buttercream will curdle in a wink.

Therefore, if you want to replace cream cheese with mascarpone in buttercream, change the recipe and the cooking technology, that's the only way.

---

## Tip 183 — Cream Cheese Brands — Cremette vs Philadelphia, Proportions Adjustment, and the Malaysia Buttercream Story

**Source:** from B6-14

So, we figured out the difference between cream cheese and Mascarpone and learnt that they are not always interchangeable.

Now let's talk about different brands of cream cheese. For example, are the most popular of them - Cremette and Philadelphia - interchangeable?

Well, yes and no.

Why? The taste and texture of these cheeses are similar, therefore, in this regard, they are completely interchangeable. But as for the consistency (thickness), the cheeses from different manufacturers can be absolutely different.

Will this somehow affect the final appearance / taste of the dessert? Certainly!

So, where does this fundamental difference in the consistency of cream cheeses come from, if the cooking technology is almost the same?

Different compositions. And first of all, it is the qualitative and quantitative content of thickeners and stabilizers.

For example, Philadelphia contains only locust bean gum which is a stabilizer, while Cremette contains starch (thickener), locust bean gum and guar gum (stabilizers).

Everyone who has worked with these cheeses should have noticed that frosting made with Cremette is always more stable and holds its shape better.

This is a key factor of the incomplete interchangeability of cream cheeses by different manufacturers. You should always make adjustments to the recipe based on the consistency of the cheese used.

I'm extremely grateful for the likes and comments under the posts. Their number gives me the opportunity to see how useful the article is, because I alway try to share relevant information with you.

So, let's continue.

If you work with thicker cream cheese like Cremette, for example, then frosting can be prepared with a 2:1 ratio – two parts of cream cheese to one part of cream, and buttercream with a 3:1 ratio – three parts of cream cheese to one part of butter.

If you want to replace the cream cheese with a softer one, but at the same time keep the same stable consistency, then you need to change the proportions, for example, to the following: frosting 3:1 – three parts of cream cheese to one part of cream, buttercream 2:1 – two parts of cream cheese to one part of butter.

But sometimes the consistency of the cream cheeses are so different that you have not only to change the proportions, but even to change the cooking technology.

I'll share with you one story from my practice.

It happened at a master class in Kuala Lumpur, the capital of Malaysia. An important thing to mention – it's Southeast Asia with an equatorial monsoon climate. You know, summer is all year round and the average temperature is about 32-33 °C / 90-91 °F.

So when, on the second day of the master class, the students started making the buttercream to coat the cakes, strange things began to happen.

To tell the truth, they did happen before too. Just then they took a new turn, let's say. Just kidding.

The butter, as I asked, was taken out of the fridge in advance, so it was at room temperature, but the cream cheese, according to my instructions, was taken straight from the fridge.

"Chef, there are lumps in the buttercream, what should we do?" – in half an hour I hear a nervous sounding voice of the organizer, who took an active part in the process ...

If you are curious to know the end of the story, leave an emoticon in the comments. If there are a lot of your comments, I'll post a continuation soon!

---

## Tip 184 — The Kuala Lumpur Buttercream Story — Ending (Cream Cheese Too Cold / Hot Climate Fix)

**Source:** from B6-15

Since you supported the continuation of the story with comments, I am posting its end. Let's create a win-win situation: I share an article, you leave likes ❤️

..."Chef, there are lumps in the buttercream, what should we do?" - in half an hour I heard a nervous sounding voice of the organizer, who took an active part in the process ...

"Whip it more until the lumps disappear." - I answered.

In 15-20 minutes:

"Chef, nothing has changed. The buttercream is still lumpy." - said one of the students.

What nonsense, I thought, and went to check the buttercream by myself. As it turned out, it wasn't nonsense, but a disaster! The buttercream contained solid lumps of cream cheese, which did not want to combine with the butter into a homogeneous mass.

I started to analyze the situation. As it turned out, due to the hot climate, the manufacturers add such a quantity of thickeners and stabilizers to cream cheese and other products, that even at room temperature the local cream cheese remains stone-like compared to the Cremette, which I usually work with.

As a result, we started to heat the cream cheese in the microwave until warm, process until smooth in a mixer, and then added whipped room temperature butter. Only in this way we succeeded in achieving a uniform texture of the buttercream.

So, have you now figured out all the nuances regarding the cream cheeses, or do you still have questions? If so, ask in the comments!

## Tip 185 — What You Need to Know About Ganache, Part 1 (Definition, Types, Ratios, Basic Method)

**Source:** from B6-16

WHAT YOU NEED TO KNOW ABOUT GANACHE

Today is Monday and I am here with a useful post. I'm starting to think about making Monday and Thursday, for example, useful information days. What do you think?

Let's start with the elementary. What is ganache?

I am sure, who is not closely connected with the confectionery world, does not even have the slightest idea what we are talking about. For example, all my friends, every time I say "ganache", are sure that this is some kind of terrible curse 😅

So, ganache is a cream that usually consists of chocolate (white, milk or dark) melted in a liquid (milk, cream, fruit juice, or even crème anglaise).

Ganache can be supplemented on various occasions with sugar, butter or flavors, after which it is cooled and used as intended.

In fact, ganache is a stable and balanced emulsion-like chain in which fat molecules are distributed in an aqueous medium.

There are different types of ganache, depending on its texture:
📌 more and less dense (regulated by changing the amount of the liquid component)
📌 whipped and prepared using Chantilly cream technology (determined by the nature of stirring).

Proportions for a thick ganache suitable for macaron fillings:

📌 On dark chocolate 54.5% - 1 part chocolate to 1 part cream (33%)
📌 On milk chocolate 33% - 3 parts chocolate to 2 parts cream (33%)
📌 On white chocolate 28% - 2 parts chocolate to 1 part cream (33%)

Ganache is used for filling tarts, for classic French cakes, for interlayers in modern cakes, in pastries, in desserts. Ganache is the main filling for chocolate sweets.

It would seem that it's very simple — heat the heavy cream, poured it over the chocolate, punch it with a blender, cool and it's done. But there are difficulties and nuances that you have to know about...

In the continuation of the article, I will tell you the possible causes of stratification, how to properly stabilize and store ganache.

Let's also take a look at all the possible additives and consider how they affect taste, texture and shelf life of ganache.

Today we will figure out how to properly make the ganache using different methods.

📝 Weigh the liquid ingredients (milk / whipping cream / puree / juice), sugar (trimoline, if any) and chocolate exactly by grams.

📝 If you don't use professional chocolate callets (which I highly recommend), you need to prepare the chocolate.

📝 In order to do this, break a chocolate bar into small pieces. The smaller the pieces are, the easier it will be to melt the chocolate.

📍1 Method

📌 Heat the liquid ingredients with the sugar, if it is in the recipe, in the microwave oven or on the stove until very hot and pour over the chocolate. Leave for 20-30 sec and then mix thoroughly with a silicone spatula until homogeneous texture.

📌 If there are small pieces of chocolate in the ganache, you may slightly heat it up in the microwave oven or over the water bath. In no case put it directly on the stove - the mixture will start to burn.

📍2 Method

📌 Pour the liquid ingredients (with or without sugar) into a container with chocolate broken into pieces and heat everything together in the microwave oven several times for 5-15 seconds.

📌 The most important thing here is not to overheat the chocolate! While it is completely hard - check it every 15 seconds - take it out and mix. Once the chocolate starts to melt - every 10 seconds. When only small pieces remain - every 5 seconds. Take it out and mix each time.

📍3 Method*

📌 Heat the liquid part (with sugar, if any) to 85 °C / 185 °F and cool it to 35 °C / 95 °F. While the liquid is cooling, melt the chocolate to 32 °C / 90 °F.

📌 Add the liquid to the chocolate in three steps and mix thoroughly with a silicone spatula each time.

📌 The last step - process the emulsion with a hand blender, trying to prevent the air bubbles from entering the ganache.

📌 If everything was done correctly, the ganache prepared in this way will be stable and ready to be used almost immediately. Also, it will not separate.

**Notes:** Covers ganache definition, ratios, and three preparation methods as one continuous "today" post.

---

## Tip 186 — Ganache — Broken Ganache Fixes and Storage/Stabilization Tips

**Source:** from B6-17

🌿 Broken ganache. Possible reasons and way outs:

📌 insufficient amount of liquid - in this case you need to add more whipping cream, milk or puree

📌 poor dispersion of fats in water - process the mass with an emulsion blender

📌 too high indoor temperature – process the mass with a blender and cool it down.

🌿 The obtained ganache can be used immediately - pour it hot into a tart base or onto a sponge, for example. Or you may let it stabilize to the desired texture. The basic principle in the latter case is to prevent the air from entering into it.

🌿 If you want to leave the ganache to stabilize to the desired thickness, it is best to do this at 15-18 °C / 59-64 °F. Pour the ganache onto a baking sheet previously covered with cling film or guitar sheet so that the ganache layer is as thin as possible - this allows the mass to cool faster and more evenly.

🌿 For storage and cooling, you should cover the ganache with cling film or guitar sheet in contact – they should touch the surface of the ganache. Thus, the ganache is protected from air, microorganisms and the evaporation of aromatic substances.

🌿 Transfer the stabilized ganache into a piping bag using a scraper or a spatula. Do not mix or whip it. The texture of the ganache should be stable, but at the same time pliable.

🌿 Store it at 4 °C / 39 °F and use within 2 days. Ganache can be frozen when added to the products in order to streamline the production process.

🌿 It is not advisable to cool the ganache until it is completely stabilized in the fridge. It is better when it sets completely after piping (for example, in macarons).

🌿 If the ganache completely sets, and you start to mix it with a spatula, heat it in a microwave oven, partially knead it in a bag with your hands, then it may result in matte, not beautiful and even broken ganache.

P.S. The photos show Prague cake decorated with a fluffy cloud of dark chocolate whipped ganache.

## Tip 187 — Ganache Components — Chocolate and Liquid (Effect on Texture/Taste, Dosage)

**Source:** from B6-18

Today we'll analyze the components of ganache, their effect on its texture and taste, as well as dosages.

🌿 Chocolate

📎 You may use white, milk (33-41%) or dark chocolate (50-79%) as well as cocoa paste (in some cases).

❗️ Chocolate should be stored in a waterproof hermetic container. When water gets into the ganache, its shelf life and quality decrease.

📌 Texture
• due to the cocoa butter in its contents, ganache becomes runny when heated and thick when cooled.
• the lower the cocoa butter content and the higher the sugar content, the softer the ganache consistency.

📌 Taste
• it has a peculiar taste of white, milk and dark chocolate
• the typical chocolate bitterness is enhanced (70% of cocoa products or more) and leveled out (50% of cocoa products or less).

🌿 Liquid

📎 Fresh or pasteurized cream with the fat content of 35-40%. Due to the minimal heat treatment, the rich taste is preserved, but the shelf life of the ready ganache is reduced, so professionals hardly ever use such cream.
📎 Sterilized or ultra-pasteurized cream with the fat content of 35-37% (preferred).
📎 Milk. Can be used to replace some amount of cream or instead of it. Concentrated milk (sugar free) is often used to reduce the amount of water added, which negatively affects the shelf life of the ganache.
📎 Fruit purees for making fruit ganaches.

📌 Dosage
• milk: maximum 40%
• cream: 20-50%
• puree: maximum 50%

📌 Texture
• fat content of 35% and more makes the texture delicate and melting when tasting
• saturation with liquid - water (about 60%) dissolves sugar and makes the consistency of the chocolate less dense.

📌 Taste
• due to fat molecules it has aroma-retaining properties
• makes the bitterness of chocolate with a high content of cocoa products (for example, more than 70%) milder.

## Tip 188 — Ganache Components — Whipping Cream Protein, Fats/Butter (Effect on Texture/Taste)

**Source:** from B6-19

In continuation of the article let's continue analyzing the components of ganache, their effect on its texture and taste, as well as dosages.

❗ In some cases (for example, when making whipped ganache), whipping cream is a necessary ingredient for increasing the volume (due to the protein content of 1-2%), and surface activity of proteins allows to retain and stabilize air bubbles in the cream.

🌿 Fats

📎 The butter with the lowest melting point (32-34 °C / 90-93 °F) is used.
Butter with fat content of 82% and more can be used (the lower the moisture content of the butter is, the longer the final product can be stored).

❗ With the excess of fat, ganache may destabilize (emulsion may break).

❗ If the fat content is insufficient, the ganache will be too thick.

📌 Recommended dosage of butter: 5-15%
📌 Recommended dosage of fat (butter together with cocoa oil): 25-40%

📌 Texture
• if butter is added to the cream before it is brought to a boil, the fat molecules will be evenly distributed in the mass. This heat treatment prolongs the shelf life of the product.
• if you add butter at the end of cooking (at 30-35 °C / 86-95 °F) without pre-melting it, the ganache will acquire a smooth and creamy consistency, glossy shine, as well as a delicate and melting texture (felt when tasted)
• when butter is heated, the consistency of ganache becomes runnier, and when butter cools, it becomes thicker.

📌 Taste
• due to fat molecules it has aroma-retaining properties
• makes the bitterness of chocolate with a high content of cocoa products (for example, more than 70%) milder.

To be continued 👉🏻

## Tip 189 — Ganache — Sugar, Additives (Sorbitol), Aromatics, and Full Recipe

**Source:** from B6-20

🌿 Sugar

📎 Sugar (sucrose), inverted sugar and/or glucose syrup (high in dextrose) can be used.

📌 The recommended dosage of sugars is at least 25% of the total weight.

📌 Storage
• due to hygroscopic properties, it retains water, binds components and increases the thickness of the mass, giving it stability.
• combining with water molecules from whipping cream, sugars reduce the number of unbound water molecules, thus limiting the environment for the growth of microorganisms (mold) (this is especially important when storing food at subzero temperatures).

📌 Texture
• gives softness and plasticity to the cream

📌 Taste
• gives sweetness

🌿 Additives

📎 Stabilizer: Sorbitol E420
Sorbitol crystals are capable of binding free water molecules.

📌 Recommended dosage: from 1.5 to 2.5% of the total weight.

📌 Texture
• it's a powerful hygroscopic agent that retains and stabilizes moisture.

📌 Storage
• prolongs the shelf life by reducing the activity of unbound water molecules.

🌿 Aromatic additives

❗️ All additives sensitive to microflora (cocoa powder, kitchen herbs, spices, etc.) are to be boiled for several minutes with whipping cream.

📌 Impact factor
• taste as well as color

Hot whipping cream or milk can be infused with spices, herbs and citrus zest in a saucepan covered with a lid. Extracts, essential oils and alcohol can be used as well.

At the end of the article as promised, I post a basic ganache recipe:

180 g of dark chocolate 54.5%
200 g of whipping cream 33-36%
70 g of butter 82-83%
20 g of inverted sugar (optional)
15 g of sorbitol

Total weight: 450g

📌 Chop the chocolate to make it easier to melt when it comes into contact with the cream. Transfer it to a bowl large enough to add the cream to the chocolate.

There is no need to chop chocolate callets.

📌 For better storage, bring the cream, butter and inverted sugar to a boil. Then add sorbitol.

📌 Gradually pour the cream onto the chocolate. First, a small amount of cream is added. You have to obtain a homogeneous mass by stirring it from the center. The emulsion should not be broken when adding the remaining cream in several steps.

📌 Stir the mass thoroughly, but gently with a whisk, then process it with a blender or in a robot-coupe. This stage is necessary to distribute fat molecules evenly throughout the mass and achieve a uniform consistency.

❗️ Process the ganache with a blender while it is hot to avoid air bubbles getting into the cream. This is important due to the fact that:
👌🏼 air is a favorable breeding ground for microorganisms
👌🏼 oxygen causes fats to go rancid.

📌 The ready ganache can be used immediately (while it's still hot, pour it into a tart base or onto a sponge), or you can let it thicken and then pipe it with a pastry bag. The basic principle in this case is to try not to let the air get into the ganache.

📌 For further storage and cooling down, cover the ganache with cling film touching the surface. Thus, you protect it from drying out, microorganisms and the evaporation of aromatic substances.

Store it at + 4°C / 39°F and use it within 2 days. You can freeze it in the products in order to rationalize the production process.

🖋 The article is based on the book Pâtisserie Les Clés de la Réussite, Herrero et Etienne

## Tip 190 — Caramelization and the Maillard Reaction, Part 1 (Caramelization Basics)

**Source:** from B6-21

CARAMELIZATION AND THE MAILLARD REACTION - IS IT THAT EASY TO MAKE CARAMEL?
Part 1

I feel like writing about something profound. Especially taking into account a recent conversation with one of my curious students. She was wondering why in one caramel recipe the cream is added first, and then comes the butter, while in the other it's vice versa – first we introduce the butter, and then pour in the cream.

So, what's the difference?

There are three large groups of reactions resulting in browned food:
1. Fermentation
2. Caramelization
3. The Maillard reaction

We're only interested in the last two groups, since they take place not only in the process of making caramel, but also in many other culinary processes.

Caramelization is a complex chemical process that takes place when sugar is being heated.

Sugar molecules subjected to high temperatures break down into characteristic aromatic fragments, which in turn form new compounds – polymers. They produce a familiar caramel taste and amber color.

The more complex the sugar molecule, the higher its caramelization point.

For example, for fructose (a monosaccharide) it's 110°C / 230°F, while for sucrose (regular white sugar, a disaccharide) – 160°C / 320°F.

The caramelization point of isomalt is 170°C / 338°F, which allows it to be heated for a long time without changing its color to brown.

There are two ways to caramelize sugar:

🌿 Dry method
- only sugar is used
- requires some practice
- no risk of sugar crystallization during the caramelization process.

🌿 Wet method
- water and sugar are used (1: 2)
- gradual heating of the syrup and evaporation of water facilitates the process of making caramel
- if you make a mistake in cooking, there is a risk of sugar crystallization.

The caramelization issue seems to have been sorted out. No tricks: sugar + high temperature = caramelization.

In the continuation of the article I'll tell you about the Maillard reaction and explain the difference between adding the cream and the butter to the caramel first.

---

## Tip 191 — Caramelization and the Maillard Reaction, Part 2 (Maillard Reaction Explained, Answers the Cream/Butter Order Question)

**Source:** from B6-22

Part 2.

The Maillard reaction is a chemical reaction between an amino acid and a reducing sugar, usually requiring the addition of heat.

Excellent example is the crust of roast meat or baked bread which has the typical smell, color and taste of cooked food. These changes are caused by melanoidins - compounds generated in the late stages of the reaction.

It is named after a French physician and chemist Louis Camille Maillard, who was one of the first to study the reaction in the 1910s. There are thousands of melanoidins and other compounds, and about two dozen of them are actively used as flavor additives in food chemistry.

So, the Maillard reaction = [protein (free amino acids) + sugar (monosugars)] + temperature (not necessarily high)

Acidity plays a crucial role in this process. The higher the acidity is, the slower the Maillard reaction occurs.

It means it will never occur when grilling kebabs in a sour marinade. It is for this reason that tomato paste is put into demi-glace sauce only at the last stages, and at the first come bones, meat and vegetables.

Moreover, you should pour the lime juice into the pho broth at the end, but a couple of tablespoons of palm sugar are to be put immediately. So, in no case should you marinate the steak in something sour - you will end up with just marinated meat, not a steak.

Now let's look at the caramel.
The thing is that when we first add 80-100 g of butter (~ 10°C/ 50°F) to 200 g of dry caramel (~ 180 °C / 356 °F), the temperature of the mass quickly drops to ~ 105-110°C / 221-230°F. Then, by pouring in the cream, we will reduce the temperature of the caramel further.

On the contrary, when the hot cream is gradually poured in, the temperature decreases moderately and stays in the range of 140-160°C / 284-320°F longer.

Therefore, in addition to caramelization of sugars, the Maillard reaction also takes place - amino acids in the cream react with sugars, which enhances the aroma and provides a darker shade of caramel.

## Tip 192 — Pectin vs Agar-Agar vs Gelatin — The Difference

**Source:** from B6-23

PECTIN, AGAR-AGAR AND GELATIN. WHAT'S THE DIFFERENCE?

Agar-agar is obtained from seaweed, pectin - from apples and citrus fruits, sometimes from sunflower, pumpkin and sugar beet.

This is how they differ from gelatin, a product of animal origin that is made from many different sources of collagen, mostly from cattle bones and hides, pigskin and fish.

To understand which is better - agar-agar, pectin or gelatin, let's see how each of them works.

Pectin gives products a soft, delicate, slightly viscous consistency. It has no color, but it can add a light sourness.

There are many types of pectin: some need a lot of sugar, that's why they are used for making very sweet masses (jam, confiture, pate de fruit), others work in tandem with calcium that allows us to use them in dairy products. There are pectins of fast and slow setting, thermostable, thermo-reversible, etc.

Agar-agar does not have its own taste and calorie content that's why it is highly valued by confectioners. The use of agar allows you to get a stable thick mass, which will hold the given shape even in the heat. This is very important in making jelly, pastila and pate de fruit.

Agar-agar is thermo-reversible - it can be reheated and then cooled again without loss of quality. This is convenient when one portion of the thickener wasn't enough and you need to add thickness to the mass.

What is the difference between agar-agar and gelatin?

📌 The gelling ability of agar-agar is much better than that of the gelatin, that's why in the preparation process you need 3 times less of it.

📌 Agar-agar sets faster (even at room temperature), while gelatin needs a fridge to set, and all the products made with it melt at 40 °C / 104 °F.

📌 Agar-agar can be dissolved in juices, but gelatin does not like fruit acids, it should be dissolved in water only.

If you need a more detailed analysis of agar-agar and gelatin, their strengths and properties, let me know in the comments 👌🏼

**Notes:** Title in source reads "ECTIN, AGAR-AGAR AND GELATIN..." (missing leading "P"), reconstructed here as "PECTIN, AGAR-AGAR AND GELATIN" — a probable extraction glitch.

## Tip 193 — Sour Cream and Cream — Whipping Cream Types and Fat Content

**Source:** from B6-24

SOUR CREAM AND CREAM – LET'S HACK THE ISSUE!

I suggest starting with the whipping cream.

Thanks to the separation, it is possible to produce the cream with different fat content:

📍 Whipping cream 30-35%
📍 Light cream 20%
📍 Half and Half 10-12%

Half and half is a mix of ½ whole milk and ½ cream, typically added to coffee, tea or hot cocoa. This dairy product is good for cooking the creamy baked meat, for example. It also can be used for making the savoury custard for a quiche, you may cook porridge with it or add to pureed soups.

Light cream is quite thick, but even if it whips, it will quickly collapse.

Therefore, the light cream is not suitable for making the cream. But it is perfect for making sauces, it also can be added to cream soups. In most cases it is added to coffee and used for baking.

Whipping cream is the thickest and most nutritious type of cream.

This cream is used for making mousses, creams, pastries, as well as ice cream. This is the cream pastry chef constantly works with.

Cream with a fat content of at least 33% is considered ideal for whipping. I personally prefer whipping cream with a fat content of 36% - any cream comes out much more stable than using 30-33% whipping cream.

An important point. Mostly, we work with either very hot whipping cream ( ~ 90 °C / 194 °F - caramel, ganache) or very cold one (4 °C / 39 °F - whipped creams, mousses).

And what about homemade cream? Can we work with it? I can't categorically say "no", but I do not advise working with it either. Why?

I'll tell you in the continuation of a series of posts about sour cream and cream 😏

## Tip 194 — Sour Cream — Origins, Types, Quality, and Substitutes (Crème Fraîche Intro)

**Source:** from B6-25

This time we will talk about sour cream. The name of this product is familiar to everyone speaking Slavic languages. In European countries, sour cream became widespread only in the XX century.

Among the Slavic peoples, its name sounds almost the same: сметана, смятана, smotana, smantana, smietana. The Germans call this product schmand or schmetten.

Residents of foreign countries often call sour cream "Russian cream". In many countries there is a product very similar to sour cream. It is called crème fraîche. We will talk about the difference between these products and their interchangeability later.

Sour cream is obtained by fermenting regular milk or cream with certain kinds of lactic acid bacteria. This can be done both at home and in the industry.

Depending on the manufacturing method, sour cream is divided into two types:

📌 produced by households (farm-fresh) obtained by natural fermentation of milk

📌 produced by industrial production, made from whole milk or pasteurized cream with the addition of pure streptococcal cultures.

The first type has a thicker consistency and a taste similar to butter. The advantages of the second are the possibility of producing sour cream with different fat content and a longer shelf life.

Based on the percentage of fat, sour cream produced by industrial production can be:
📌 Light (10-14%)
📌 Regular (15-19%)
📌 Classic (20-34%)
📌 High-fat (35-48%)

In the pastry industry sour cream of 30-40% fat is used most often since it can be whipped. Sour cream is used to make sweet unleavened dough, tea-cakes, muffins, cookies, pies, sometimes sponges and, of course, for making creams.

Let's talk about the quality of sour cream and the possibility of substituting it with other products.

Sour cream is a delicate white dairy product with a moderately thick, homogeneous consistency and glossy surface.

Having the smell, typical for pasteurized dairy products, good-quality sour cream has a pronounced, but pure taste of fermented milk with slight sourness.

Too strong acidic taste or sour smell, the presence of grains in sour cream are the indicators of violations in the production technology or storage conditions of the product.

Both farm fresh and industrial sour cream should not contain anything except milk (cream) and ferments, otherwise this product cannot be called sour cream.

In this regard, it is worth paying attention to the ingredients and name of fermented milk products. The absence of the word combination "Sour cream" on the packaging, as a rule, means the presence of vegetable fats among the ingredients of such products.

What can be used to substitute sour cream in frosting?

I can suggest you several options to choose from, and the first one, preferable, is crème fraîche.

Crème fraîche (lit. from French "fresh cream") is a dairy product with the fat content of not more than 30% (usually 15—18 or 28%).

It is made by fermenting pasteurized cream from cow's milk. Crème fraîche has a pleasant, slightly sour taste, and without additives it is indistinguishable from sour cream either in taste or in the production technology.

In England, Spain, Italy, Denmark, the Benelux countries, crème fraîche and sour creame often stand on the shelves of shops nearby and even have the same manufacturers. Moreover, both names are often present on the same package.

---

## Tip 195 — Homemade Cream — Can You Use It? (Heat Treatment Categories, Shelf Life, Fat Content Risk)

**Source:** from B6-26

Can we work with homemade cream? I can't categorically say "no", but I don't recommend working with it either. Why? Let's figure it out.

Firstly, in addition to different fat content, the cream can also be divided into categories according to the method of heat treatment:

📌 Raw – natural.
📌 Pasteurized - heated up to 80 ± 2 °C / 176 °F for 20 sec and then cooled to 4 ± 2 °C / 39 °F.
📌 Sterilized - heated up to 137 ± 2 °C / 279 °F for 4 sec and then cooled to 20 °C / 68 °F.

I think it is obvious to everyone that homemade cream is sold completely fresh, not subjected to any heat treatment, that significantly reduces its shelf life.

The shelf life of homemade cream is from 12 hours to several days, depending on its fat content.

That's why a cake made with fresh homemade cream should be eaten not later than the next day after assembling.

Secondly, most often the fat content of homemade cream which can be purchased on the market is about 50-60%. It's not very convenient, but you can work with it if you dilute the cream with whole milk.

But this is where another problem arises. Since you do not know the exact fat content of your homemade cream, you might make a mistake when diluting it with milk - make it too runny or leave it too fatty.

Let's draw the conclusions. It is definitely possible to use fresh homemade cream for making frosting for a cake, but only for a cake that is meant for your own home consumption. I do not recommend working with it, especially if you bake cakes to order.

---

## Tip 196 — Chilling Whipping Cream Before Whipping, and Checking Whipping Cream Quality at Home

**Source:** from B6-27

📍 Why should we chill the whipping cream before whipping?

The answer is super simple – by doing this we let the fats crystallize. Otherwise, the whipping cream will quickly collapse or won't whip up at all. In addition, it is recommended to cool also all the tools involved in the work with it, i.e. a whisk and a bowl.

I haven't done this for a long time, and I don't advise you to do it. Why?

Nowadays almost any whipping cream in its contents contains various food additives, namely stabilizers, thickeners and often preservatives. Thanks to these additives, it whips well and keeps its shape perfectly without any problems.

📍 How to check the quality of whipping cream at home?

To do this, simply bring the whipping cream to a boil and look at its consistency. If your hot whipping cream becomes runny like milk, everything is fine, you can work with it.

If, on the contrary, it has become even thicker, almost like sour cream, I doubt that fats of animal origin predominate in its contents.

📍 Why does the packaged whipping cream with a normal shelf life sometimes happens to be as thick as homemade fat sour cream?

Well, it happens sometimes. Dishonest suppliers are to blame for this, because they violated the storage conditions of the product and exposed the whipping cream to temperature extremes to which the latter is extremely sensitive.

If this happens to you, do not hesitate to ask the supplier to replace such product with the one it's possible to work with.

---

## Tip 197 — Sour Cream vs Crème Fraîche — The Difference (Abroad Context)

**Source:** from B6-28

Today I suggest talking about the difference between sour cream and crème fraîche.

So, nevertheless, crème fraiche and sour cream are far from the same thing. If we talk about sour cream that can be purchased abroad, then it will most likely be a kind of "sour cream product", and not real sour cream.

Since we will be looking for a substitute for sour cream in non-CIS countries, I'd like to compare the crème fraîche and the "sour cream product", which is usually sold abroad in packages labelled "sour cream".

📌 The fat content of the "sour cream product" may vary from 10 to 24%. "Sour cream" with the fat content of 15% is the easiest to find. Find the one with a higher fat content may be problematic.

📌 The fat content of crème fraîche is 15-35%. The product with a fat content of 30% is the most common.

📌 "Sour cream" may typically include ingredients such as gelatine, pectin, renin, modified starch, sodium citrate, and plant enzymes. They are used to stabilize and thicken the product.

📌 Crème fraîche does not contain added thickeners and stabilizers. It is much thicker and has a richer and creamier flavor compared to "sour cream".

📌 "Sour cream" has a pronounced sour taste.

📌 Crème fraîche is a dairy product with a pH of approximately 4.5. It is less acidic than sour cream.

In recipes where crème fraîche and sour cream are not the main ingredient, they can be used interchangeably.

Since crème fraîche contains more fat and less protein, it may be used in soups and sauces - it will melt easily and won't curl, unlike sour cream.

So, what is better to use for making frosting for a honey sponge cake - "sour cream" or crème fraiche? It will depend on what kind of frosting you want to make and the result you want to get.

---

## Tip 198 — Frosting Choice — Low-Fat Sour Cream vs Crème Fraîche (Practical Guidance, Draining Sour Cream)

**Source:** from B6-29

Today let's figure out what's better to use for frosting — low-fat sour cream or crème fraîche.

To make frosting for a honey cake, you can use either crème fraîche or sour cream with a low fat content that contains thickeners.

In the first case, you will get a very thick, stable frosting, and it will be a pleasure to assemble a cake with it. It's perfect for tall cakes with complex fillings.

Main disadvantage - crème fraîche will not give a pronounced sourness typical of sour cream.

Using sour cream with a fat content of less than 25-30%, you will get an unstable frosting that won't keep its shape at all.

Thickeners in the contents won't add thickness to the frosting — the sour cream containing thickeners can be quite stable itself, but if you add sugar to it and start whipping, the whole mixture turns into a runny substance that is difficult to whip and thicken.

Unstable frosting can be used to assemble a classic honey sponge cake in a cake ring, but the result will be corresponding — too soaked cake and no distinct stripes in the cut.

What can be done? First of all, try to drain the sour cream using a strainer and a doubled or tripled piece of cheesecloth. If the amount of thickeners and stabilizers in its contents is not enormous, then over the night of draining your 15-20% sour cream will turn into 30-35%, while losing up to 40% of its weight.

Optionally, you may replace a part (up to 50%) of the sour cream in the frosting with 33-36% heavy cream or mascarpone, for example.

Ideally, if you use 15-20% sour cream for the frosting, first drain it in the fridge overnight, and then whip with heavy cream.

If you've read to the end and liked the article, please let me know in the comments. This is extremely important to me, as every comment you leave inspires me to write more!

P.S. In the photos you can see a classic honey sponge cake with 30% sour cream. Then, in the carousel, there are photos of the same honey sponge cake with frosting made with farm-fresh sour cream with high fat content. Do you see the difference?

**Notes:** Covers similar ground to Tip 046 (from Batch 1, "How and Why to Drain Sour Cream") — both discuss draining sour cream to increase fat content, but this post frames it specifically as a crème-fraîche-vs-sour-cream frosting decision with different draining percentages cited (15-20%→30-35% here vs. 30%→36% in Tip 046). Kept as an independent tip per this task's continuation-only scope; a human reconciler should compare both for consistency.

## Tip 199 — Sour Cream Substitutes — Greek Yogurt and Mascarpone-with-Cream

**Source:** from B6-30

Today we will find out what else can be used instead of sour cream in recipes, except for crème fraîche.

So, apart from crème fraîche, sour cream can also be replaced with
📌 Greek yogurt with high fat content
📌 Mascarpone with cream

Now let's have a closer look at pros and cons of these options.

My students who live abroad often write me that instead of sour cream they use Greek yogurt and everything turns out just fine - the frosting is light and the cake is delicious.

Possibly.

It is possible if you have access to high fat yogurt abroad, which can be used in making a good frosting.

My first and last experience with Greek yogurt frosting ended in disaster.

The frosting turned out to be good - stable and tasty. I assembled the honey sponge cake without any problems. But what I saw the next morning just shocked me.

There was a puddle of whey under the cake. Quite a real puddle, which I diligently wiped out with a paper towel, already perfectly aware that the experiment had failed.

In short, about 2 tablespoons of liquid had been appearing under the cake every 2 hours. You may imagine the cut and taste of that honey sponge cake - it was as if the cake had been dipped in a container filled with water for several hours.

The result, in principle, is logical - there are only 10-12% of fat in yogurt. It is almost the same as using 10% sour cream.

From my experience, even 2-3 tablespoons of yogurt added to the frosting with cream and cream cheese will significantly reduce the stability of the latter.

Thus, I only accept the use of "fat" yogurt for frosting if it has been drained well at least overnight, then mixed with heavy cream and / or mascarpone. But this version of frosting is not suitable for all types of honey layers.

Have you tried using yogurt in frosting instead of sour cream? Share your experience, please.

As for the recipes in which sour cream is used for making the dough, in most cases you can safely replace it with Greek yogurt and kefir or buttermilk.

## Tip 200 — A Perfect Cheesecake — Myths vs Reality (Room Temperature Ingredients, Whisking Cold Ingredients)

**Source:** from B6-31

A PERFECT CHEESECAKE. MYTHS VS REALITY

«...Why should the ingredients for a cheesecake be of a room temperature? Does it mean that if I take ingredients right from the fridge, nothing will work?

Which tool should be used to properly combine the ingredients for the cheese mass - a spoon, a spatula or a whisk? You say any of them except from a whisk, but why?

Great, then what happens if you take cold ingredients and give them a good whisk? 🤔

Nothing terrible?! No way!! But what about cracks, lumps and other horrifying consequences that everyone is talking about? .. "

Surely, everyone who has ever tried to bake a cheesecake has asked similar questions. Especially after reading various tips.

Let's try to deal with them and understand the real reasons why such superstitions appear.

In fact, the temperature of the ingredients does not affect the taste, texture or appearance of a cheesecake at all. Only the temperature of the cream cheese can be of minor importance, as the cream cheese becomes soft at room temperature. That's why the cheese mass with room temperature cream cheese will be much easier to mix until smooth, avoiding lumps.

If you forgot to take the cream cheese out of the fridge in advance, don't worry. If desired, it can be slightly heated up over a water bath or in a microwave oven, or even used cold.

If you are a lucky owner of a Kenwood Cooking Chef Induction Mixer, you can mix the cold cheese mass turning on the heating at 40 °C / 104 °F. It is very convenient.

The cheese mass for a cheesecake has to be mixed until smooth, but not whipped until fluffy. Therefore, work at low speed using the paddle attachment.

So what will happen if you whisk cold ingredients together?

In fact, absolutely nothing to worry about. The mass will be actively saturated with air, that's why its volume might increase significantly.

Still, you may face some difficulties such as:
📌 the whole cheese mass won't fit into the shortbread shell
📌 after baking, the excess air will come out of the cheesecake and a dent will form in the center. Its depth directly depends on how much you whipped the cheese mass.

---

## Tip 201 — Cheesecake — Hot vs Cold Infusion Method, and Baking-Ring Myths (Metal Pan Myth, Unmolding)

**Source:** from B6-32

HOW TO MAKE A PERFECT CHEESECAKE

Personally, I prefer the hot infusion, since it is much more effective and does not require such a long preparation.

The hot method suggests heating the whipping cream / milk with flavoring up to 90 °C / 194 °F over low heat. Then you should cover the mixture with a lid and let it infuse for at least 2-3 hours. For an intense flavor, you may repeat the process one more time.

If you use flowers, leaves, coffee grains, tonka beans or tea as a flavoring, it's a good idea to process the whipping cream together with the flavoring with a blender before heating.

But in this case you should increase the amount of whipping cream / milk by 30-40%, and after infusion simply strain the mass through a strainer or a piece of cheesecloth.

ℹ Only a metal cake pan with a removable bottom is good for baking cheesecake.

At first glance, this is a correct statement, because in this case unmolding the cheesecake can be done with your eyes shut. In fact, cheesecakes can be baked equally well in cake pans with removable bottom and in baking rings.

Just when dealing with baking rings, beginners will immediately face two seemingly difficult tasks:

☑ how to shape and then transfer a cheesecake in a baking ring without a bottom, because after baking the dessert is unstable and requires careful treatment.

There are several solutions to this problem.

In order to easily transfer the cheesecake to the oven and then to the fridge, you can place the baking ring on the bottom of the old split cake mold of a slightly larger diameter, covered with a sheet of parchment paper.

I use another method - I wrap the bottom of the baking ring in several layers of foil and put it on a thick cake base.

You can, of course, put the ring directly on the baking sheet, provided that its surface is perfectly flat, but in this case you will not be able to use it until the cheesecake is completely cooled. To be continued.

☑ how to unmold a seemingly glued cheesecake from a baking ring without destroying its ideal shape.

Firstly, remember to apply a thin layer of oil (I prefer coconut oil) on the baking ring and unmold the cheesecake out only after 6 hours of cooling.

Secondly, if you leave a cheesecake at room temperature for 15-20 minutes, the oil will slightly melt and you will only need to carefully lift the baking ring up. The cheesecake will remain on the cake base.

✅ Useful tips:

✔ To speed up the process of unmolding the cheese, you may heat the baking ring up with a heat gun or a hair dryer.

✔ Preferably, the cheesecake should not be higher than 6 cm (without décor).

✔ Try to shape the sides of the shortbread shell so that the cheese mass is only slightly below the level of the sides, otherwise it may detach slightly after baking.

**Notes:** **[ANOMALY]** This post opens with the hot-infusion method, which is the answer to a "two methods: cold and hot" setup that is only introduced later in the source (Tip 203) — the cold method is only described there. Source paragraph order does not match logical/chronological order for this sub-topic (see consolidated Flagged Anomalies section).

---

## Tip 202 — Cheesecake Baking Temperature — Water Bath Explained, Oven Modes

**Source:** from B6-33

ℹ Cheesecake must be baked at low temperatures (not higher than 120 °C / 248 °F) with a water bath.

Let me remind you that a water bath is a baking sheet full of boiling water installed on the lowest level of the oven.

Please, pay your attention to the fact that you should not pour water into the baking sheet with the cheesecake, otherwise the water will get into the mold and your cheesecake will get wet.

In fact, the baking mode for a cheesecake depends on the characteristics of your oven. Cheesecakes can be baked at either 90-100 °C / 194-212 °F or 150-160 °C / 302-320 °F, with or without a water bath.

If you bake your cheesecake at a very low temperature (90-100 °C / 194-212 °F), then you probably won't need a water bath. But if your oven is so powerful that even at 90 °C / 194 °F the cheesecake rises and its top burns - put a water bath - it lowers the heat in the oven.

I used to bake cheesecakes with a water bath and strictly at a temperature not more than 120 °C / 248 °F. It would take my cheesecakes at least 3-4 hours to bake, and for a long time I could not understand what I was doing wrong.

Then a brilliant idea came across my mind and I bought a thermometer for the oven. It turned out that my 120 °C / 248 °F + a water bath = 80-90 °C / 176-194 °F of real temperature. Now, when baking a cheesecake with a water bath, I set the temperature to at least 150 °C / 302 °F and my thermometer shows exactly 120 °C / 248 °F. The cheesecake is ready in 1.5 hours!

ℹ You can bake cheesecakes using absolutely any oven mode.

It all depends on the situation, and, again, on the characteristics of your oven. If the top heating element heats too much, then turn on only the bottom one. In 80% of cases, conventional heating is the best option. If you need to bake several trays at the same time, use the convection mode.

✅ Useful tip:
If you have difficulties with the baking mode and the uniformity of baking, you can cover the top of the cheesecake with foil. This will keep the light color of the cheesecake and prevent it from being burnt.

## Tip 203 — Cheesecake Flavoring — Adding to the Cheese Mass Directly, and Infusing Cream or Milk (Cold Method)

**Source:** merged B6-34 + B6-35 (kept together — see notes)

✅ Add the flavouring directly to the cheese mass.

🔹️ What could it be?

✔ berry, fruit purees
✔ whole berries and fruit pieces
✔ caramel, dulce de leche
✔ chocolate, ganache, Nutella
✔ nuts and nut paste
✔ instant coffee, matcha tea
✔ liqueurs, cognac and other alcoholic beverages.

You need to work very carefully, since each of these flavourings has a different water and sugar content. The most important advice I want to give is that berry or fruit purees should be pre-thickened with cornstarch.

Add the starch to the puree and constantly stirring bring it to a boil - you should get a thick mass. It is also possible to go without pre-cooking, but then the texture of the cheesecake will be a little softer.

If you add alcohol, then reduce the cream / milk in the recipe by the same amount, if you do not want the cheesecake to be very tender.

✅ Infuse cream or milk.

🔹️ You may infuse it with:

✔ cinnamon stick, vanilla pod, saffron, whole star anise, cardamom, or any other spice
✔ lavender and violets flowers, rose petals
✔ leaves of mint, lemon balm, basil, thyme
✔ coffee beans
✔ tonka beans
✔ lemon, orange or lime zest
✔ green, black, milk or any other tea
✔ blue cheeses and more.

There are two ways of infusion - cold and hot one.

As for the cold method, it is enough to add flavoring to the cream / milk and leave it overnight, or even better for a day, in the fridge.

**Notes:** Batch 6 treated these as two separate tips (B6-34: direct-add method; B6-35: infusion method) since each has its own "✅" header. Merged here into one tip because both are two halves of the same "how to flavor a cheesecake" post with no publish-date break between them (unlike other cheesecake-myth posts in this range, which do have distinct openers/closers). This tip's closing sentence ("cold method... leave overnight") is the setup that Tip 201/B6-32 already answered with the hot method earlier in the source — confirming the out-of-order sequencing flagged at Tip 201.

## Tip 204 — Perfect Cheesecake Borders — Achieving a Clean Shortbread Crust

**Source:** from B6-36

PERFECT BORDERS

Perhaps the most difficult thing in the process of baking cheesecakes is to achieve perfectly beautiful borders, both externally and in the cut.

For sure, the even borders take your cheesecake from the category of an ordinary homemade pastry to the rank of a culinary masterpiece, don't they?

By the way, how are you doing with the borders? Are you satisfied with your result?

At the very beginning, when I baked my first cheese cakes, making a crust was the most lengthy and painful moment of the whole process.

I tried almost all the tools! Glasses, mugs, spoons, dough scrapers - I've tested everything! I used to spend at least 40-50 minutes for one cheese cake. There was even a period when I awaited this moment with horror and panic.

But hard work and experience overcame everything. Six months later, the perfect base was ready in 15 minutes using a spoon as an improvised tool.

Now I form an ideal crust in 4-5 minutes, sometimes with only my bare hands staying absolutely calm.

What's the secret, you ask? Is it just a vast experience?

Not really. The secret of a quick and perfect base for a cheesecake is a good recipe and the right technique. Well, experience, of course, will come in handy 😉

A few tips and tricks for you:

📌 reconstituted shortbread should shape well and keep its shape when squeezed, while remaining crumbly

📌 both butter and coconut oil can be used to change the structure the shortbread

📍 in the latter case, the base will be more crumbly and crunchy, and it will also be much more easier to unmold it

📌 forming the base of the cheesecake, try to distribute the crumbs along the bottom and sides in an even layer

📌 pay attention to the fact that the thickness of the crust from bottom to top should be the same

📌 also make sure that a thick layer of dough does not form between the bottom and the borders

📍 in order to avoid the latter, first completely form the borders, and only then proceed to the bottom
