## Tip 205 — Cheesecake — Room Temperature Ingredients Myth (Cheese Filling) and Whipping/Cracks Myth

**Source:** from B6-37

HOW TO MAKE A PERFECT CHEESECAKE

I am glad that you find the tips on making the perfect cheesecake useful, so I will be happy to continue. If you like this idea, leave + in the comments!

ℹ Is it necessary to use room temperature ingredients to make the cheese filling?

No, it won't affect the taste, the texture or the look of the pastry in any way. In fact, only the temperature of the cream cheese matters, because when the cream cheese is at room temperature, it is much softer and then it's easier to mix the mass until smooth, avoiding lumps.

Useful tip:

✅ If you forgot to get the cream cheese out of the fridge beforehand, you may slightly heat it up over a water bath or in the microwave oven.

ℹ Do not whip the mass for a long time, otherwise there might be cracks on the surface of the cheesecake due to the excess of air in the mass. Work at low speed.

In fact, it is enough to understand that the cheesecake should be mixed until smooth, and not whipped until fluffy.

As for the cracks that appear due to air bubbles, I'm sure this is more of a myth. There were several cases when I used cold cream cheese and overwhipped the cheese mass trying to get rid of the lumps. There were bubbles, but no cracks.

The reason for the formation of cracks on the surface of the cheesecake is actually very simple - the baking temperature is too high, but I will write about it in detail later in the article.

✅ Useful tip:

It is better to introduce the eggs into the cheese filling one at a time, each time mixing the mass until smooth.

**Notes:** Same myth-busting format and title as Tips 173/200 but a distinct posting (own opening comment-solicitation line), covering the room-temperature-ingredients myth again with slightly different framing/wording than Tip 200, plus a whipping/cracks myth and an egg-introduction tip.

## Tip 206 — Water Bath and Cheesecake — Over vs In, and Why Not To Use "In"

**Source:** from B6-38

WATER BATH AND CHEESECAKE

One of the questions that I receive quite often from people who bought the cheesecake recipe concerns the water bath: what's that? Where should I pour the water?

I'm wondering, without reading the post, what are your ideas? Write them in the comments section, please! I will read your answers with pleasure.

Done? Great! Now let's figure out how the cheesecake should be baked.

In fact, you may bake a cheesecake both over a water bath and in a water bath, or even without using water at all. So, what is the water bath in cooking in general and how is it better to bake a cheesecake - over or in the water bath?

💦 A water bath is a method of cooking, in which the liquid is heated without direct contact of the container with fire. This method is used when the required temperature of the mass that is being cooked should not exceed 100 °C / 212 °F.

💦 Cooking over a water bath suggests pouring the water into the lower bowl or container, and placing the product, which will be cooked over a water bath, in the upper one. In this case, the height of the upper bowl should be smaller in order to leave room for boiling water. Moreover, the water in the larger bowl should not boil too much. This construction is usually used for cooking on the stove.

💦 Cooking in a water bath suggests placing a small bowl directly into a larger container, into which the hot water is poured. The level of water should reach the middle of the inner bowl. This construction is often used for baking in the oven.

When baking over the water bath, the cheesecake is placed on a baking sheet / wire rack at the top of the oven, while the second baking sheet or any other container filled with hot water is placed at the bottom.

When baking in the water bath, the cheesecake pan is placed directly into a large container filled with hot water.

I do not recommend baking the cheesecake in the water bath, as there is a high chance of water getting into the mold resulting in a wet base of the cheesecake!

## Tip 207 — A Perfect Cheesecake — Shortbread Crust Myths and Practical Tips (Homemade vs Store-Bought Cookies, Shaping, Freezing, Coconut Oil)

**Source:** from B6-39

HOW TO MAKE A PERFECT CHEESECAKE

I suggest paying closer attention to this popular American dessert and trying to figure out together which rules for its preparation should be considered as indisputable facts, and which can be neglected.

Also, in this article, I will share some tips that will help you to make not only incredibly delicious, but also gorgeous-looking cheesecake.

Let's bust the myths! And I'll start with making the shortbread crust.

ℹ The crust can be made using the ready-made or home-made shortbread cookies, you won't notice much difference.

Although I completely agree with the first part of the statement, the second seems quite controversial. If you want to make a really delicious cheesecake, do not be too lazy to bake home-made sables with almond or hazelnut flour.

Useful tips:

✅ While forming the base of the cheesecake, try to distribute the crust along the bottom and sides of the springform pan in an even layer. Mind the thickness of the sides and the bottom, it should be the same.

✅ Make sure that the edges are not thick. To avoid this common problem, do the sides first, and only then proceed to the bottom.

✅ It is very convenient to use a flat bottom mug (not the one with the concave bottom) with sides at right angle for forming the cheesecake base. Also, a tablespoon might be quite helpful.

✅ If after baking the sides of the shortbread crust have slid down, replace the butter with a thicker one and try to use less of it.

✅ It is much easier and faster not to bake the formed shortbread crust, but simply put it in the freezer for 30-60 minutes.

✅ If you use the coconut oil instead of butter for the reconstituted shortbread crust, the base will come out crispier and crumblier, and it will be easier to unmold it.

**Notes:** This reads like it could be the actual intro/opening post of the whole "HOW TO MAKE A PERFECT CHEESECAKE" myth-busting series (the general framing device "let's bust the myths" suggests this is either the series opener published out of order, or the opener of a crust-specific sub-series). Content itself (shortbread crust myth + 6 practical tips) is self-contained and complete — this is the final tip in the entire six-batch corpus; the source document ends here.

- **Tip 169 (B5-33, "Shelf Life of Frosting")** vs. **Tip 006 (B1-06, "Shelf Life of Frostings")**
  — same general topic, different numeric ranges and structure (e.g., buttercream: "24–72 hours
  depending on ingredients" in Tip 006 vs. a flat "no more than 36 hours" governmental-standard
  figure in Tip 169). Likely two genuinely separate source posts on the same recurring topic, not
  duplicates — but worth reconciling against site data.
- **Tip 198 (B6-29)** vs. **Tip 046 (B2-12, "How and Why to Drain Sour Cream")** — both cover
  draining sour cream to raise its fat content, with different cited percentages/multipliers
  (15–20%→30–35% in Tip 198 vs. 30%→~36% in Tip 046).
- **Tips 119/121/129/130 (Infusion series)** vs. **Tip 001 (Flavor Infusion mega-tip)** — see note
  under "Numbering / sequencing inconsistencies" above; likely the same underlying content captured
  at two different granularities by two different batches.

## Source typos preserved verbatim (per the instruction to keep original text faithful)
